The climate of the countries of Anahuac varies according to their

§ 4. situation. The maritime countries are hot, and for the most part hu‐ Climate of Anahuac. mid and unwholesome. Their heat, which makes one sweat even in January, is caused by the extreme depression of the coasts relative to the inland regions, or by the hills of sand heaped up on the beaches, as happens at Veracruz, my native city. The humidity comes no less from the sea than from the waters that descend in abundance from the mountains that overlook the coasts. In the hot lands there is never any frost, and very many inhabitants of such regions have no other idea of snow than what they acquire by reading books or from the accounts of strangers. Lands that are too elevated, or too near the very high mountains that are eternally covered with snow there, are cold; and I have been on a mountain not more than twenty-five miles distant from the capital, where there is frost and ice even in the dog days. All the other inland countries, where the greatest population of that land was, enjoy a climate so mild and so gentle that they feel neither the rigor of winter nor the heats of summer. It is true that in many of those countries there is frequent frost in the three months of December, January, and February, and sometimes it even snows; but the slight inconvenience that such cold causes lasts no longer than until sunrise: there is no need of any other fire than that of the sun's rays to warm oneself in winter, nor of any other refreshment in time of heat than that of shade. The same garment that covers men in the dog days defends them in January, and the animals sleep the whole year under the open sky.

This mildness and pleasantness of the climate beneath the torrid zone is the effect of several natural causes entirely unknown to the ancients, who believed it uninhabitable, and not well understood by some moderns, by whom it is held to be little favorable

to

p. 40

to living beings. The clearness of the atmosphere, the lesser obliquity

Bk. I.

of the solar rays, and the longer stay of this planet above the horizon

in winter, in comparison with other regions farther from the equator,

combine to lessen the cold and to avoid all that horror with which

Nature is seen disfigured under other zones. One enjoys even at that

season the beauty of the sky and the innocent delights of the coun‐

tryside, whereas under the cold zones, and for the most part even un‐

der the temperate, clouds rob one of the prospect of the sky, and

snow buries the fair productions of the earth. Nor are lesser causes at

work to temper the heat of summer. The copious rains that frequent‐

ly water the earth after midday from April or May to September and

October, the high mountains always laden with snow, scattered here

and there throughout the land of Anahuac, the cool winds that then

blow, and the shorter stay of the Sun above the horizon, in compari‐

son with the regions of the temperate zone, transform the summer of

those happy countries into a cheerful and cool spring.

But the pleasantness of the climate is offset by the thunderstorms,

which are frequent in summer, especially in the neighborhood of

Matlalcueye, or the mountain of Tlaxcala, and by the earthquakes

that are sometimes felt, though with more fright than damage. Both

effects are caused by the sulphur and the other combustible materials

deposited in great abundance in the bowels of the earth. As for hail‐

storms, they are neither more frequent nor greater there than in

Europe.

been observed at different times. The Poyauhtecatl, called by the

Spaniards the Volcano of Orizaba, began to throw out smoke in

the slightest sign of fire. This celebrated mountain, which is of coni‐

Bk. I. cal shape, and is without doubt the highest in all the land of Anahuac, is by reason of its height the first land seen by navigators sailing toward that quarter, at a distance of 150 miles. (k) Its summit is always covered with snow, and its slope is adorned with great cedars, pines, and other trees of precious wood; whence its prospect is beautiful from every side. It is more than ninety miles from the capital toward the east.

The Popocatépetl and the Iztaccíhuatl, near each other and thirty-three miles distant from Mexico toward the southeast, are also of astonishing height. The Popocatépetl, to which they give there by antonomasia the name of Volcan, has a mouth or vent of more than half a mile, through which in the time of the Mexican kings it often threw out flames, and in the last century many times hurled a great quantity of ashes over the neighboring places; but in this century hardly any smoke has been observed. The Iztaccíhuatl, known to the Spaniards by the name of Sierra nevada, has also at times thrown out smoke and ashes. Both mountains always have their summits crowned with snow, which is so abundant that from what tumbles down on the neighboring slopes the cities of Mexico, Puebla, and Cholula are supplied, and other neighboring places, even as far as forty miles distant from those mountains, in which an incredible quantity is consumed all the year in ices. The mountains of Anahuac abound in mines of every sort of met‐

al, and of an infinite variety of other fossils. The Mexicans had gold in

the countries of the Cohuixcas, the Mixtecs, the Zapotecs, and in

the most part this precious metal in grains among the sands of the

Bk. I. rivers, and the aforesaid peoples paid a certain quantity of it to the crown of Mexico. Silver was dug from the mines of Tlachco (celebrated even at that time), of Zumpango, and others; but it was not so highly prized by them as by other nations. After the conquest so many silver mines have been discovered in that country, especially in the provinces that lie to the northwest of the capital, that it is quite impossible to number them. Of copper they had two kinds, one hard, which they used in place of iron for making axes, hatchets, hoes, and other instruments of war and of agriculture, and the other common and pliant, for making basins, pots, and other vessels. This metal abounded more than elsewhere in the provinces of Zacatula and of the Cohuixcas, as it does today in the kingdom of Michoacán. They dug tin from the mines of Tlachco, and lead from those of Ixmiquilpan, a place in the country of the Otomis. Of tin they made money, as we shall say in its place, and of lead we know that it was sold in the markets; but we are wholly ignorant of the use they made of it. They had also iron mines in Tlaxcala, in Tlachco, and in other places; but either they did not discover the mines, or else they did not know how to take advantage of the metal. They had likewise in Chilapa mines of quicksilver, and in many places mines of sulphur, alum, vitriol, cinnabar, ochre, and of a white earth very much like white lead. As for the quicksilver and the vitriol, we do not know what use they made of them; of the other minerals they made use for their paintings and dyes. Of amber and of asphaltum, or bitumen of Judea, there was, and there still is, great abundance on the coasts of both seas, and several places of the empire paid tribute in both of them to the king of Mexico. The amber, which they set in gold, they used only for ornament and pleasure; of the asphaltum they made use in certain incensings, as we shall see elsewhere.

Among the precious stones there were, and there still are, diamonds,

F2 though

p. 44

though few, emeralds, amethysts, cat's-eyes, turquoises, cornelians, Bk. I.

and certain green stones resembling emeralds and not much inferior

to them, and of all these stones the provinces of the Mixtecs, of the

Zapotecs, and of the Cohuixcas, in whose mountains the mines of

such gems are found, paid tribute to the king. Of their abundance, of

the esteem in which the Mexicans held them, and of the manner in

which they worked them, we shall speak more opportunely in anoth‐

er place. The mountains that lie on the coast of the Gulf of Mexico

between the port of Veracruz and the river Coatzacoalco, as also

those of Chinantla and of the province of the Mixtecs, supplied them

with rock crystal, and the cities of Tochtepec, of Cuetlachtlan, of

Cosamaloapan, and others were obliged to contribute annually a cer‐

tain quantity for the luxury of the court.

No less do those mountains abound in various kinds of stones val‐

ued for use in architecture, sculpture, and other arts. There are quar‐

ries of jasper and of marble of various colors in the mountains of

Calpulalpan east of Mexico, in those that separate the two valleys of

Mexico and of Toluca, now called Monte de las Cruces, and in those of

the Zapotecs; of alabaster at Tecalco (at present Tecali), a place near

the province of Tepeyacac, and in the country of the Mixtecs; of

Tetzontli in the same valley of Mexico, and in many other places of the

kingdom. The stone Tetzontli is for the most part of a dark red color,

quite hard, porous, and light; it binds lime and sand to itself very

tightly, and for that reason is sought after more than any other stone

for the buildings of the capital, whose soil is marshy and not very

firm. There are also whole mountains of lodestone, and among others

a very large one between Teoixtla and Chilapa in the country of the

Cohuixcas. Of the Quetzalitztli, commonly known by the name of

nephritic stone, the Mexicans made various curious figures, some of

which are preserved in several museums of Europe. The Chimaltizatl,

which is a species of selenite, or ass's mirror, is a diaphanous whitish

stone,

and

p. 45

and easily divisible into thin plates, which by means of calcination

Bk. I. yields a fine plaster, and the ancient Mexicans used it for the white of their paintings. There is also an infinite quantity of gypsum and of talc; but as to this stone we do not know the use they made of it. The Mezcuitlatl, that is, dung of the moon, is of the class of those stones which, because of their resistance to the action of fire, are called by the chemists Lapides refractarii. It is transparent, and of a reddish gold color. But no other stone was so commonly used among the Mexicans as the Itztli, of which there is abundance in many places of Mexico. The Itztli is semi-diaphanous, of vitreous substance, and for the most part black; but there is also found white and blue. Of this stone they made mirrors, knives, lancets, razors, and even swords, as we shall say where we treat of their military affairs, and after the introduction of the Gospel highly prized altar stones were made of it. (n)

Since the mineral kingdom of Mexico is so abundant and rich, the § 6. vegetable kingdom is even more plentiful and varied. The celebrated Plants valued for

their flowers. Dr. Hernández, that is, the Pliny of New Spain, describes in his Natural History as many as twelve hundred plants proper to that land; but his description, being limited to medicinal plants, scarcely comprises a part, though a large one, of what provident Nature has produced there for the benefit of mortals. Of medicinal plants we shall say a word when we treat of the medicine of the Mexicans. With regard to the other classes of vegetables, there are some valued for their flowers, others for their fruits, others for their leaves, others for their root, others for their stem or their wood, and others finally for their gum, resin, oil, or juice. The Floripundio, which by its size deserves the first place, is a

white flower, beautiful, very fragrant, and monopetalous, that is, of a

single leaf, but so large that it measures eight inches and even more in

length, and three or four in diameter at the upper part. Many hang

together from the branches in the form of bells, but not quite round,

since their leaf, or corolla (*), makes five or six angles in it at propor‐

tionate distances from one another. These flowers grow on a pretty

little shrub, whose branches form a round crown in the manner of a

dome. Its trunk is tender, its leaves large, angular, and of a dull green.

The flowers are followed by round fruits, as big as oranges, which

have almonds inside.

The Jolloxochitl, or Flower of the Heart, is also large, and no less

valued for its beauty than for its scent, which is so strong that a single

flower suffices to fill a whole house with the sweetest fragrance. It has

many glutinous leaves, white on the outside and reddish or yellowish

within, and so arranged that when the flower is open and its leaves

spread it has the shape of a star, but when closed it somewhat resem‐

bles a heart, and for that reason it was given such a name. The tree

that bears it is quite large, and its leaves long and rough. (p)

The Coatzontecoxochitl, or Flower of the Viper's Head, is of incom‐

parable beauty. (q) It is composed of five petals,

I. plant that bears it has leaves resembling those of the iris, or flag, but longer and broader, and small, slender stalks. This flower was one of the most esteemed by the Mexicans.

The Oceloxochitl, or Tiger Flower, is large, composed of three pointed leaves, and red, but toward the middle variegated with white and yellow, and in some manner representing the spots of that beast, whence it took its name. The plant has leaves likewise resembling those of the flag, and a bulbous root.

The Cacaloxochitl, or Raven's Flower, is small, but very fragrant, and painted white, red, and yellow. The tree that bears these flowers is seen to be covered with them everywhere, forming at the ends of the branches natural bunches most pleasing both to the sight and to the smell. There is nothing more common than these flowers in the hot lands: the Indians use them to adorn their altars, and the Spaniards make delicious preserves of them. (r)

The Izquixochitl is a small white flower, resembling in shape the dog rose, or wild rose, and in taste the cultivated rose, but far superior to it in fragrance. It grows on large trees.

The Cempoalxochitl, or Cempasuchil as the Spaniards say, is the flower transplanted into Europe that the French call Œillet d'Inde, or Indian pink. It is very common in Mexico, where they also call it Flower of the Dead, and there are several different species of it, differing in size, in shape, and in the number of leaves of which they are composed.

name of Arbol de manitas.

Besides these and other innumerable flowers peculiar to that coun‐

try, in the cultivation of which the Mexicans delighted, the land of

Mexico was enriched with all those that were transplanted there from

Asia and from Europe, such as lilies, jasmines, pinks of various

species, and many others, which now in the gardens of Mexico vie

with the American flowers.

As for fruits, the land of Anahuac is indebted in part to the Canary

All the maritime lands abound in coconut palms. Of oranges there are seven very different species, and of lemons at least four. There are as many, and very different ones, of (u) Musa, or Platano, as the Spaniards call it. The largest, which is the Zapalote, is from fifteen to twenty inches in length, and up to three in diameter. It is hard, and little esteemed, nor is it eaten otherwise than roasted or cooked. The Platano largo, that is, long, is eight inches at most in length

(u) The Musa was not entirely unknown to the ancients. Pliny, citing the account that the soldiers of Alexander the Great gave of all that they saw in India, makes this description of it: Another tree is greater, and more excellent in its fruit and sweetness, on which the wise men of the Indians live. Its leaf imitates the wings of birds, three cubits in length, two in breadth. It puts forth its fruit from the rind, wonderful for the sweetness of its juice, so that one satisfies four. The name of the tree is pala, of the fruit ariena. Hist. Nat., bk. XII, ch. VI. Besides these characteristics proper to the Musa, it must be added that the name Palan, given to the Musa in those remote times, is preserved to this day in Malabar, as Garcia de Orta, a learned Portuguese physician who lived there many years, attests. It might be suspected that from the name Palan the name Platano has been derived, which so ill suits it. The name Bananas, which the French give it, is what it has in Guinea, and that of Musa, which the Italians give it, is taken from the Arabic language. By some it is called Fruit of Paradise, and there was no lack of those who persuaded themselves that this was precisely the fruit that made our first Fathers transgress.

(v) Several European writers on the things of America confuse the Chirimoya with

the Anona and with the Guanabana: but these are three different species of fruit,

Marcgrave, Piso, Barrère, Sloane, Ximénez, Ulloa, and many other naturalists: so that I shall speak only of some of those least known in Bk. I. Europe.

All the fruits comprised by the Mexicans under the generic name of Tzapotl are round, or approach roundness, and all have a hard stone. (x) The black Zapote has a thin rind, green, smooth, and tender, and the pulp black, soft, and sweetly flavored, which at first sight resembles that of cassia. (y) Inside the pulp it has flat blackish stones, no longer than a finger's breadth. It is perfectly spherical, and its diameter is from one and a half to four or five inches. The tree is of medium size, and dense, and its leaves small. The pulp of this fruit, iced and seasoned with sugar and cinnamon, is of a delicate taste.

The white Zapote, which for its narcotic virtue was called by the Mexicans Cochitzapotl, is somewhat like the black one in size, in shape, and in the color of the rind, though in the rind of the white the green is lighter; but in other respects it differs greatly, for the pulp of this one is white and delightful. Its stone, which is believed to be poisonous, is large, round, hard, and white. The tree is dense, and larger than the black one, and the leaves are larger still. Moreover, the black is proper to a hot climate, and the white, on the contrary, to a cold or temperate one.

The Chicozapote (in Mexican Chictzapotl) is of spherical shape, or approaches it, and measures an inch and a half or two inches in diameter. Its rind is gray, its pulp reddish white, and its stones black, hard, and pointed. From

Chicozapote, well ripened, is one of the most delicious fruits, and in

the opinion of many, even Europeans, superior to all the fruits of

orange tree. It grows without cultivation in the hot lands, and in the

Mixteca, in the Huasteca, and in Michoacán there are woods of such

trees twelve and fifteen miles long. (A)

The Capollino, or Capulin, as the Spaniards call it, is the cherry of

Mexico. The tree is hardly distinguishable from the cherry tree of

Europe, and the fruit is similar in size, in color, and in the stone; but

not so in flavor.

The Nance is a small round fruit, yellow, aromatic, and savory, with

very small seeds, which grows on trees proper to the hot lands.

The Chayote is a round fruit, and similar in the husk with which it

is covered to the chestnut, but four or five times larger, and of a deep‐

er green color. Its flesh is greenish white, and in the middle it has a

large stone, white and similar to the flesh in substance. It is eaten

cooked together with the stone. This fruit grows on a twining and

perennial plant, whose root is also good to eat.

The imprisoned nut is so called by the common people because

its

(z) Gemelli persuaded himself that the Chicle was a composition made on pur‐

pose; but he was mistaken, since it is nothing other than the simple milk of the un‐

ripe fruit condensed in the air. The aforesaid author makes mention of these fruits in

(A) Among the gross lies of Thomas Gage is that of saying that in the garden of

San Jacinto (the hospice of the Dominicans of the Mission of the Philippine Islands,

in a suburb of Mexico, where he lodged for several months) there were

p. 53

its almond is closely shut up inside a very hard shell. It is smaller than

Bk. I. the common walnut, and in shape it resembles the nutmeg; its shell is smooth, and its almond is sparser and of inferior taste to the common one. (B) The common walnut, brought there from Europe, has likewise multiplied greatly, and has become as common as in Europe itself.

The plant of the Tlalcacahuatl, or Cacahuate, as the Spaniards call it, is without doubt one of the rarest that are found there. It is an herb, but very bushy, and well furnished with roots. Its leaves are somewhat like those of purslane, but less thick. Its little flowers are white, and from them comes no fruit. Its fruit grows not on the branches or on the stem, as in other plants, but attached to the hairlike fibers of the roots, inside a white sheath, grayish, long, roundish, ridged, and rough, just as it is represented in our third figure among those of the flowers and fruits. Each sheath holds two, three, or four cacahuates, which have the shape of pine nuts, but are much larger and thicker, and each is composed, like so many other seeds, of two lobes, and has its germinating point. It is edible, and of good taste, not raw, but a little toasted. If they are toasted more, they take on a smell and a taste so like those of coffee that it is not difficult to deceive anyone. An oil is made from the cacahuates which is not of bad taste; but it is believed to be harmful, because it is too heating. It gives a fine light, but is easily put out. This plant would surely thrive in Italy. It is sown in March or in April, and the harvest is gathered in October or in November.

Among the very many other fruits which, to shorten my description, I pass over, I cannot refrain from making mention

of

(B) We speak only of the imprisoned nut of the Mexican empire, since that of New Mexico is larger, and of better flavor than the common walnut of Europe, according to what a man worthy of trust has told me. It may be believed that this walnut of New Mexico is the same as that of Louisiana, called Pacana, or Pacaria.

of the cacao, the vanilla, the chia, the chilli, or capsicum, the tomato, Bk. I.

Tabasco pepper, cotton, and the grains and pulses most in use among

the Mexicans.

Of the cacao (a name taken from the Mexican Cacahuatl) Dr.

Hernández counts four species; but the Tlalcacahuatl, the smallest of

all, was the one the Mexicans most commonly used in their chocolate

and in other daily drinks, while the other species served them more

as money for trading in the market than as food. The cacao was one

of the most cultivated plants in the hot lands of that kingdom; and

several provinces paid tribute in it to the Crown of Mexico, among

others that of Soconusco, whose cacao is excellent and better not

only than that of Caracas, but also than that of Magdalena. The de‐

scription of this celebrated plant, and of its cultivation, is found in

many authors of all the civilized nations of Europe.

Vanilla, or Vainiglia, so well known and so much used in Europe,

grows without cultivation in the hot lands. The ancient Mexicans

used it in their chocolate and in other drinks which they made of

cacao.

The chia is the small seed of a beautiful plant, whose stem is

straight and quadrangular, the branches spread toward the four quar‐

ters and symmetrically opposed, and the little flowers blue. There are

two species, the one black and small, from which an excellent oil for

painting is drawn, and the other white and larger, from which a re‐

freshing drink is made. Both the one and the other were used by the

Mexicans for these and other purposes, which we shall see later.

Of the chilli, or capsicum, (C) which was as common among the

Mexicans as salt among the Europeans, there are at least eleven

species, differing in size, in shape, and in pungency. The Quauhchilli,

which is the fruit of a

shrub

(C) In other countries of America they call the chilli Axì, in Spain Pimiento, and in

France Poivre de Guinée, and by other names. In Italian I use the name by which it is

known in the places of Italy where I have been.

shrub, and the Chiltecpin are the smallest, but also the most pungent.

Bk. I. Of the tomato there are six species, distinct in size, in color, and in taste. The largest, which is the Xictomatl, or Xitomate, as the Spaniards of Mexico call it, is already very common in Europe: in Spain and in France under the name of Tomate (D), and in Italy under that of Pomo d’oro. The Miltomatl is smaller, green, and perfectly round. How much both were used by the Mexicans at their meals will be told later, when we come to speak of their foods.

The Xocoxochitl, commonly known by the name of Tabasco pepper because it abounds in that province, is larger than the pepper of Malabar. It grows on a large tree, whose leaves have the color and the luster of those of the orange tree; and the flowers are a fine red, and similar in shape to those of the pomegranate, and of a lively and most pleasing odor, in which the branches also share. The fruit is round and grows in clusters, which, being green at first, afterwards turn almost black. This pepper, already used by the ancient Mexicans, can take the place of that of Malabar.

Cotton was, by reason of its usefulness, one of the most considerable fruits of that country: for it took the place of flax (although this plant, too, was not lacking to them (E)), and of it the inhabitants of Anahuac for the most part clothed themselves. There is a white kind, and a tawny kind, commonly called coyote. It is a plant very common in the hot lands, but much more cultivated by the ancients than by the moderns.

The

(D) The Tomatl of the Mexicans is the generic name of all the fruits of that class. The Spaniards of Europe and the French adopted it to signify the Xictomatl, which is the species known to them, and the Spaniards of Mexico to signify the Miltomatl, which in that country is the most usual.

(E) Flax was in fact found in Michoacán, in New Mexico, and in Quivira in great abundance, and of excellent quality; but we do not know that those nations cultivated it or made use of it. The Court of Spain, aware of the lands in the kingdom of Mexico that are suited to the cultivation of flax and hemp, sent to that country last year (1778) twelve peasant families from the Vega of Granada, so that they might employ themselves in this branch of agriculture.

p. 56

The fruit of the Achiote, called Rocou by the French, served at that Bk. I. time for the dyes of the Mexicans, as it serves now for those of the

Europeans. Of the bark of the tree they made cords, and with the

wood they drew fire by friction, after the custom of the ancient shep‐

herds of Europe. This plant is well described in the Dictionary of M.

de Bomare.

As for the grains and pulses properly so called, that land received

from Europe wheat, barley, rice, chickpeas, peas, broad beans, lentils,

and others; all of which throve happily in lands suited to their nature,

and have multiplied there as much as we shall show in our disserta‐

tions. (F)

Among the grains the chief, the most useful, and the most usual

was maize, called by the Mexicans Tlaolli, of which there are many

species differing in size, in color, in weight, and in taste. There is a

large kind and a small, white, yellow, blue, purple, red, and black. Of

maize the Mexicans made their bread and several dishes, of which we

shall speak elsewhere. Maize was carried from America to Spain, and

thence to other countries of Europe, to the great advantage of the

poor, although there is no lack in our day of an author who claims to

make America indebted to Europe for maize. A notion truly the most

extravagant and the most improbable that can enter a man’s head. (G)

(F) Dr. Hernández describes in his Natural History of Mexico the species of wheat

that was found in Michoacán, and boasts of its prodigious fecundity; but the an‐

cients either did not know how, or else did not wish, to make use of it, valuing more

highly, as they still do today, their maiz, or maize. The first who sowed European

wheat in that land was a Black slave of the conqueror Cortés, who had found three

or four grains inside a sack of rice that was carried for the provisioning of the

Spanish soldiers.

The principal pulse of the Mexicans was that of the beans, of which there are several species, and much more varied than those of Bk. I. maize. The largest species is that of the Ayacotli, which is the size of a broad bean, and springs from a handsome red flower; but the most esteemed is that of certain small, black, heavy beans. This pulse, which in Italy is little esteemed, because poor, is in Mexico so good that it serves not only for the sustenance of poor people, but also for the delicacies of the Spanish nobility.

As for the plants valued for their root, their leaves, their stem, or § 8.

Plants valued for their wood, the Mexicans had very many that served them for food, their root, for their

leaves, for their stem, such as the Xicama, the Camote, the Huacamote, the Cacomite, and oth‐ or for their wood. ers, or else that provided them with thread for their cloths and their cords, such as the Iczotl, and several species of Metl or maguei, or again that furnished them with wood for buildings and other works, such as the cedar, the pine, the cypress, the fir, the ebony, etc.

The Xicama, called Catzotl by the Mexicans, is a root of the shape and the size of an onion, all white, firm, fresh, juicy, and savory, which is always eaten raw.

The Camote is another root, most common in all that land, of which there are three species, one white, another yellow, and another purple. Cooked camotes are of good taste, especially those of Querétaro, which are deservedly prized throughout the kingdom. (*)

The Cacomite is the edible root of the plant that bears the beautiful Tiger Flower, which we have already described

By the Spaniards of Europe and of America maize is called by the name Maiz, taken from the Haitian language that was spoken in the island now called Hispaniola, or Santo Domingo.

(*) Many call the camotes Batate, or Patate; but I have avoided this name, because it is equivocal and is used indifferently by authors to signify both the camotes and the potatoes, which are quite different roots.

p. 58

The Huacamote is the sweet root of a species of Jucca (H), which is Bk. I.

also eaten cooked. The potato, a root transplanted to Europe and

much esteemed in Ireland and in Sweden, was also carried to Mexico

from South America, its own country, just as there were brought

thither from Spain and from the Canaries several other roots and sal‐

ad plants, such as turnips, radishes, carrots, garlic, lettuces, asparagus,

cabbages, and the like. Of onions, Cortés bears witness in his letters

to Charles V that they were sold in the markets of Mexico: so that

they had no need to have them brought from Europe. Moreover, the

name Xonacatl, which they give to the onion, and that of Xonacatepec,

by which a place has been known since the time of the Mexican

kings, make us know that this plant was very ancient in that land, and

not transplanted from Europe.

The Maguei, called Metl by the Mexicans, Pita by the Spaniards,

and by many authors American aloe, since it is in fact very like the true

aloe, is among the most common and most useful plants of Mexico.

Dr. Hernández describes as many as nineteen species of it, still more

different in the inner substance than in the form and the color of

their leaves. In Book VII of our History we shall have occasion to set

forth the great advantages which the Mexicans drew from these

plants, and the incredible profit which the Spaniards draw from them

today.

The Iczotl is a species of mountain palm, quite tall, which for the

most part has a doubled trunk. Its branches have the shape of a fan,

and its leaves that of a sword. Its flowers are white and fragrant, of

which the Spaniards make good preserves, and its fruit is like the

Musa at first sight, but quite useless. Of the leaves they made in an‐

cient times, and still make today, fine mats, and the Mexicans drew

thread from them for their manufactures.

This is not the only palm of that country. Besides the

royal

(H) The Jucca is the plant from whose root they make the bread called Casave in

some countries of America.

p. 59

royal palm, which surpasses the others in the beauty of its branches, the coconut palm, and those of dates, (I) there are other species wor‐ Bk. I. thy of mention.

The Quauhcojolli is a palm of middling size, whose trunk is inaccessible to quadrupeds, being entirely armed with long, strong, and very sharp spines. Its branches have the shape of a handsome plume, among which its fruits are seen hanging in large clusters, round, as large as common walnuts, and like them composed of four parts, namely a husk at first green and afterwards dark, a yellow pulp tenaciously attached to the stone, a round and very hard stone, and inside the stone an almond, or white marrow.

The palm Ixhuatl is smaller, and has no more than six or seven branches, for where a new branch sprouts, one of the old ones soon dries up. Of its leaves they made baskets and mats, and today they make hats also and other articles. Its bark, to the depth of three finger-breadths, is nothing but a mass of membranes about a foot long, thin and flexible, but otherwise strong, of which, by joining many together, some poor people make their mattresses.

The palm Teoiczotl is also small. The pith of its trunk, which is soft, is surrounded by certain leaves of a peculiar substance, round, thick, white, smooth, and shining, which look like so many shells piled one upon another, and which the Indians used anciently, and still use today, for the ornament of the arches of boughs that they make for their festivals.

There is another palm, which bears the oil nuts (called by the Spaniards cocos de aceite), so named because a good oil is drawn from them. The oil nut is a nut similar in shape and in size to the nutmeg,

(I) Besides the date palm proper to that country, there is also that of Barbary. Dates are sold in the month of June in the markets of Mexico City, Puebla, and other cities, but in spite of their sweetness they are not much sought after.

As for the excellence, the variety, and the abundance of woods,

that country yields to none in the world; for since no sort of climate

is lacking there, neither are the trees proper to each lacking. Besides

the oaks, the durmast oaks, the firs, the pines, the cypresses, the

beeches, the elms, the walnuts, the poplars, and very many others

common in Europe, there are whole forests of cedars and of ebonies,

the two kinds of trees most esteemed by the ancients; there is a great

quantity of agalloch, or aloes-wood, in the Mixteca, of Tapinzeran in

Michoacán, of Caoba in Chiapas, of Palo gateado, which we might call

striped wood, in Zoncoliuhcan (today Zongolica), of Camote in the

mountains of Texcoco, of Granadillo, or red ebony, in the Mixteca and

elsewhere, of Mizquitl or true acacia, of Tepehuaxin, of Coptè, of Jabin,

of Guayacan, or lignum vitae, of Ayaquahuitl, of Oyametl, of Zopilote

wood, and of other innumerable woods valued for their incorruptibil‐

ity, for their hardness and weight, (K) for their pliancy or ease of carv‐

ing, for the beauty of their colors, or for their fragrance. The Camote

has a most beautiful purple color, and the Granadillo a dark red; but

still more beautiful are the Palo gateado, the Caoba, and the

Tzopiloquahuitl, or Zopilote wood. The hardness of the Guayacan is al‐

ready well known in Europe, and that of the Jabin is no less. The

aloes-wood of the Mixteca, although different from the true agalloch

of the Levant, judging by the description

There is also in that country a tree whose wood is precious, but otherwise of so malignant a nature that it causes swelling of the scrotum in anyone who handles it carelessly when it has newly been cut. The name which the Michoacans give it, which I do not remember, expresses that bad effect plainly. I have not been a witness of such a phenomenon, nor have I seen the tree; but I learned of it when I was in Michoacán from persons worthy of trust.

Dr. Hernández describes in his Natural History about a hundred species of trees; but since he devoted his study principally, as we have already said, to medicinal plants, he leaves out the greater part of the trees which that most fertile land bears, and especially those most considerable for their size and for the preciousness of their wood. There are trees of a height and breadth so surprising that they are in no way inferior to those that Pliny boasts of as miracles of Nature.

Father Acosta makes mention of a cedar that was in Atlacuechahuayan, a place nine miles distant from Antequera, or Oaxaca, whose trunk had a circumference of sixteen brazas, that is, more than eighty-two Paris feet; and I have myself seen in a country house a beam a hundred and twenty Castilian feet long, or a hundred and seven Paris feet. In several houses of the capital, and of other cities of the kingdom, very large cedar planks of a single piece are to be seen. In the valley of Atlixco there is still preserved alive a very ancient fir (L), so large that in the cavity in its trunk made by

Comparable also with this famous fir are the Ceibas which I saw in

the maritime province of Jicayán. The breadth of these trees is pro‐

portioned to their prodigious height, and their prospect is supremely

delightful in the season when they are seen adorned with new foliage,

and laden with fruit, inside which is a certain kind of cotton, white,

fine, and most delicate. Cloths could be made of it, and in fact have

been made, as soft and delicate as those of silk, and perhaps even

more so; (M) but it is hard to spin on account of the smallness of the

threads, besides which the advantage would be less than the labor,

since the cloth would have to be of little durability. Some use that

cotton in cushions and mattresses, which have the peculiarity of

swelling enormously in the heat of the sun.

The fruit of this tree is quite useless, but the wood is good. (N)

As for the plants, finally, that yield us resins, gums, oils, or prof‐ § 9. itable juices, the land of Anahuac is most singularly fertile in them, as Plants useful for their

resins, gums, oils, and Acosta acknowledges in his Natural History. juices.

The Huitziloxitl, from which the balsam distills, is a tree of middling height. Its leaves are somewhat like those of the almond tree, but larger; its wood is reddish and fragrant, and the bark ashen, but covered with a reddish film. Its pale flowers come at the ends of the branches. Its seed is small, whitish, and curved, and comes likewise at the end of a thin husk a finger long. At whatever point an incision is made, especially after rains, there distills from it that most noble resin so greatly esteemed in Europe, and in no way inferior to the renowned balsam of Palestine. (O) Our balsam is reddish black, or else whitish yellow; for of the one color and of the other it flows from the incision; of an acrid and bitter taste, and of an intense but most agreeable odor. The balsam tree is common in the provinces of Pánuco and of Chiapas,

and

(N) Father Andrés Pérez de Ribas, in his History of the Missions of Sinaloa, and M. de Bomare, in his Dictionary under the names of Figuier des Indes, Grand Figuier, and Figuier admirable, make mention of this singular fig tree. The historians of the East Indies describe another tree like this, which is found in those regions.

(O) The first balsam that was brought from Mexico to Rome was sold at a hundred ducats the ounce, as Dr. Monardes attests in his History of the Medicinal Simples of America; and it was declared by the Apostolic See to be fit matter for chrism, though different from that of Palestine, as Acosta and other historians of America observe.

and in other hot lands. The Mexican kings had it transplanted to the Bk. I. celebrated garden of Oaxtepec, where it throve happily, and multi‐

draw more balsam from it, after making an incision in the tree, burn

its branches. The abundance of these precious plants makes them dis‐

regard the loss of a great number of them, so as not to have to wait

for the slowness of the distillation. Not only did the ancient

Mexicans gather the opobalsamum, or tear, distilled from the trunk,

but they also obtained the xylobalsamum by the decoction of the

branches. (P) From the Huaconex, and from the Maripenda (*) they ob‐

tained likewise an oil equivalent to balsam. The Huaconex is a tree of

middling height, and of aromatic and hard wood, which keeps uncor‐

rupted for years, even when buried in the earth. Its leaves are small

and yellow, the flowers likewise small and whitish, and the fruit like

that of the laurel. They drew the oil by distillation from the bark of

the tree, after breaking it up, keeping it three days in ordinary water,

and then drying it in the sun. They drew also from the leaves an oil of

agreeable odor. The Maripenda is a shrub, whose leaves have the

shape of a lance head, and the fruit is like the grape, and comes in

clusters, at first green, then red. They drew the oil by the decoction

of the branches mixed with some fruit.

The Xochiocotzotl, commonly called liquidambar, is the liquid

storax of the Mexicans. The tree is large (not merely a small shrub, as

Pluche says); its leaves are somewhat like those of the maple,

toothed, whitish on one side, dark on the other, and arranged in

threes. The fruit is spiny and roundish, but polygonal, with black sur‐

faces and yellow angles. The bark of the tree is in part green and in

part tawny. From the trunk is drawn by incision

p. 65

that precious resin, which the Spaniards called Liquidambar, and the oil of the same name, which is still more fragrant and valuable. Bk. I. Liquidambar is also drawn by the decoction of the branches, but it is inferior to that which distills from the tree.

The Mexican name Copalli is generic, and common to all resins, but especially signifies those that serve for incense. There are as many as ten species of trees that yield these sorts of resin, differing among themselves not so much in name as in the form of the foliage and of the fruit, and in the quality of the resin. The Copal, by antonomasia, is a white and transparent resin, which distills from a large tree, whose leaves resemble those of the oak, but are larger, and the fruit is roundish and reddish. This resin is well known in Europe under the name of gum copal, and the use made of it both in medicine and in varnishes is also well known. The ancient Mexicans used it principally in the incensings which they made, now for the religious worship of their idols, now as homage to ambassadors and other persons of the first rank. Today they consume a great quantity of it in the worship of the true God and of his saints. The Tecopalli, or Tepecopalli, is a resin like the frankincense of Arabia in color, in odor, and in taste, which distills from a tree of middling size, that grows in the mountains, whose fruit is like an acorn containing a pine nut moistened with a mucilage, or viscous saliva, and inside it a little almond, which is usefully employed in medicine. Both these trees, and all the others of this class, in whose description we cannot linger, are proper to hot lands.

The caranna and the tacamahac, resins well known in the apothecaries’ shops of Europe, distill from two very large Mexican trees. The tree of the caranna (*) has a tawny trunk, smooth,

The Mizquitl, or Mezquite, as the Spaniards call it, is a species of

true acacia, and the gum that distills from it is true gum arabic, as Dr.

Hernández and other learned naturalists bear witness. The mesquite

is a thorny shrub, whose branches are very irregularly arranged, and

the leaves, thin, slender, and conjugate, that is, growing on one side

and the other of the branches, resemble the feathers of birds; its

flowers are similar to those of the birch. Its fruits are sweet and edi‐

ble pods, and contain the seed, of which the barbarous Chichimecs

anciently made a paste that served them for bread. Its wood is very

hard and heavy. These trees are almost as common in Mexico as oaks

in Europe, especially on the hills of the temperate countries. (Q)

The lac, or gum lac (as it is wont to be called by apothecaries),

flows in such abundance from a tree resembling the mesquite that the

branches are covered with it. (R)

Dragon’s blood distills from a large tree, whose leaves are broad and angular. This tree grows in the mountains of Huauchinango and in those of the Cohuixcas. (S)

The elastic resin, called Olin or Olli by the Mexicans, and Ule by the Spaniards of that kingdom, distills from the Olquahuitl, which is a tree of sufficient size, whose trunk is smooth and yellowish, the leaves quite large, the flowers white, and the fruit yellow and roundish, but angular, inside which there are almonds as large as hazelnuts, white, but covered with a yellowish skin. The almond has a bitter taste, and the fruit is always attached to the bark of the tree. The Ule, as it distills from the wounded trunk, is white, liquid, and viscous: then it becomes yellowish, and finally takes the color of lead, somewhat blacker, which it keeps from then on. Those who gather it have it take in various molds whatever form they wish, according to the use for which they intend it. This resin, condensed, is of all solid bodies the most elastic that we know.

The Mexicans made their balls of this resin, which, though heavy, bound higher than those of air. Today, besides this use, they use it, as wax is used in Europe, for making hats, boots, greatcoats, and cloaks

I2 impervious Tlahuicas, where all the fruits grow marvelously well, and from there a very great quantity is carried to the capital. Now so great a harvest of fruits could not be made if there were in that land as many millions of ants as would be needed for the making of so excessive a quantity of lac, since the trees are very many, and almost all of them are full of it. 4. The Quauhxiotl is a tree of middling size, whose leaves are round,

and the bark reddish. There are two subordinate species of it: one

yields a white gum, which, put in water, tinges it a milky color; the

other distills a reddish gum, both most useful for dysentery.

In this class of plants there should find a place the fir, the higuerilla

(a plant like the fig tree), and the ocote, a certain species of very aro‐

matic pine, for the oil they yield; and brazil-wood, logwood or

Campeachy wood, indigo, and many others for their juices; but sever‐

al of these plants are already known in Europe, and of others we shall

have occasion to speak elsewhere.

That small part of the vegetable kingdom of Anahuac which we

have so far set forth renews in us the sorrow we feel at seeing van‐

ished, and for the most part lost, that exact knowledge of natural his‐

tory which the ancient Mexicans had acquired. We know that those

woods, those mountains, and those valleys are full of infinite plants

most useful and precious, without finding anyone who is willing to

turn his gaze to examine them. Whom will it not grieve that of the

immense treasures drawn during the last two centuries and a half

from those richest mines, no part has been set aside to found acad‐

emies of naturalists, who, following in the footsteps of the most dis‐

tinguished Hernández, might discover for the benefit of society those

precious gifts which the Creator has so liberally bestowed upon

them?

§ 10. The animal kingdom is no less unknown, despite the diligence Quadrupeds of the kingdom of Mexico. which Dr. Hernández applied in this part also. The difficulty there is

in distinguishing the species, and

the

(T) In Michoacán there is a tree called by the Tarascans Tarantaqua, of the same

species as the Olquahuitl, but different in the leaves.

p. 69

the impropriety of the nomenclature caused by analogy, have made

Bk. I. the history of animals difficult and arduous. The first Spanish nomenclators, more versed in the art of war than in the study of nature, instead of retaining, as would have been better, the names which the Mexicans gave to their animals, called several animals of very different species tigers, wolves, bears, dogs, squirrels, etc., either from the similarity of the color of the skin, or of other features, or from conformity in certain operations and properties. Now I do not pretend to correct their errors, nor even to illustrate the natural history of that vast kingdom; but only to give my readers some idea of the quadrupeds, the birds, the reptiles, the fish, and the insects that live on the land and in the waters of Anahuac.

Among the quadrupeds some are new, and others ancient. The new ones (so we call those which in the sixteenth century were carried to that land from the Canaries and from Europe) are the horses, the asses, the bulls, the sheep, the goats, the hogs, the dogs, and the cats, all of which have multiplied as happily and as greatly as we shall show in our Fourth Dissertation against certain philosophers of the age, who have taken it upon themselves to persuade us of the degradation of all the quadrupeds in the New World.

Of the ancient quadrupeds, that is, of those which from time immemorial have bred in that land, some were common to both continents, others peculiar to the New World, but yet common to Mexico and to other countries of North or South America, and others finally proper only to the kingdom of Mexico.

The ancient quadrupeds common to Mexico and to the old continent are the lions, the tigers, the wild cats, the bears, the wolves, the foxes, the deer, both the common and the white (U), the fallow deer, the wild goats, the badgers, the beech martens, the

weasels

(U) The white deer, whether of the same species or of a species distinct from the other deer, are undoubtedly common to both continents

p. 70

weasels, the martens, the squirrels, the polatouches, the rabbits, the Bk. I.

hares, the otters, and the mice. I know well that M. de Buffon denies

to America the lions, the tigers, and the rabbits; but since his opinion

rests chiefly on the weak foundation of the alleged impossibility of

the passage to the new continent of animals proper to the hot lands

of the old world, and is sufficiently contested by us in our disserta‐

tions, there is no need to interrupt the course of our History to re‐

The Miztli of the Mexicans is nothing other than the maneless lion

mentioned by Pliny, (V) and quite distinct from the lion of Africa;

and the Ocelotl is not different from the African tiger, as Dr.

Hernández, who knew both, bears witness. The Tochtli of Mexico is

the same rabbit as that of the old continent, and at least as ancient in

that country as the calendar of the Mexicans, in which the figure of

the rabbit was the first character of their years. The wild cats, much

larger than the domestic ones, are very fierce and feared. The bears

are all black, and more corpulent than those that are seen in Italy,

brought from the Alps. The hares are distinguished from the

European by having longer ears, and the wolves by having a larger

head. Both species abound too much in that land. Polatouche we call,

with M. de Buffon, the Quimichpatlan, or flying mouse of the

Mexicans. They called it mouse because it is like one in the head,

though larger; and flying, because, having in its natural state the skin

of the flanks loose and wrinkled, when it wants to make a violent leap

from one tree to another, it stretches

and

They were known to the Greeks and to the Romans. The Mexicans called them

kings of the deer. M. de Buffon would persuade us that whiteness in deer is an effect

of their servitude; but since there are found in the mountains of New Spain, as in

fact there are, white deer that have never been slaves of men, such a thought no

longer has any place.

The rats were brought to Mexico by European ships; not so the mice, which were always known to the Mexicans under the name of Quimichin, which they also gave metaphorically to their spies. (*)

The species of quadrupeds that were in the kingdom of Mexico, common also to other countries of the New World, are the Cojametl, the Epatl, several species of monkeys, comprised by the Spaniards under the generic name of Monos, the Ajotochtli, the Aztacojotl, the Tlacuatzin, the Techichi, the Tlalmototli, the Techallotl, the Amiztli, the Mapach, and the Danta (X).

The Cojametl, to which the Spaniards give, because of its resemblance to the boar, the name of Javali, or wild hog, is called in other countries of America Pecar, Saino, and Tayassù. That gland which it has in a cavity of the back, from which a watery and stinking liquid abundantly distills, led the first historians of America, and from them many other authors, into the error of believing that there were found in America hogs which had the navel on the back; and even now there are those who believe so, although it is now more than two centuries

since

(*) Although in the Tuscan language the names Topo and Sorcio are used indiscriminately, as in Latin the names Mus and Sorex, there being nevertheless two species, as M. de Buffon says, the one larger and fiercer, the other smaller and more domestic, we give to the latter the name Sorcio (Sorex), and to the former the name Topo (Mus), in order to distinguish them. The Spaniards distinguish the species well, calling the large one Rata and the small one Raton: the latter was in America, the other was brought from Europe, and has in Peru the name of Pericote.

(X) Several authors count among the animals of Mexico the Paco, or Peruvian sheep, the Huanaco, the vicuña, the taruca, and the sloth; but all these quadrupeds are proper to South America, and none of them to North America. It is true that Dr. Hernández makes mention among the quadrupeds of New Spain of the Paco, gives its portrait, and uses the Mexican name Pelonichcatl; but he did so on account of some individuals brought there from Peru, to which the Mexicans gave that name, as he also describes several animals of the Philippine Islands; yet they have never been bred in Mexico, nor are they found in any country of North America, unless perhaps some individual taken there out of curiosity, as one has been taken to Europe.

p. 72

since the error was discovered through the anatomy of that animal. Bk. I. So hard is it to uproot popular opinions! The flesh of the Cojametl is

good to eat, provided that as soon as it is killed the gland is cut out

and all that stinking liquid is removed from the back; for otherwise

the whole of the flesh would be tainted.

The Epatl, called Zorrillo by the Spaniards, is less known in Europe

for the beauty of its skin than for the insufferable stench it gives off

when the hunters are upon it. (Y)

The Tlacuatzin, which in other countries has the names Churcha,

Sarigua, and Opossum, has been described by many authors, and is

quite famous for the doubled skin which the female has on her belly,

from the beginning of the stomach to the orifice of the womb; it cov‐

ers her teats and has in the middle an opening, where she receives her

young after bearing them, to keep them well guarded and to suckle

them. In walking, and in climbing the walls of houses, she holds the

skin stretched out and the opening shut, so that the young cannot get

out; but when she wishes to send them out, that they may begin to

seek their own food, or to bring them in again, whether to suckle

them or to snatch them from some danger, she opens the passage by

loosening the skin, counterfeiting pregnancy while she carries them

and birth every time she sets them out. This curious quadruped is the

destroyer of poultry-yards.

The Ajotochtli, called Armadillo or Encobertado by the Spaniards,

and by others Tatú, is also well known to Europeans

for

(Y) M. de Buffon counts four species of Epatl under the generic name of

Mouffettes. He then says that the first two, which he calls Coaso and Conepata, belong

to North America, and the Chincho and the Zorrillo, which are the other two, to

South America. We find no reason to believe them four different species, but only

four breeds of one and the same species. The names which the Mexicans give to the

first two breeds are Izquiepatl and Conepatl, which breeds are distinguished only in

size and color. The name Coaso or Squash, taken from the traveler Dampier, who af‐

firms that it is common in New Spain, has never been heard in that whole land. The

Indians of Yucatán, where the said traveler stayed, call that quadruped Pai.

p. 73

for those bony plates with which it keeps its back covered, in imitation of the ancient armor of horses. The Mexicans gave it that name Bk. I. for the resemblance, though imperfect, which it bears to the rabbit when it puts out its head, and to the gourd when it takes shelter beneath its plates, or shells; (Z) but it resembles no other animal more than the tortoise, although in several features it is not a little unlike it: one might give it the name of testaceous quadruped. Whenever this animal finds itself pursued on some plain, it has no way of escaping from the hands of the hunter; but since it ordinarily inhabits the mountains, where it finds some slope, it curls up, makes itself into a ball, and, rolling down the hillside, leaves the hunter mocked.

The Techichi, which elsewhere had the name of Alco, was a quadruped of Mexico and of other countries of America, which, because it was like a small dog in shape, was called Perro, that is, dog, by the Spaniards. It was of a melancholy aspect and altogether mute: whence arose the fable, spread by several authors still living, that all the dogs carried over from the old world become mute in the new. The flesh of the Techichi was eaten by the Mexicans, and, if we give credit to the Spaniards who still ate it, was of good taste and nourishing. The Spaniards after the Conquest of Mexico, having as yet neither herds nor flocks, supplied their slaughterhouses with these quadrupeds; so that in a short time they consumed the species, although it was numerous.

The Tlalmototli, or ground squirrel, called the Swiss by M. de Buffon, resembles the true squirrel in the eyes, the tail, the swiftness, and in all its movements

squirrel does, in trees, but in the small burrows which it digs in the

in which it does considerable damage because of the great quantity of

grain that it steals from them. It bites furiously anyone who ap‐

quadrupeds is among the most numerous, especially in the kingdom

of Michoacán. The Techallotl is hardly to be distinguished from the

aforesaid animals, except in having a smaller and less hairy tail.

The Amiztli, or water lion, is an amphibious quadruped that lives

on the shores of the Pacific Ocean and in some rivers of that king‐

dom. Its body is three feet long, and its tail two. Its muzzle is long, its

legs short, and its claws curved. Its skin is valuable for the length and

softness of the hair. (aa)

The Mapach of the Mexicans is, in the opinion of M. de Buffon,

the very same quadruped as is known in Jamaica by the name of

Ratton. The Mexican one is the size of a badger, with a black head, a

long and slender muzzle like that of the greyhound, small ears, a

rounded body, hair variegated with black and white, a long and well-

furred tail, and five toes on each foot. Above each eye it has a little

white band, and, like the squirrel, it uses its paws to carry to its

mouth what it wishes to eat. It feeds indifferently on grain, fruit, in‐

sects, lizards, and the blood of hens. It is easily tamed, and is very

graceful in its play; but

it

(aa) We count the Amiztli among the quadrupeds common to other countries of

America, because it seems to us to be the very one that M. de Buffon describes for us

under the name of Saricovienne.

p. 75

it is treacherous like the squirrel itself, and is apt to bite its master.

Bk. I.

The Danta, or Anta, or Beori, or Tapir (for it has various names in various countries) is the bulkiest quadruped of all that were in the lands of the Mexican kingdom, (bb) and the one that comes nearest to the hippopotamus, not only in size but in certain features and in some properties. The Danta is as large as a middling mule. Its body is somewhat arched like that of the hog; its head is thick and long, with an appendage in the skin of the upper lip, which it widens or shortens at will; its eyes are small, its ears small and round, its legs short, its forefeet have four hooves and its hind feet three, its tail is short and pyramidal; its skin is very thick and clothed with dense hair, which in maturity is brown. Its dentition, made up of twenty molars and as many incisors, is so strong and sharp, and the bites it inflicts are so terrible, that it has been seen, as the historian Oviedo attests as an eyewitness, to tear away at one stroke of its teeth a span or two of skin from a hunting dog, and from another a whole leg with the thigh. Its flesh is edible, (cc) and its skin valuable, since it is so strong that it resists not only arrows but even musket balls. This quadruped inhabits the solitary woods of the hot lands, near some river or little lake, since it lives no less in the water than on land.

All the species of monkeys that are in that kingdom are known to the Mexicans under the general name of Ozomatli, and to the Spaniards under that of Monos. There are some of various sizes and shapes: small ones and singularly

As for the anteaters, those quadrupeds so singular for the enor‐

mous length of their snout, the narrowness of their throat, and their

immense tongue, which they use to draw ants out of the anthills,

whence they got their name, I have never seen them in that kingdom,

nor do I know that there are any there; but I believe that the

Aztacojotl, that is, ant-eating coyote, mentioned but not described by

Dr. Hernández, is of no other species. (ee)

The quadrupeds most peculiar to the land of Anahuac, whose

species I do not know to be found in South America, nor in other

countries of North America not subject to the Crown of Spain, were

the Cojotl, the Tlalcojotl, the Xoloitzcuintli, the Tepeitzcuintli, the

Itzcuintepotzotli, the Ocotochtli, the Cojopollin, the Tuza, the Ahuitzotl,

the Huitztlacuatzin, and others perhaps unknown to us.

The Cojotl, or Coyote as the Spaniards call it, is a wild beast resem‐

bling the wolf in voracity, the fox in cunning, the dog in form, and in

the jackal; whence several historians of Mexico have assigned it now to one species, now to another; but it is nonetheless indubitably dif‐ Bk. I. ferent from all of these, as we demonstrate in our dissertations. It is smaller than the wolf, and as large as a mastiff, but leaner. It has yellow, sparkling eyes, small, pointed, erect ears, a blackish muzzle, strong legs, feet armed with thick, curved claws, a thick and hairy tail, and a skin variegated with black, dusky brown, and white. Its voice has something of the howl of the wolf and of the barking of the dog. The Coyote is among the most common quadrupeds in Mexico, (ff) and among those most harmful to flocks. It assails a herd, and where it finds no lamb to carry off, it seizes a sheep by the neck with its teeth, and, joined to it in this way and beating its rump with its tail, leads it where it wishes. It pursues deer, and sometimes even attacks men. In flight it commonly does nothing but trot; but this trot is so lively and so swift that a horse at the gallop can hardly overtake it. The Cuetlachcojotl seems to us a quadruped of the same species as the Coyote; for it is not distinguished from it except by having a thicker neck and hair like that of the wolf.

The Tlalcojotl, or Tlalcoyote, is the size of a middling dog, but stouter, and is in our opinion the bulkiest of all the quadrupeds that live underground. In the head it somewhat resembles the cat, and the lion in color and in the length of its hair. It has a long, bushy tail, and feeds on hens and other small animals, which it hunts in the darkness of the night.

The Itzcuintepotzotli, the Tepeitzcuintli, and the Xoloitzcuintli were three species of quadrupeds resembling dogs. The Itzcuintepotzotli, or humpbacked dog, was as large as a Maltese dog, and its skin was variegated with white, tawny, and

black

(ff) Neither M. de Buffon nor M. de Bomare makes mention of the Coyote, although its species is the most common among wild beasts and the most numerous in Mexico, and is sufficiently described by Dr. Hernández, whose Natural History they often cite.

black. Its head was small in proportion to the body, and seemed Bk. I. joined directly to it because of the smallness and thickness of the

a wild beast so small that it looks like a puppy, yet so bold that it at‐

tacks deer, and sometimes kills them. Its hair is long, as is the tail,

and the body black, but the head, neck, and breast white. (*) The

Xoloitzcuintli is larger than the two preceding, for there are some

whose body is as much as four feet long. Its face is that of a dog, but

its fangs those of a wolf, its ears erect, its neck thick, and its tail long.

The most singular thing about this animal is that it is entirely with‐

out hair, except upon the muzzle, where it has a few thick and twist‐

ed bristles. Its whole body is covered with a smooth, soft skin of ash-

gray color, but partly spotted with black and tawny. These three

species of quadrupeds have been entirely consumed, or at least few

individuals of them remain. (gg)

The Cojopollin is a quadruped as large as an ordinary rat; but it has a thicker tail, and uses it in place of a hand. The muzzle and the ears are like those of a little pig; the ears are transparent, the legs and feet white, and the belly yellowish white. It lives and rears its young in trees. The young, when they are afraid, cling tightly to their mother.

The Tozan, or Tuza, is a quadruped equivalent to the mole of Europe, but very different. Its body, which is well formed, is seven or eight inches long. Its muzzle is like that of the rat, its ears small and round, and its tail short. Its mouth is armed with very strong teeth, and its feet furnished with strong, curved claws, with which it digs the earth and makes for itself the burrows where it lives. The Tuza is most pernicious to the fields because of the grain it steals, and to travelers because of the many burrows or holes it makes in the roads; for where, because of its poor sight, it does not find the first burrow, it makes another, thus multiplying the inconveniences and dangers for those who travel on horseback. It digs the earth with its paws and with two canine teeth, larger than the others, which it has in the upper jaw; in digging it puts the earth into two membranes made in the manner of pouches, which it has beneath the ears, furnished with the muscles necessary for their contraction and dilation. When it has filled the membranes, it empties them by shaking the bottoms of the membranes with its paws, and then returns to dig in the same manner, using in this labor the canine teeth, and the claws in place of a hoe, and the membranes

(hh) Dr. Hernández says that when the Ocotochtli makes a kill, it covers it with foliage, and then, climbing a nearby tree, begins to utter howls as if inviting the other animals to eat of its prey; and that in fact the last to eat is the Ocotochtli itself; for such is the poison of its tongue that, if it ate first, the prey would be infected and the other animals would die in eating it. This fable is still heard from the mouth of the common people.

The Ahuitzotl is an amphibious quadruped, which for the most

part lives in the rivers of the hot lands. Its body is a foot long, its

muzzle long and pointed, and its tail large. Its skin is variegated with

the colors black and brown.

The Huitztlacuatzin is the porcupine, or spiny hog, of Mexico. It is

as large as a middling dog, which it resembles in the face, although its

muzzle is flattened; its feet and legs are quite thick, and its tail is pro‐

portioned to the size of the body. Its whole body, except the belly, the

hinder part of the tail, and the inner part of the legs, is armed with

hollow, sharp quills or spines, four finger-breadths long. On the muz‐

zle and the forehead it has long, erect bristles, which rise above the

head in the manner of a plume. Its whole skin, even between the

spines, is covered with soft black hair. It feeds only on the fruits of

the earth. (ii)

The Cacomiztle is a quadruped very much like the beech marten in

its manner of living. It has the size and form of a common cat; but its

body is thicker, its hair longer, its legs shorter, and its aspect wilder

and fiercer. Its voice is a shrill cry, and its food hens and other small

animals. It lives and rears its young in the less frequented parts of

houses. By day it sees little, and does not come out of its hiding place

except at night to procure its food. Both the Cacomiztle and the

Tlacuatzin are found even in the houses of the capital. (jj)

Besides these quadrupeds there were others in the Mexican empire of which I do not know whether they ought to be counted Bk. I. among the animals proper to that land or among those common to other American countries, such as the Itzcuincuani, that is, eater of dogs, the Tlalmiztli, a small lion, and the Tlalocelotl, a small tiger. Of the others, which, though they did not belong to the kingdom of Mexico, are found in other countries of North America subject to the Spaniards, we make mention in our dissertations.

The birds would give us more trouble than the quadrupeds if we § 11. were to undertake to enumerate their species and to describe their Birds of the kingdom

of Mexico. form and character. Their abundance and variety, and their excellence, have led some authors to say that Mexico is the kingdom of birds, as Africa is that of wild beasts. Dr. Hernández in his Natural History describes more than two hundred species peculiar to that country, and yet he leaves out several worthy of record, such as the Cuitlacochi, the Zacua, and the Madrugador. We shall content ourselves with running through a few classes, pointing out some particular feature where occasion calls for it. Among the birds of prey there are kestrels, goshawks, and several species of eagles, falcons, and sparrowhawks. To the birds of this class the aforesaid naturalist grants superiority over those of Europe. Because of the well-known excellence of the Mexican falcons, Philip II, king of Spain, ordered that a hundred of them be brought to his court every year. Among the eagles the largest, the handsomest, and the most renowned is the one the Mexicans call Itzquauhtli, which hunts not only the largest birds and hares, but attacks even men and wild beasts. Of the kestrels there are two species: the one called Cenotzqui is very handsome.

History of Mexico, Vol. I. L The Roman academicians who saw to the edition of Hernández; for it should be written Zacamiztli. Now this quadruped belongs to Pánuco, and the Cacomiztle to Mexico: the Zacamiztli lives in the countryside, and the Cacomiztle inside the houses of the cities. The Zacamiztli is a Castilian braccio in length, and the Cacomiztle is smaller.

p. 82

The ravens of Mexico, called Cacalotl by the Mexicans, are for the Bk. I. most part employed not in cleaning the fields of carrion, as else‐

where, but rather in stealing the grain from the ears. The task of

cleaning the fields is here chiefly reserved to the Zopilotes, known in

South America by the name of Gallinazos, by others by that of Auras,

and by some, finally, by the most improper one of ravens. (kk) There

are two species of these birds, very different from each other: that of

the true Zopilote, and that of the Cozcaquauhtli. Both are larger than

the raven. These two species agree in having a curved beak and claws,

and on the head, instead of feathers, a wrinkled membrane with a few

curly hairs. In flight they rise to such a height that, though so large,

they pass out of sight, and especially when a hailstorm is about to

come they are seen wheeling in great numbers beneath the high

clouds until they vanish in the distance. They feed on carrion, which

they see with their most keen eyes, or else sense with their very lively

sense of smell, from the greatest height, and then descend, forming

with majestic flight a great spiral line down to the carcass on which

they wish to feed. Both are almost mute. The two species are distin‐

guished in size, color, number, and some property. The true Zopilotes

have black feathers, and the head, beak, and feet brown; they often

go in troops, and spend the night together in the trees. (ll) Their

species is very numerous, and common to all climes. The species

of

It can be true only if it is understood of birds properly of prey, such as the eagles, vul‐

p. 83

of the Cozcaquauhtli, on the contrary, is not numerous, and is proper to the hot climes. It is, moreover, larger than the Zopilote; it has the Bk. I. head and feet red, and the beak white at the tip and blood-colored for the rest. Its feathers are brown, except those of the neck and of the parts near the breast, which are reddish black; its wings are ash-gray underneath, and above variegated with black and tawny.

The Cozcaquauhtli is called by the Mexicans King of the Zopilotes, (mm) and they say that when both species gather to eat a carcass, the Zopilote never touches it before the Cozcaquauhtli has tasted it. The Zopilotes are, besides, birds most useful to that kingdom; for not only do they clean the land, but they also pursue and destroy the eggs of the crocodiles in the very sand where the females of those formidable amphibians lay them so that they may be hatched by the sun: so that the killing of such birds ought to be forbidden under penalties.

Among the night birds there are eagle owls, owlets, scops owls, and others, to which the bats may be added, although properly they do not belong to the class of birds. Bats abound in the hot and shady lands, where there are some that with terrible bites draw much blood from horses and other animals. In several excessively hot countries very large bats are found, but not so large

(mm) That bird which now has in New Spain the name of King of the Zopilotes seems different from the one we describe. That modern King of the Zopilotes is as large as a common eagle, robust, and of a majestic air, with strong talons, lively and handsome eyes, and fine black, white, and tawny feathers: the most singular thing is that fleshy growth of scarlet color which surrounds the neck like a collar, and covers the head like a little crown. So it was described to me by a capable and trustworthy person, who says he has seen three individuals of that species, and in particular the one that in 1750 was sent from Mexico to the Catholic King Ferdinand VI. He says besides that the portrait of a bird published in the work entitled Il Gazzettiere americano is true to life. The Mexican name Cozcaquauhtli, which means eagle with a collar, really suits this one more than the other. Among the water birds we wish to count not only the web-footed

birds (palmipedes), which swim and commonly live in the water, but

also the long-shanked waders (himantopodes), and other fishers,

which live for the most part on the shores of the sea, the lakes, and

the rivers, and find their food in the water. In this order of birds there

is a prodigious number of geese, at least twenty species of ducks, sev‐

eral kinds of herons and egrets, very many swans, gulls, moorhens,

cormorants, halcyons, kingfishers, pelicans, and others. The multitude

of the ducks is so great that at times they cover the fields, and seen

from some distance they look like flocks of grazing sheep. Among the

egrets and herons there are gray ones, wholly white ones, and others

which, having the feathers of the body white, have the neck, the ex‐

tremities, the front part of the wings, and a part of the tail adorned

with a vivid scarlet color or a beautiful blue. The pelican, or onocro‐

talus, known to the Spaniards of Mexico by the name of Alcatraz, is

well known for that enormous pouch, or belly, as Pliny calls it, which

it has under the beak. There are two species of it in Mexico: one has

the beak smooth, and the other has it toothed. I do not know

whether, as this bird is known to Europeans, so is known its rare prop‐

erty of succoring the disabled individuals of its species, which some

Americans make use of to provide themselves with fish without labor.

They take a pelican alive, break one of its wings, and, tying it to a tree,

lie in ambush in a place nearby, where they wait for the arrival of the

other pelicans with their provision, and when they have seen them

throw the fish from the pouch, they run up at once, and, leaving the

prisoner a part of it, carry off the rest.

But if the pelican is worthy of admiration for its providence toward

others of its species, no less admirable is the Toalquachilli for the arms

with which the Creator has furnished it for its defense. This is a small

water bird with

a

p. 85

a long, slender neck, a small head, a long yellow beak, long legs, feet, and claws, and a short tail. The color of its legs and feet is ash-gray, Bk. I. and that of its body black, with some yellow feathers near the belly. On its head it has a little circlet or coronet of horny substance, divided into three very sharp points, and it has two more on the front part of the wings. (nn)

In the other classes of birds there are some valued for their flesh, others for their feathers, others for their song or their voice, and others finally for their instinct, or for some notable property of theirs which may interest our curiosity.

As to the birds whose flesh is wholesome and pleasant nourishment, I have counted more than seventy species. Besides the common hens carried from the Canary Islands to the Antilles, and thence to Mexico, there were and are hens proper to that country, which, because they are in part like the common hens and in part like peafowl, were called Gallipavos by the Spaniards, and Huexolotl and Totolin by the Mexicans. These birds, carried to Europe in return for the hens, have multiplied excessively, particularly in Italy, where, in view of their character and their size, they were given the name of turkeys; (*) but the multiplication of the European hens in Mexico has been far greater. There are also in great abundance wild turkeys, everywhere like the domestic ones, but larger, and in many countries of more savory flesh. There are partridges, quails, pheasants, cranes, turtledoves, doves, and very many others esteemed in Europe. The prodigious number of the quails may be known from what we shall say when we come to speak of the ancient sacrifices. The birds known there by the name of pheasants are of three species different from the

European
