Inhuman sacrifice of a Colhua maiden.

he history of the primitive peopling of Anahuac is so ob‐

T scured, and so enveloped in fables (as is that of the other

peoples of the world), that to find the truth is not merely difficult but altogether impossible. Certain it is nevertheless, as much from the venerable testimony of the holy books as from the constant and universal tradition of those peoples, that the first inhabitants of Anahuac descended from those few men whom Divine Providence snatched from the waters of the Flood to preserve the human species on earth. Nor can it be doubted that the nations that anciently peopled that land came to it from countries farther north in America, where their ancestors had settled many years and centuries earlier. On these two points I find all the Toltec, Chichimec, Acolhua, Mexican, and Tlaxcalan historians in agreement; but it is not known who the first inhabitants were, nor the time of their passage, nor the events of their journey and of their first settlements. Several historians, wishing to penetrate this chaos armed with feeble conjectures, vain combinations, and certain suspect paintings, have become lost

in

p. 125

in the thick darkness of antiquity, adopting as an expedient childish and baseless narratives. Bk. II.

There were authors who, relying on the tradition of the American peoples, and on the discovery of bones, skulls, and entire skeletons of immense size, which at different times and in several places of New § 1.

The Toltecs. Spain have been dug up, (a) believed that the first inhabitants of that land were giants. I for my part do not doubt their existence, in this country no less than in other countries of the New World; (b) but we cannot guess the time in which they lived, although we have reason to believe them very ancient, nor can we persuade ourselves that there was ever an entire nation of giants, as those authors imagined, but only extraordinary individuals of the known nations, or else of other, more ancient and wholly unknown ones. (c)

The

(a) The places where gigantic skeletons have been found are Atlancatepec, a village of the province of Tlaxcala, Texcoco, Toluca, Cuauhximalpan, and in our own days in California a hill not far from Kada-Kaaman.

(b) I know well that many philosophers of Europe, who laugh at the asserters of giants, will mock me too, or at least pity my too great credulity; but I must not betray the truth to avoid censure. I know that among the civilized peoples of America the tradition ran of the existence in those countries of certain men of immense height and corpulence, and I do not recall that among any American nation there is a memory either of elephants, or of hippopotami, or of other quadrupeds of such size. I know that human skulls and entire skeletons of surprising size have been found, on the deposition of innumerable authors, and especially of two eyewitnesses above all exception, Dr. Hernández and Father Acosta, who lacked neither learning, nor criticism, nor sincerity; and I do not know that so far, among so many excavations made in New Spain, the skeleton of a hippopotamus has ever been uncovered, or, what is more, even a tooth of an elephant. I know that some of those bones are found in tombs built expressly; and I do not know that tombs are built to bury hippopotami or elephants. All this, and still more, ought to be considered, before deciding, by those authors who affirmed without hesitation that all the bones discovered in America belonged to those, or to I know not what other immense animals.

(c) Several historians of Mexico say that the giants were treacherously killed by the Tlaxcalans; but this report, besides having no other support than certain poems of those same Tlaxcalans, does not agree with the chronology of the aforesaid historians, since they make the giants too ancient, and the Tlaxcalans too modern in the country of Anahuac.

p. 126

The nation of the Toltecs is the first of which some notices remain to us, Bk. II. though very scanty. These men, exiled, as they said, from their homeland

Huehuetlapallan, a place, as we conjecture, in the kingdom of Tollan, (d) from

which they took their name, situated to the northwest of New Mexico, began

their migration in the year I Tecpatl, that is, 596 of the common era. They

stayed in every place where they came, for as long a time as their fancy sug‐

gested, or the need of providing for the preservation of life. Where they

thought it advisable to make a longer stay, they built houses, and cultivated

the land, sowing maize, cotton, and other plants, the seeds of which they car‐

ried with them in order to procure what was necessary. In this fashion they

went wandering, always making their way toward the south, for the space of

104 years, until they arrived at the place to which they gave the name of

Tulancingo, more than 50 miles to the northeast of the site where, some cen‐

turies later, the famous city of Mexico was founded. Throughout their whole

journey they marched under the orders of certain captains, or lords, who were

seven in number when they arrived at Tulancingo. (e) In this country, al‐

though of mild climate and fertile soil, they would not settle, but when

scarcely twenty years had passed they withdrew forty miles to the west, along

the bank of a river, where they founded the city of Tollan, or Tula, after the

name of their homeland. This city, the most ancient, so far as is known, of the

land of Anahuac, and one of the most celebrated in the history of Mexico,

was the metropolis of the Toltec nation and the court of their kings. Their

monarchy began in the year VIII Acatl, that is, in 667 of the common era of

Christianity, and lasted 384 years. Here is the series of their kings, with the

Christian year in which they began to reign. (*)

Chalchiuhtlanetzin

It is no wonder that only eight monarchs reigned in little less than four centuries, since a strange law of that nation ordained that none of their kings should reign either more or less than one Toltec century, which (as we shall explain elsewhere) consisted of fifty-two years. If the king completed the century on the throne, he at once left the government, and another was put in his place; but if the king failed before the century was out, the nobility succeeded to the government, and governed the rest of the century in the name of the deceased king. This was seen to happen in the case of Queen Xiutzaltzin: she died in the fifth year of her reign, and the nobility governed for the forty-eight remaining years.

§ 2.

Most celebrated among all the nations of Anahuac were the Toltecs, Civilization of the both for their civilization and for their excellence in the arts; whence in Toltecs. later centuries the name of Toltecs was given as a mark of honor to the most distinguished artisans. They always lived in society, gathered in well-regulated cities under the dominion of sovereigns and the direction of laws. They were little warlike, and more devoted to the cultivation of the arts than to the exercise of arms. The later nations acknowledged themselves indebted to their agriculture for maize, cotton, chili pepper, and other most useful fruits. And they practiced not only the arts of primary necessity, but also those that serve luxury. They knew how to smelt gold and silver, and to give them every sort of figure in casting. They worked skillfully every species of gem, and this was precisely the art that made their name most famous; but nothing could make them more esteemed among us than their having been the inventors,

or

p. 128

or at least the reformers of the system of the ordering of time which all the Bk. II. civilized nations of Anahuac adopted; which presupposes, as we shall see later,

many observations and exact knowledge of astronomy.

The Chevalier Boturini (*), on the authority of the ancient histories of the

Toltecs, says that these people, observing in their ancient homeland

Huehuetlapallan the excess of almost six hours of the solar year over the civil

year that was in use among them, regulated it by the intercalary day that they

inserted every four years; which they did a hundred and more years before the

Christian era. He says further that in the year 660, Ixtlilcuechahuac reigning

in Tula, Huematzin, a celebrated astronomer, with the approval of the king

convoked the sages of the nation, and with them painted that famous book

which they called Teoamoxtli, that is, Divine Book, in which, with well-distin‐

guished figures, were set forth the origin of the Indians, their dispersion after

the confusion of tongues at Babel, their migration in Asia, their first settle‐

ments on the continent of America, the founding of the empire of Tula, and

their progress up to that time. The heavens, the planets, the constellations,

the Toltec calendar with its cycles, the mythological transformations (in

which they enclosed their moral philosophy), and the secrets of popular wis‐

dom wrapped up among the hieroglyphics of their gods, together with every‐

thing that belonged to religion and to customs, were described there. The

said Chevalier adds, moreover, that there was seen noted in their paintings

the solar eclipse that occurred at the death of our Redeemer, in the year VII

Tochtli, (f) and that when

some

(*) In the work composed by him and printed at Madrid in 1746 under this title: Idea of a

General History of New Spain, founded upon a great abundance of figures, symbols, characters, hieroglyph‐

ics, songs, and manuscripts of Indian authors newly discovered.

(f) All persons who have studied at the source the history of the nations of Anahuac know

very well that those peoples were accustomed to note in their paintings the eclipses, the

comets, and other celestial phenomena. Now, reading what Boturini says, I took the trouble to

compare the Toltec years with ours, and found the year 34 of Christ, that is, 30 of the com‐

mon era, to be VII Tochtli. I did this out of mere curiosity, and not because I pretend to con‐

firm, or because I believe, the anecdotes of that author.

p. 129

some learned Spaniards, versed in the history and paintings of the Toltecs, compared their chronology with ours, they found that that Bk. II. nation counted from the creation of the world to the time of the birth of Christ 5199 years, which is precisely the chronology of the Roman Calendar.

Whatever may be said of these curious anecdotes of the Chevalier Boturini, which I leave to the free judgment of prudent readers, it is certain and indubitable among all those who have studied the history of those nations that the Toltecs had a clear and distinct knowledge of the universal Flood, of the confusion of tongues, and of the dispersion of peoples; indeed, they named their first ancestors, who separated from the rest of the families in that universal dispersion. It is likewise certain, as we shall show elsewhere (though it may seem an incredible thing to the critics of Europe, accustomed to believe all Americans cut to one measure), that the Mexicans and all the other civilized nations of Anahuac had their civil year as well adjusted to the solar by means of intercalary days as the Romans had it after the ordinance of Julius Caesar, and that such exactness was owed to the enlightenment of the Toltecs. As regards religion, they were idolaters, and, so far as appears from history, were the inventors of the greater part of the Mexican mythology; but we do not know that they had in use those barbarous and bloody sacrifices which afterward became so frequent among the other nations. The Texcocan historians believed the Toltecs to be the authors of that most famous idol representing the god of water, placed on Mount Tlaloc, of which we shall speak later. It is certain indeed that they built, in honor of their beloved god Quetzalcoatl, the very lofty pyramid of Cholula, and probably also the famous ones of Teotihuacan, in honor of the Sun and the Moon, which, though disfigured, still subsist. (h) The painting alleged by the Chevalier Boturini represented the pyramid of

to set out for Mexico, where on a mountain made by hand (that is, the pyramid of

and lies: for neither were there ever kings in Cholula, nor could that baptism, of which

no writer speaks, have been celebrated on August 6, 1521, because at that time

Aguilar was with the other Spaniards in the greatest heat of the siege of the capital,

which seven days later was to surrender to the victors. Of the alleged apparition of the

Mother of God I find no memory anywhere among the Franciscan historians, who in

their Chronicles omitted nothing of what occurred in this kind. We have demonstrat‐

ed the falsity of that account in order to make those more cautious in accepting mod‐

ern paintings who in the future might undertake the History of Mexico.

p. 131

side into many large cities; but the stupendous calamities that befell them in the first years of the reign of Topiltzin overturned their pow‐ Bk. II. er and their happiness. For some years heaven denied them the rain necessary for their fields, and the earth the fruits on which they sustained themselves. The air, infected with mortal corruption, filled the land each day with corpses, and with consternation the minds of those who survived the ruin of their fellow countrymen. Thus a great part of the nation died of hunger and of contagion. Topiltzin also died in the year II Tecpatl, the twentieth of his reign, which was probably 1052 of the common era, and with him ended the monarchy of the Toltecs. The wretched remnants of the nation, thinking to escape the common calamity, sought in other countries a fitting remedy for their ills. Some made for Onohualco, or Yucatán, others toward Guatemala; meanwhile there remained in the kingdom of Tula various families scattered here and there in the great valley, where Mexico was afterward founded, in Cholula, in Tlaximaloyan, and in other places, and among them the two princes, sons of King Topiltzin, whose descendants in the course of time became related by marriage to the royal families of Mexico, of Texcoco, and of Colhuacan.

These scanty notices concerning the Toltecs are the only ones that we judge worthy of being related here, passing over various fabulous narratives of which several historians have made use. (i) We would wish to have their Divine Book, cited by Boturini and by Don Fernando de Alva Ixtlilxochitl in his highly prized manuscripts, to bring greater light to the history of this celebrated nation.

(i) Torquemada says that at a dance held by the Toltecs the wretched Devil appeared to them in gigantic form and with enormous arms, and in the midst of the dance, embracing them, strangled them; that he then let himself be seen in the form of a child with a rotting head, and brought them the pestilence; and finally that at the persuasion of the same Devil they abandoned the country of Tula. But that good author understood literally certain symbolic paintings, in which those people represented with such figures the famine and the pestilence that overtook them just when they found themselves at the height of prosperity.

§ 4. north of America, with good reason, equally with that of Europe, the The Chichimecs.

Their native country, whose situation we do not know, was called

The character of the Chichimecs was indeed singular, as is seen

from their history; for to a certain kind of civility they added many

traits of barbarism. They lived under the command of a sovereign,

and of chiefs and governors entrusted with the supreme authority,

with as much submission as is commonly seen among the most civi‐

lized nations. There was distinction between the commoners and the

nobility, and the commoners were accustomed to revere those whom

birth, merit, or the favor of the prince raised above their condition.

They lived gathered in places composed, as one must believe, of

wretched huts; (l) but they practiced neither agriculture nor those

Their religion was reduced to the simple worship of the Sun, to whose alleged divinity they offered grass, and the flowers they found growing in the fields. As for their customs, they were certainly less unpleasing, and less coarse, than the temper of a hunting people would lead one to expect.

The motive they had for abandoning their homeland is uncertain, § 5. as is also uncertain the etymology of the name Chichimecatl. (m) The Xolotl, first king of last king they had had in Amaquemecan left the government divided the Chichimecs in

Anahuac. between his two sons, Achcauhtli and Xolotl. The latter, either because he ill endured, as often happens, the division of his own authority, wished to try whether fortune destined other lands for him, where he might command without a rival; or because, seeing that the mountains of his kingdom were not sufficient to provide sustenance for a number of inhabitants perhaps grown too large, he determined to remedy the necessity by a timely departure. Having therefore taken such a resolution, for the one motive or the other, and having first had a good part of the southern lands reconnoitered by his scouts, he set out from his homeland with a large army of his subjects, who chose, whether from affection or from interest, to accompany him. down as they passed in review lasted down to his own times. For it is

neither likely that so great an army would set out on so long a journey,

nor does it seem possible that so small a district could have sustained

a million hunters. (n)

one of his captains, called Achitomatl, to go and reconnoiter the origin

of certain smokes that the prince had observed on his expedition.

and in other places several Toltec families, from whom he learned the cause and the time of their desolation. Not only did the Chichimecs Bk. II. refrain from molesting these wretched remnants of that celebrated nation, but they even contracted alliances with them, many nobles marrying Toltec women, and among others Prince Nopaltzin himself married Azcaxochitl, a maiden descended from Pochotl, one of those two princes of the royal house of the Toltecs who survived the ruin of their nation. This humane conduct helped the Chichimecs greatly; for through contact with that industrious nation they began to taste maize and other fruits of industry, they learned agriculture, the manner of extracting metals and the art of smelting them, as well as those of working stone and of spinning and weaving cotton, and others, by which they improved their sustenance, their clothing, their dwellings, and their customs.

Nor did the arrival of other civilized nations contribute less to the

It is to be believed that Xolotl sent word to his homeland of the advantages of the country where he had settled, and that such word, spread among the neighboring nations, urged many families to follow his footsteps, so as to share in his felicity. A few years later there arrived also three princes with a large army

of the Acolhua nation, natives of Teoacolhuacan, a country near, or not

very far from, the kingdom of Amaquemecan. These princes were

called Acolhuatzin, Chiconcuauhtli, and Tzontecomatl, and were of the

most noble house of Citin. The nation was the most civilized and pol‐

ished of all that were in that country after the Toltecs. One may well

understand how great must have been the stir that so great a novelty

made in that kingdom, and the disquiet into which so great a multi‐

tude of unknown people threw the Chichimecs; nor does it seem like‐

ly that the king would have allowed them to enter the country with‐

out first having informed himself of their condition and of the motive

of their coming. The king was then in Texcoco, whither he had

moved his court, either weary of the site of Tenayuca, or else attract‐

ed by the advantageous situation of that new place. Here the three

princes arrived, and, presented to the king, after a deep bow and that

ceremony of veneration so familiar to those peoples, of kissing the

hand after touching the earth with it, they said to him in substance

these words: “We have come, great King, from the kingdom of

Teoacolhuacan, not far from your homeland. We are all three broth‐

ers, and sons of a great lord; but aware of the felicity which the

Chichimecs enjoy under the rule of so humane a king, we have set

aside the comforts we had in our homeland for the glory of being

your subjects. We beg you therefore to give us a place in your happy

land, where we may live dependent on your authority and subject to

your commands.” The Chichimec was gratified, more than by the

lordly air and the courtly manners of those most noble youths, by the

flattering vanity of seeing humbled in his presence three princes at‐

tracted by such distant

countries

p. 137

countries by the fame of his power and of his clemency. He replied pleasantly to their expressions, and offered to comply with their Bk. II. wishes; but while he was deliberating on the manner of doing so, he ordered his son Nopaltzin to lodge them and to take care of them.

The king had two unmarried daughters, whom at first he thought to marry to the two elder princes; but he would not disclose his thought until he should have informed himself of their character and been certain of the will of his subjects. When he was then sure of the one and of the other, he called the princes to him, who were somewhat anxious about their lot, and made known to them his resolution not only to grant them establishments in his kingdom, but also to marry them to his daughters, complaining that he had not another, so that none should be excluded from the new alliance. The princes thanked him with singular expressions of gratitude, and offered to serve him with the greatest fidelity.

The day appointed for the wedding having arrived, so great a crowd of people flocked to Tenayuca, the place destined for that great ceremony, that the city not being sufficient to hold it, many people remained in the countryside. Acolhuatzin married the elder of the two princesses, called Cuetlaxochitl, and Chiconcuauhtli took the other. The third prince had Coatetl, a maiden born in Chalco of most noble parents, in whom Toltec blood had been mingled with Chichimec. The public rejoicings lasted as long as sixty days, in which there was wrestling, racing, and combats with wild beasts, exercises suited to the genius of the Chichimecs, and in all of them Prince Nopaltzin distinguished himself. Following the example of the royal persons, those two nations went on knitting themselves together with marriages, until of both one was made, which, taking its name from the nobler part, was called Acolhua, and the kingdom Acolhuacan, the name of Chichimecs remaining then for those men who, prizing the exercise of the chase more than the labors of agriculture, or else impatient of subordination, went off to the mountains that are toward the north

History of Mexico, Vol. I. S and

p. 138

and toward the northwest of the Mexican valley, where, giving them‐

Bk. II. selves up to the sway of their barbarous liberty, without a chief, with‐

These barbarians, mingled with the nation of the Otomis, who fol‐

three hundred miles, and by their descendants the Spaniards were for

many years greatly harassed after the conquest of Mexico.

When the rejoicings of the wedding were over, Xolotl divided his

§ 7. kingdom into several states, and gave the investiture of them to his

Division of the states, and rebellions. sons-in-law and to other nobles of the one nation and of the other.

He granted to Prince Acolhuatzin the state of Azcapotzalco, eighteen

miles west of Texcoco, and from him descended the kings under

whose yoke the Mexicans remained for more than fifty years. To

Chiconcuauhtli he conferred the state of Xaltocan; and to

Tzontecomatl that of Coatlichan.

The population increased every day, and with it the civilization of

the peoples; but there were awakened together in their minds ambi‐

tion and other passions, which lie asleep for want of ideas in the time

of savage life. Xolotl, who during the greater part of his government

had ruled his subjects with gentleness, and had found in them the

greatest docility, was then seen, in the last years of his life, compelled

to use severity to repress the restlessness of some rebels, now strip‐

ping them of their offices, now punishing the most guilty with death.

These just punishments, instead of frightening them, so exasperated

them that they formed the detestable design of taking the king’s life,

nor did a ready occasion for carrying it out fail them. The king had

shortly before made known his wish to increase the water of his gar‐

dens, where he was accustomed to divert himself, and often also went

to sleep there, weighed down by years, or enticed by the freshness

and the pleasantness of the place. Aware of this, the rebels, having

made a dam in the stream that crossed the city, opened a trench

to

p. 139

to conduct the water to the gardens; but to do so they waited for the time when the king was accustomed to sleep there, and then, the dam Bk. II. having been removed on the side of the trench, they sent all the water at once into the gardens, so that he should be drowned. They flattered themselves that their crime would never be discovered, since the misfortune of the king might be ascribed to some accident, or to measures ill taken by subjects who sincerely desired to serve their lord; but they deceived themselves, and their blow did not succeed; for the king had secret warning of the conspiracy, and, dissembling that he knew of it, he went at the accustomed hour to the garden and lay down to sleep in an elevated spot, where he would not be in danger. When he then saw the water enter, although the treachery was already evident, he continued his dissimulation in order to mock his enemies: “I,” he then said, “was well persuaded that my subjects loved me; but now I see that they love me far more than I thought. I wanted to increase the water of my gardens, and behold, my subjects have brought it to me without any expense. We must therefore make a feast of my good fortune.” Then he ordered rejoicings to be held in the court, and when they were over he departed for Tenayuca, full of grief and indignation, and determined to give a rigorous punishment to the conspirators; but there he was seized by a mortal illness, which calmed his anger.

Now, feeling the nearness of death, he called to him Prince § 8.

Death and funeral of Nopaltzin, his daughters, and Acolhuatzin his son-in-law (since the Xolotl. other two princes were already dead), and to them he commended peace among themselves, the care of the peoples entrusted to them, the protection of the nobility, and kindness toward all their subjects; and then, a few hours later, amid the tears and sobs of his children, he ceased to live, at a very advanced age, and after having reigned in that country, as it seems, more than forty years. He was a robust and courageous man, but of a most tender heart toward his children, and very kind toward his subjects. His reign would have been happier had it been shorter. (o)

The news of the king’s death spread at once throughout the king‐

Bk. II. dom, and prompt notice was given to the principal lords, so that they

might be present at the funeral. They adorned the royal corpse with

several small figures of gold and silver, which the Chichimecs, in‐

structed by the Toltecs, had already begun to make, and placed it on a

seat made of copal gum and other aromatic materials; and so it re‐

mained five days, while the lords summoned to the obsequies were ar‐

riving. When all had assembled amid an infinite throng of people, the

corpse was burned, according to the custom of the Chichimecs, and

the ashes gathered in an urn of very hard stone. This urn was kept on

display for forty days in a hall of the royal house, where every day the

nobility came to render to their dead lord the homage of tears, and

afterward the urn was carried to a cave near the city with the same

demonstrations of grief.

king of the

Chichimecs. joicings for another forty days. As the lords were then taking leave of

the eyes of a true father, and to protect us with your power, that we

may be secure beneath your shadow. You are at once a restoring water

and a devouring fire, and in your hands you hold equally both our

death and our life.”

The lords having taken their leave, the king remained in Tenayuca

with his sister Cihuaxochitl, widow of Prince Chiconcuauhtli. He was

then, as far as we can conjecture, about sixty years of age, and already

had children and grandchildren. His

legitimate

p. 141

legitimate sons by the Toltec queen were Tlotzin, Cuauhtequihua, and

Bk. II. Apopozoc. To Tlotzin, who was the first-born, he conferred the government of Texcoco, that he might go on learning the difficult art of ruling men, and to the other two he gave the investiture of the states of Zacatlán and of Tenamitic. (p)

For a year the king remained at the court of Tenayuca, setting in order the affairs of the state, which was no longer so tranquil as at the beginning. Thence he went to Texcoco to treat with his son about the most suitable means to be taken to restore the kingdom to its former tranquillity. Being there, he once entered the royal gardens with his son and with other lords of his court, and while he was conversing with them, he burst unexpectedly into violent weeping, and being asked the cause: “Two,” he replied, “are the causes of my tears: one is the memory of my dead father, which is revived in me by the sight of this place, where he used to divert himself; and the other is the comparison I make of those happy times with these bitter days. When my father planted these gardens, he had subjects more peaceable, who served him with sincerity in those offices that he conferred on them, and they accepted them with humility and with gratitude; but today ambition and discord reign everywhere. It grieves me to be compelled to treat as enemies those subjects whom once I treated in this very place as friends and brothers. You, my son,” he added, speaking to Tlotzin, “keep ever before your eyes the image of your mighty grandfather, and strive to imitate the examples of prudence and of justice that he left us. Fortify your heart with all that you will afterward need for ruling

your

See on this point our Dissertation II.

your subjects well.” When he had consoled his son for a while, the Bk. II. king went back to his court of Tenayuca.

Prince Acolhuatzin, who was still living, thinking the limits of his

state of Azcapotzalco too narrow, resolved to take possession of that

of Tepotzotlán, and in fact took it by force, in spite of the resistance

made by Chalchiuhcua, lord of that state. It is to be believed that

Acolhuatzin would not have undertaken such violence without the

express consent of the king, who perhaps wished in that way to

avenge some offense received from Chalchiuhcua.

Somewhat more bloody was the contest which broke out a little

later over an interest of a very different nature. Huetzin, lord of

Coatlichan, son of the late Prince Tzontecomatl, (q) wished to marry

Atotoztli, a beautiful and noble maiden, and a niece of the queen. The

same pretension was held by Yacazozolotl, lord of Tepetlaoztoc; but

he, either because he was more in love with the maiden, or because

he was of a more violent character, not content with asking for her

from her father, wished to make himself master of her beauty by

arms, and to this end gathered a small army of his subjects, to whom

was joined Tochintecuhtli, who had been lord of Cuahuacan, and for his

misdeeds had been stripped of it and confined to Tepetlaoztoc. Aware

of this attempt, Huetzin came to meet him with a larger number of

troops, and offered him battle in the neighborhood of Texcoco, in

which some of Yacazozolotl’s people perished along with him, and the

rest of the army was routed. Tochintecuhtli saved himself by flight,

taking refuge in the city of Huexotzinco beyond the mountains.

p. 143

Huetzin, freed from his rival, took possession, with the consent of the king, of the maiden and of the state of Tepetlaoztoc. Bk. II.

After these little wars among the feudatories, another, more considerable, was set in motion by the crown against the province of Tulancingo, which had rebelled. The king himself went there in person with a large army; but as the rebels were in great number and well trained in arms, the royal army at times had the worse of it in the nineteen days that the war lasted, until, reinforced with new troops sent by Prince Tlotzin, the rebels were routed, and the leaders of the rebellion punished with the extreme penalty. Their bad example, imitated by other lords, met likewise with the same fate.

Nopaltzin had already restored tranquillity to the kingdom, when the celebrated Prince Acolhuatzin, first lord of Azcapotzalco, died, leaving the state to his son Tezozomoc. His funeral was celebrated with great magnificence, the king attending with the nobility of both nations, Acolhua and Chichimec.

It was not long before the king himself also died, after thirty-two § 10. years of reign, having previously declared as his successor to the Tlotzin, third king of crown his first-born, Tlotzin. The obsequies were held in the same the Chichimecs. court, and with the same apparatus and ceremonies as those of King Xolotl, to whom he was like no less in character than in robustness and courage.

Among the other lords who attended the exaltation of the new king were his two brothers Cuauhtequihua and Apopozoc, whom he kept for a year in his royal house. Tlotzin was of so benign and loving a temper that he was the whole delight of his vassals. All the nobles sought pretexts to visit him and to enjoy the pleasantness and sweetness of his manner. Notwithstanding this disposition so inclined to peace, he took great care of the affairs of war, causing his subjects to exercise often in arms, and he himself delighted in the chase; but we know nothing in particular of his actions, nor of the events of his reign in the thirty-six years that he occupied the throne of Acolhuacan. He died

racked

p. 144

racked by most grievous pains in Tenayuca. His ashes were deposited

Bk. II.

in an urn of precious stone, which stood for forty days exposed to the

view of the people under a canopy.

§ 11. There succeeded Tlotzin in the kingdom his son Quinatzin, (*) Quinatzin, fourth

king of the born of Cuauhcihuatzin, daughter of the lord of Huexotla. His exalta‐

Chichimecs. tion was celebrated with greater solemnity than that of his predeces‐

sors, not in Tenayuca, but in Texcoco, where he established his court,

and from then until the conquest by the Spaniards that city was al‐

ways the capital of the kingdom of Acolhuacan. To pass from the old

to the new court he had himself carried in an open litter on the

shoulders of four principal lords, and under an umbrella, which four

others bore. Until that time all the lords had always walked on foot.

This king was the first to whom vanity suggested such a kind of mag‐

nificence, and this example was imitated by his successors, and by all

the kings and magnates of that country, each striving to surpass the

others in pomp: an emulation too pernicious no less to the states

than to the princes themselves.

The beginnings of his government were quite tranquil; but a little

later the states of Metztitlan and of Totoltepec, situated in the moun‐

tains which lie to the north of that capital, rebelled. As soon as the

king had notice of it, he marched with a large army, and sent word to

the leaders of the rebellion that if their courage was equal to their

perfidy, they should descend within two days to the plain of

Tlaximalco, where in a battle their fate would be decided; if not, he

was determined to put their cities to fire and flame, sparing neither

women nor children. The rebels, as they were already well prepared,

came down before the appointed time to that plain to show off their

courage. The signal for battle having been given there, they fought fu‐

riously and obstinately on both sides, until night separated the

armies

(*) This king also had the name of Tlaltecatzin.

p. 145

armies, leaving the victory undecided. Thus they continued for forty days in frequent fights, the rebels never losing heart in spite of the Bk. II. advantages which the royal troops won every day; but perceiving at last, from the slaughter and the diminution of their soldiers, their imminent ruin, they surrendered to their sovereign, who, punishing the leaders of the rebellion severely, pardoned the peoples their crime. He did the same with the state of Tepepulco, which had also rebelled.

This spirit of rebellion went spreading through the whole kingdom like a contagion: for scarcely was Tepepulco subdued when Huehuetoca, Mixquic, Totolapan, and four other cities declared themselves in rebellion. The king wished to go in person with a good body of troops against Totolapan, and sent against the other six cities as many bodies under the command of brave and faithful generals: and so great was his good fortune that in a very short time, and without considerable loss, he brought all seven cities back under his obedience. These victories were celebrated with great rejoicings for eight days at the court, and the generals and the soldiers who had most distinguished themselves were rewarded. Just as the bad example of some states had driven others to rebellion, so the unhappy outcome served them as a lesson for the future not to plot any more novelties against the due subordination to their sovereign: whence in the rest of his government, which, according to what the historians say, lasted sixty years, Quinatzin enjoyed great tranquillity.

When this king came to die, there were made toward him certain demonstrations that had never been made toward his predecessors; for his body having been opened, and the viscera removed, they prepared it with I know not what composition of aromatics, so that it might be preserved for some time from corruption. They then placed him in a great seat, clad in the royal garments, and armed with bow and arrows, and put at his feet a wooden eagle, and behind him a tiger, to signify his intrepidity and bravery. In this posture they kept him on public display for forty days, and after the customary weeping they burned him

History of Mexico, Vol. I. T and

p. 146

and buried the ashes in a cave of the mountains near Texcoco. Bk. II.

There succeeded Quinatzin on the throne his son Techotlalatzin; but as the

events of this king and of the following Chichimec kings are connected with

those of the Mexicans, who had already at this time (in the fourteenth centu‐

ry of the common era) founded their famous capital, we reserve the account

of such events for another place, contenting ourselves now with presenting

to the readers the series of all their kings, indicating, as far as is known, the

year of the common era in which they began their reign, in order then to dis‐

course briefly of the other nations that arrived in that country before the

Mexicans.

We cannot state the year in which the first five kings began to reign, because

we do not know how long Xolotl and Techotlalatzin reigned; we judge it,

however, probable that the Chichimec monarchy had its beginning in

Anahuac toward the end of the twelfth century, and lasted about 330 years,

until 1521, in which it ended together with the kingdom of Mexico.

Eleven

p. 147

Eleven legitimate kings at least occupied the throne, and two Tyrants. (*)

Bk. II.

The Acolhuas arrived in the country of Anahuac after the thirteenth century had begun. As for the other nations, the diversity of opinions, and the confusion of the historians, about their origin, their number, and the time at which they came into Anahuac, is incredible. The very great study that I have made to trace out the truth has served me for nothing else than to increase my uncertainty and to make me lose altogether the hope of knowing in the future what up to now has been unknown. Passing over, then, the fables, we shall say only what is certain, or at least very probable.

The Olmecs and the Xicalancas, whether they were a single nation, or two § 12. different ones, but perpetually allied and joined, were so ancient in the coun‐ The Olmecs and the

Otomis. try of Anahuac that several authors believed them earlier than the Toltecs. Nothing is known of their origin (r), nor do the ancient paintings of those peoples tell us anything else, except that they inhabited the country surrounding the great mountain Matlalcueye, and that, driven out from there by the Teochichimecs, or Tlaxcalans, they moved to the coasts of the Gulf of Mexico. (s)

The Otomis, who composed one of the most numerous nations, were probably among the most ancient in

that country; but they remained for many centuries in barbarism, liv‐

Bk. II.

ing scattered in the caves of the mountains, and sustaining them‐

selves by the chase, in which they were most skillful. They occupied a

tract of land of more than three hundred miles from the mountains

of Ixmiquilpan toward the northwest, bordering on the east and on

the west on other nations likewise savage. In the fifteenth century

they began, as we shall say elsewhere, to live in society, subject to the

crown of Acolhuacan, either compelled by force or else stimulated by

the example of the other nations. They founded in the country of

Anahuac, and even in the very valley of Mexico, countless places: the

greater part of them, and especially the largest, such as those of

Jilotepec and of Huitzapan, in the neighborhood of the country

which they had occupied before; others scattered among the

Matlatzincas and the Tlaxcalans, and in other provinces of the king‐

dom, preserving down to our times without alteration their primitive

language even in the isolated colonies, surrounded everywhere by

other nations. It is not, however, to be thought that the whole nation

was then reduced to civil life, since a great part, and perhaps the

greatest, remained still together with the Chichimecs in savage life.

The barbarians of both nations, confounded by the Spaniards under

the name of Chichimecs, made themselves famous by their raids, and

were not wholly subjugated by the Spaniards until the seventeenth

century. The Otomis have always been reputed the coarsest nation of

Anahuac, as much because of the difficulty that all find in under‐

standing their language as because of their servile life; since even in

the time of the Mexican kings they were treated like slaves. Their lan‐

guage is very difficult, and full of aspirations, which are formed partly

in the throat and partly in the nose; but otherwise it is sufficiently co‐

pious and expressive. Anciently they were renowned for their skill in

the chase; today they trade for the most part in coarse cloths, with

which they clothe the other Indians.

§ 13. The nation of the Tarascans occupied the vast, rich, and pleasant The Tarascans. country of Michoacán, where they multiplied greatly, and founded

many cities and countless villages. Their kings were rivals

of

p. 149

of the Mexicans, and had frequent wars with them. Their artisans either surpassed or equaled those of the other nations: at least after the Bk. II. conquest of Mexico the best works of mosaic were made in Michoacán, and there alone down to our times has this so precious art been preserved. The Tarascans were idolaters, but not so cruel in their worship as the Mexicans. Their language is abundant, sweet, and sonorous. They often use the soft R: their syllables consist for the most part of a single consonant and a vowel. Besides the natural advantages of their country, the Tarascans had the good fortune to have as first bishop Don Vasco de Quiroga, one of the most distinguished prelates that Spain has produced, truly worthy of being compared with the ancient Fathers of Christianity; whose memory has been preserved alive down to our times, and will be preserved eternally among those peoples. The country of Michoacán, which is among the most valuable of the New World, was annexed to the crown of Spain by the free and spontaneous cession of its legitimate sovereign, without costing the Spaniards even a drop of blood, although it is to be believed that the fear instilled in him by the recent ruin of the Mexican empire impelled that monarch to such a cession. (t) The Chevalier Boturini says that the Mexicans, finding themselves besieged by the Spaniards, sent an embassy to the king of Michoacán to procure his alliance; that he assembled a hundred thousand Tarascans, and as many Teochichimecs, in the province of Ávalos, but, frightened by a certain vision that he had of a sister of his, already dead and returned to life, he dismissed the army and abandoned the enterprise of succoring the Mexicans, as he had intended. The principal places inhabited by them were on the western moun‐

tains of the Valley of Mexico, and made up the province of

Mazahuacan, belonging to the crown of Tacuba.

The Matlatzincas formed a considerable state in the fertile Valley

of Toluca; and however great their reputation for bravery had an‐

ciently been, they were nonetheless subjected by King Axayacatl to

the crown of Mexico, as we shall relate elsewhere.

The Mixtecs and the Zapotecs peopled the vast countries that

bear their names, to the southeast of Texcoco. The many states into

which these two countries were divided remained for a long time un‐

der several lords or petty kings of the same nations, until they were

conquered by the Mexicans. Those nations were nonetheless civilized

and industrious: they had their laws, they practiced the arts of the

Mexicans, and they used the same method of reckoning time and the

same paintings to perpetuate the memory of events, in which they

represented the creation of the world, the universal Flood, and the

confusion of tongues, though all mixed with a good many fables. (v)

After the Conquest the Mixtecs and the Zapotecs have been among

the most industrious peoples of New Spain. As long as the silk trade

lasted, they were the rearers of the worms, and to their labors is owed

all the cochineal that for many years now has been carried from

Mexico to Europe.

The Chiapanecs were, if we are to credit their traditions, the first

settlers of the New World. They said that Votan, grandson of that

venerable old man who built the great ark to save himself and his

family from the Flood, and one of those who undertook the building

of the lofty edifice that was raised to climb up to Heaven, went by ex‐

press command

of

(v) On the mythology of the Mixtecs see the work of Brother Gregorio García,

Dominican, entitled Origen de los indios, in bk. 5, ch. 4.

p. 151

of the Lord to people that land. They also said that the first settlers

Bk. II. had come from the north, and that when they arrived at Soconusco they separated, some going to live in the country of Nicaragua, and the others remaining in that of Chiapas. This nation, according to what the historians say, was not governed by a king but by two military chiefs elected by the priests. So they continued until they were subjected to that crown by the last Mexican kings. They made the same use of paintings as the Mexicans and had the same way of reckoning time; but the figures with which they represented the years, the months, and the days were altogether different.

As for the Cohuixcas, the Cuitlatecs, the Yopis, the Mazatecs, the Popolocas, the Chinantecs, and the Totonacs, we know nothing of their origin or of the time when they arrived in Anahuac. Of their particular customs we shall say something, wherever it may serve the history of the Mexicans.

But among all the nations that peopled the country of Anahuac, § 15. the most renowned, and those that figure most in the history of The Nahuatlacas. Mexico, are those that were commonly called Nahuatlacas. This name, whose etymology we set out at the beginning of this History, was given principally to those seven nations, or rather to those seven tribes of one and the same nation, which arrived in that country after the Chichimecs, and peopled the islets, the shores, and the neighborhood of the Mexican lakes. These tribes were those of the Xochimilcas, the Chalcas, the Tepanecs, the Colhuas, the Tlahuicas, the Tlaxcalans, and the Mexicans. The origin of all these tribes was the province of Aztlan, whence the Mexicans came out, or else another one contiguous to it and peopled by the same nation. All the historians represent them as originating in one and the same country: all spoke the same language. The various names by which they are known were taken from the places they founded or those in which they settled.

The

p. 152

The Xochimilcas took their name from the great city of Bk. II. Xochimilco, which they founded on the southern shore of the freshwa‐

ter lake, that is, of Chalco. The Chalcas took theirs from the city of

Chalco, on the eastern shore of the same lake, the Colhuas from

Colhuacan, the Mexicans from Mexico, the Tlaxcalans from Tlaxcala,

and the Tlahuicas from the land where they settled, which, being

abundant in cinnabar, was called Tlahuican. (u) The Tepanecs may per‐

haps have taken their name from some place called Tepan (x), where

they will have been before founding their celebrated city of

Azcapotzalco.

It is beyond doubt that these tribes did not all arrive in that coun‐

try together, but at different times and in the order we have indicat‐

ed; but there is a great variety of opinion among the historians about

the precise time at which they came into Anahuac. We are persuaded,

for the reasons set out in our dissertations, that the first six tribes ar‐

rived led by those six lords who appeared in Anahuac immediately af‐

ter the Chichimecs, and that there was not so great an interval of

time, as Father Acosta believes, between their arrival and that of the

Mexicans.

The Colhuas, mostly confused by the Spanish historians with the

Acolhuas on account of the similarity of the names, founded the

small monarchy of Colhuacan, which was afterward joined to the

crown of Mexico by the marriage of a princess, heir of that state, to a

Mexican king.

The Tepanecs likewise had their petty kings, of whom the first was

Prince Acolhuatzin, after he had married the daughter of Xolotl. His

descendants usurped, as we shall relate, the kingdom of Acolhuacan,

and ruled all

The Tlaxcalans, called Teochichimecs by Torquemada and other au‐ § 16. thors and considered as a tribe of the Chichimec nation, (y) settled at The Tlaxcalans. first in Poyauhtlan, a place situated on the eastern shore of the Lake of Texcoco, between this capital and the village of Chimalhuacán. There they lived for some time in great misery, sustaining themselves only on game for want of arable land; but having multiplied, and wishing to enlarge the bounds of their territory, they drew upon themselves the resentment of the neighboring nations.

History of Mexico, Vol. I. V The

(y) Torquemada not only says that the Tlaxcalans were Teochichimecs, but even affirms in bk. 3, ch. 10, that these Teochichimecs were Otomis. If the Tlaxcalans were Otomis, why did they not speak the Otomi language? And if they ever spoke it, why did they abandon it for the Mexican? Where has a free nation ever been seen to abandon its native speech in order to adopt that of its enemies? It is no less incredible that the Chichimecs were Otomis, as the aforesaid author supposes here, although in bk. 1, ch. 11, he affirms the opposite. Who compelled the Chichimecs to abandon their primitive speech? Only one who is unacquainted with the character of those nations, and does not know how constant they are in retaining their national tongue, could be persuaded that the Chichimecs, through intercourse and alliance with the Acolhuas, would have left Otomi for Mexican. If the true Otomis have not altered their idiom in so many centuries, neither under the domination of the Mexicans nor under that of the Spaniards, how can it be believed that the Chichimecs entirely changed their language, being masters of that country, and always occupying the throne of Acolhuacan from Xolotl, founder of that kingdom, down to the conquest of the Spaniards? I, however, do not doubt that the proper language of the ancient Chichimecs was the same as that of the Acolhuas and Nahuatlacas, that is, the Mexican. The same seems to me true of the Toltecs, whatever other authors may say, nor can I be persuaded of the contrary after the most diligent study of history. We know, too, that the names of the places whence the Toltecs and the Chichimecs came out, and of those which they founded in Anahuac, of the persons of both nations, and of the years they used, were Mexican. We know that the Toltecs and the Chichimecs, and the Chichimecs and the Acolhuas, from the very beginning communicated with one another and understood each other reciprocally without an interpreter. The finding of the Mexican language spread as far as Nicaragua cannot be ascribed to anything but the dispersion of the Toltecs, who spoke it, since it is not known that the Nahuatlacas advanced beyond Chiapas. Finally, we do not find even an argument to confirm the opposite opinion, although common among our historians.

p. 154

The Xochimilcas, the Colhuas, the Tepanecs, and in all likelihood the Bk. II. Chalcas too, who, being their neighbors, were likewise the most

harmed, formed a confederacy and raised a considerable army to

drive such pernicious settlers out of the Valley of Mexico. The

Tlaxcalans, whom the consciousness of their usurpations kept ever on

the watch, came out well drawn up to meet them. The battle was one

of the bloodiest and most memorable that are read in the history of

Mexico. The Tlaxcalans, though inferior in number, made such

slaughter of their enemies that they left the field full of corpses and a

part of the lake, on whose shore the fight took place, stained with

blood. Although they came out of this battle so glorious, they never‐

theless resolved to abandon that site, well persuaded that as long as

they were there they would be forever harassed by their neighbors;

and therefore, after having surveyed the whole country by means of

their scouts, and not having found a place where they could all settle

together, they agreed to separate, one part of them going toward the

south and the other toward the north. The latter, after a short jour‐

ney, settled, with the permission of the Chichimec king, in

Tulancingo and in Huauchinango. The others, going around the great

volcano Popocatépetl by way of Tetela and Tochimilco, founded in

the neighborhood of Atlixco the city of Cuauhquechollan, and some,

passing farther on, founded Amaliuhcan and other villages, and so ex‐

tended as far as the Poyauhtecatl, or Mount Orizaba, to which they

probably gave that name in memory of the place in the Valley of

Mexico that they had left.

But the greater and more considerable part of the tribe made its

way by Cholula to the foot of the great mountain Matlalcueye,

whence it drove out the Olmecs and the Xicalancas, ancient inhabi‐

tants of that country, and put to death their king Colopechtli. There

they settled under a chief called Colhuacatecuhtli, endeavoring to forti‐

fy themselves so as to be better able to resist the neighboring peo‐

ples, should they ever attack them. In fact it was not long before the

Huexotzincas and other peoples, aware of the bravery and the

strength of their new neighbors, fearing that in the future

they

p. 155

they might prove harmful to them, raised a large army to drive them Bk. II. out altogether from the whole country. The blow was so violent that the Tlaxcalans were forced to abandon the place and retire to the summit of that great mountain. Finding themselves there in the greatest consternation, they implored through their ambassadors the protection of the Chichimec king, and obtained from him a large body of troops. The Huexotzincas, not having forces sufficient to withstand the royal army, called the Tepanecs to their aid, believing that they would not refuse so fine an occasion for revenge; but these, remembering the tragic event of Poyauhtlan, although they sent troops, gave these too the order to do no harm to the Tlaxcalans, and the Tlaxcalans themselves were warned by them, lest they should take them for enemies, and be assured that those people were sent only to deceive the Huexotzincas and so as not to disturb the harmony that existed between them and the Tepanecs. With the help of the Texcocans, and through the perfidious laziness of the Tepanecs, the Huexotzincas were defeated and forced to return in ignominy to their own state. The Tlaxcalans, delivered from so grave a danger, and peace made with their neighbors, returned to their first settlement to continue the peopling already begun.

This was the origin of the famous city and republic of Tlaxcala, eternal rival of the Mexicans and the cause of their ruin. At the beginning they all obeyed one chief; but their population having afterward considerably increased, the city was divided into four quarters called Tepeticpac, Ocotelolco, Quiahuiztlan, and Tizatlan. Each quarter was under its own lord, to whom were likewise subject all the places dependent on that quarter; so that the whole state was composed of four petty monarchies; but these four lords together with other nobles of the first rank formed a kind of aristocracy with respect to the common concerns of the state. This diet or senate was the arbiter of war and of peace; it fell to it to prescribe the number of troops that were to be armed, and the general who was to command them.

V2 In

p. 156

In the state, though small, there were many cities and large villages,

Bk. II.

in which in 1520 were counted more than a hundred and fifty thou‐

sand houses and more than five hundred thousand inhabitants. The

district of the republic was fortified on the west side with ditches and

trenches, and on the east side with a wall six miles long; on the south

it was naturally defended by Matlalcueye, and on the north by other

mountains.

The Tlaxcalans were warlike, courageous, and very jealous of their

honor and their liberty. They preserved the splendor of their republic

for a long time, despite the opposition they had to suffer from their

enemies, until, having allied themselves with the Spaniards against

the Mexicans, their ancient rivals, they were involved in the common

ruin. They were idolaters, and as superstitious and cruel in their wor‐

ship as the Mexicans. Their favorite god was Camaxtle, the very one

revered by the Mexicans under the name of Huitzilopochtli. Their arts

were those of the other neighboring nations. Their commerce was

principally in maize and in cochineal. From the abundance of maize

the capital was given the name of Tlaxcallan, that is, place of bread.

Their cochineal was valued above all other, and after the Conquest

brought the capital each year an income of two hundred thousand

scudi; but they abandoned this trade altogether for the reasons indi‐

cated elsewhere.

§ 17. The Aztecs, or Mexicans, who were the last settlers of the country

Journey of the

The

(z) In our dissertations we speak of these journeys made from New Mexico toward

the northwest. Vetancurt makes mention of them in pt. 2, treatise 1, ch. 10 of his

Teatro mexicano. This author makes Aztlan distant 2700

p. 157

The reason for abandoning their homeland will have been the very

Bk. II. same that other nations had. But whatever it was, it will not be altogether useless to set before the free judgment of readers what the Mexican historians themselves relate of the origin of this resolution.

There was, they say, among the Aztecs a personage of great authority called Huitziton, to whose opinion all deferred in great measure. He had taken it upon himself, I know not for what motive, to persuade his countrymen to a change of country; and while he was turning this thought over, he happened to hear a little bird singing on the branches of a tree, whose voice imitated the Mexican word Tihui, which means, Let us go. This seemed to him a fine occasion to obtain what he wanted from his countrymen. Calling therefore another notable person, named Tecpaltzin, he led him to that tree, where the little bird was wont to sing, and said to him thus: “Do you not perceive, friend Tecpaltzin, what this little bird is telling us? That Tihui, Tihui, which it ever repeats to us, what does it mean, if not that we must leave this country and find another? This without doubt is a warning from some hidden deity who watches over our good. Let us obey his voice, then, and not draw his displeasure upon ourselves by our refusal.” Tecpaltzin fully assented to Huitziton’s interpretation, either from the opinion he had of his wisdom, or because he too had been struck by the same thought. These two authoritative personages being now in agreement, it was not long before they drew the body of the nation to their side.

Although I do not trust such a narration, it does not seem to me otherwise altogether improbable; for it is not hard for

much harder for me to be persuaded of what the Spanish authors

commonly say, namely, that the Mexicans undertook that journey by

express command of the Devil. The good historians of the sixteenth

century, and those who have copied them, assume as altogether be‐

yond doubt the continual and familiar intercourse of the Devil with

all the idolatrous nations of the New World, and scarcely relate any

event of history of which they do not make him the author. But al‐

though it is certain that the malice of those spirits strives to do men

all the harm it can, and at times they have shown themselves to them

in visible form in order to seduce them, especially those who have not

entered by regeneration into the bosom of the Church; nevertheless

it cannot be believed that such apparitions were so frequent, nor

their intercourse with the aforesaid nations so free, as these histori‐

ans believe; for God, who watches with loving providence over his

creatures, does not grant to such enemies of mankind so much liberty

to do harm. Readers ought not, however, to wonder if several events

of this History which they have read in other authors they find in this

one little in conformity with their credulity. I, in truth, am not dis‐

posed to ascribe any effect to the Devil on the sole testimony of some

Mexican historians, since they could easily fall into error, whether

through the superstitious ideas by which their minds were clouded,

or through the trickery of the priests, too common among idolatrous

nations.

Finally, the journey of the Aztecs, which is certain, whatever the

motive for undertaking it, was undertaken by them about the year

1160 of the common era, so far as we can conjecture. Torquemada

says that he has observed, in all the ancient paintings of this journey,

an arm of the sea (A) or large river represented. If ever there were

represented

(A) I believe that this alleged arm of the sea is nothing other than the image

some river in such paintings, it would have been the Colorado, or Red River,

Bk. II. which empties into the Gulf of California at 32½° of latitude, since this is the most considerable of all that are found on the road they took. Having crossed the Red River, then, beyond the 35th degree, they went southeast as far as the river Gila, where they halted for some time; for to this day are seen remains of large buildings made by them on the banks of that river. Then, resuming their road toward the south-southeast, they stopped at a latitude of about 29 degrees, in a place that is distant more than 250 miles from the city of Chihuahua, to the north-northwest. This place is known by the name of Casas Grandes, on account of a vast edifice, still standing, which, according to the universal tradition of those peoples, was built by the Mexicans during their migration. This edifice is made on the plan of those of New Mexico, that is, composed of three stories, with a terrace above them, and without a door in the lower story. The door for entering the building is in the second story, so that a ladder is needed. So do the inhabitants of New Mexico, in order to be less exposed to the assaults of their enemies, putting out the ladder only for those whom they permit to enter their houses. The Aztecs doubtless had the same motive in making the edifice in that form; for in it are observed the marks of a fortress, defended on one flank by a high mountain, and for the rest surrounded by a wall about seven feet thick, whose foundations still exist. In that fortress are seen stones as large as millstones; the beams of the roofs are of pine, and well worked. In the

center of the universal Flood, represented in the Mexican paintings before the beginning of their journey, as is seen in the copy published by Gemelli of a painting shown him by the celebrated Dr. Sigüenza. The Chevalier Boturini claims that this arm of the sea is the Gulf of California, since he was persuaded that the Mexicans passed from Aztlan to California, and then, crossing that gulf, made their way to Culiacán; but since remains of the buildings constructed by the Mexicans on their journey have been found on the Gila River and in Pimería, and not in California, there is no reason to believe that they passed by sea, but rather by land to Culiacán.

From this place, crossing the rugged mountains of the Tarahumara

and directing themselves toward the south, they arrived at

Hueicolhuacan, today called Culiacán, a place situated on the Gulf of

California at 24½°, where they stayed three years. (*) It is to be be‐

lieved that they built houses and huts for their lodging, and sowed for

their sustenance the seeds they carried with them, as they did in all

those places where they stopped for any considerable time. Here they

made of wood a statue representing Huitzilopochtli, protecting god of

the nation, so that it should accompany them on their journey, and

made a seat of canes and rushes to carry it, which they called

Teoicpalli (seat of god), and chose the priests who were to carry it on

their shoulders, who were four at a time, on whom they bestowed the

name of Teotlamacazque (servants of god), and the very act of carrying

it they called Teomama, that is, carrying God on one’s back.

From Hueicolhuacan, journeying many days toward the east, they

went to Chicomoztoc, where they stopped. Up to this point all seven

tribes of the Nahuatlacas had made the migration together; but here

they divided, and while the Xochimilcas,

They say that the division took place by express command of their god; but we are persuaded that some discord separated them. The situation of Chicomoztoc, where the Mexicans stayed nine years, is not known; but it seems to me that this place is twenty miles south of the city of Zacatecas, where to this day are seen the remains of a very vast edifice, which is indubitably the work of the Aztecs on their journey; for, besides the tradition of the Zacatecos, ancient inhabitants of that country, who, being altogether barbarous, neither had houses nor knew how to build them, that building found there by the Spaniards can be ascribed to no one but the Aztecs. That they were then reduced to a smaller number by the dismemberment of the other tribes will probably have been the reason why the Mexicans did not undertake such buildings in the rest of their migration.

From the country of the Zacatecos, journeying south by Ameca, Cocula, and Sayula, they descended to the maritime province of Colima, then to that of Zacatula; whence, turning toward the east, they climbed to Malinalco, a place situated in the mountains that surround the valley of Toluca, (C) and then, taking the road toward the north, they arrived in 1196 in the celebrated city of Tula. (D)

On the journey from Chicomoztoc to Tula they stopped for a while at Coatlicamac, where the tribe divided into two factions, which in the future were eternally rivals, and caused each other in turn the gravest hardships. The cause of this discord

of their camp. Some of them, approaching the first bundle to exam‐

on to unwrap the other bundle, they found nothing but two sticks. At

first sight they despised them as a worthless thing, but warned by the

wise Huitziton of the use they could make of them for striking fire,

they prized them much more than the gem. Those who appropriated

the gem were the ones who, after the founding of Mexico, were called

Tlatelolcas, from the place they founded near that city; the others,

who took the sticks, were those who had in the future the names of

Mexicans or of Tenochca. But this account is not true history, but only

an apologue devised to teach that in things the useful ought to be

prized more than the beautiful. In spite of this discord both parties

continued to travel together, on account of the imaginary interest of

the protection of their god. (E)

It should cause no wonder that the Aztecs made so many circuits

and traveled more than a thousand miles beyond what was needed to

arrive at Anahuac; since they had set themselves no goal, seeking here

and there a country where they could enjoy to advantage all the com‐

forts of life. Nor is it any less to be wondered at that in some places

they made large buildings, thinking, as is to be believed, every place

where they stopped to be the end of their migration. Several sites

seemed to them at first suitable for their settlement, which they af‐

terward abandoned through the experience of unforeseen inconve‐

niences. Wherever they stopped they erected an altar to their god,

and on departing they left the infirm, and probably some others who

looked after them,

and perhaps also some one who, weary of so long a migration, did not wish to expose himself to new hardships. Bk. II.

At Tula they stayed nine years, and then eleven in other places not far off, until in 1216 they arrived at Zumpango, a considerable city of the Valley of Mexico. Tochpanecatl, lord of this city, received them with singular kindness, and, not content with granting them a comfortable lodging and with giving them abundant gifts, being attached to them through long and familiar acquaintance, asked the chiefs of the nation for some noble maiden to be the wife of his son Ilhuicatl. The Mexicans, obliged by so great a benevolence, gave him Tlacapantzin, whom that illustrious young man promptly married, and from them descended, as we shall see, the Mexican kings.

After they had stayed seven years in Zumpango, they went away together with the young Ilhuicatl to Tizayuca, a city not far from that one, where Tlacapantzin gave birth to a son, who was named Huitzilihuitl, and at the same time they gave another maiden to Xochiatzin, lord of Cuauhtitlan. From Tizayuca they passed to Tolpetlac and Tepeyac, where at present there is the town and the most renowned Sanctuary of Our Lady of Guadalupe, places all on the shores of the Lake of Texcoco and very near the site of Mexico, in which they stayed twenty-two years.

As soon as the Mexicans appeared in that country, they were recognized by order of Xolotl, then reigning, who, having nothing to fear from them, permitted them to settle where they could; but finding themselves much harassed in Tepeyac by Tenancacaltzin, a Chichimec lord, they were forced to take refuge in Chapultepec, a mountain situated on the western shore of the lake, barely two miles distant from the site of Mexico, in 1245, in the reign of Nopaltzin, not Quinatzin, as Torquemada and Boturini say. (F)

X2 The

(F) If Quinatzin were believed to be reigning then, it would be necessary that his reign and that of his successor comprise a space of 161 years,

The persecutions that they suffered in this place from some lords, and par‐

Bk. II.

ticularly from the one of Xaltocan, made them, after seventeen years, aban‐

don it to find a safer refuge in Acocolco, a place of several islets at the southern

extremity of the lake. There they led for the space of fifty-two years the most

wretched life in the world. They sustained themselves on fish and every sort

of insect and on marsh roots, and covered themselves with the leaves of the

plant amoxtli, which grows abundantly in that lake, since their garments were

quite worn out and they found there no way of procuring new ones. Their

dwellings were very poor huts, made of the canes and rushes that the lake

produces. It would be altogether incredible that for so many years they could

have survived in so inconvenient a place and in so hard a life, if it were not

verified both by the testimony of their historians and by subsequent events.

§ 18. But here at least, in the midst of misery, they were free; and liberty someSlavery of the

Mexicans in what sweetened their hardships; but in 1314 there came upon their other

site, not being able to endure that the Mexicans should maintain themselves

in his district without paying him tribute, made open war on them, and hav‐

sent them an embassy saying that, taking compassion on the wretched life

that they led in those islets, he granted them a better place, where they might

live more comfortably; that the Mexicans, who desired nothing more, accept‐

ed the favor at once and gladly left that site; but scarcely had they left it when

they were assailed by the Colhuas and made prisoners. Whether it was in one

way or in the other, it is certain that the Mexicans were led as slaves to

Tizapan, a place belonging to the state of Colhuacan.
