Huitzilihuitl, called Moteuczoma (commonly Moctezuma), a young man of great bodily strength and invincible courage, to whom, for his immortal deeds, they gave also the name of Tlacaelel, that is, a man of great heart, and that of Ilhuicamina, that is, the Archer of Heaven; and to designate him in the ancient paintings they represented over his head the sky pierced with an arrow, as may be seen in the seventh and eighth paintings of the Mendoza collection, and as we shall show elsewhere among the figures of the kings of Mexico. This is that Mexican hero whom Father Acosta, or rather Father Tovar, from whom that author took the eulogy, so greatly celebrates under the name of Tlacaelel, although in several of the deeds attributed to him he has fallen into error. His embassy happily concluded, he took leave of the Prince to re‐

turn to Mexico; but on the road he fell into an ambush that his ene‐

mies had laid for him; he was taken prisoner with all his retinue, led

to Chalco, and presented to Toteotzin, lord of that city and a mortal

enemy of the Mexicans. The latter at once had them shut up in a nar‐

row prison under the care of Cuateotzin, a person of consequence, or‐

dering him to furnish the prisoners no other food than what he him‐

self prescribed, until it should be determined what kind of death they

were to end their days with. Cuateotzin, thinking such an order too

inhuman, provided for them abundantly at his own expense. But the

cruel Toteotzin, thinking to do a great service to the Huexotzincas,

sent them the prisoners, so that, if it seemed good to them, they

might sacrifice them in Huexotzinco with the assistance of the

Chalcas, or else in Chalco with the assistance of the Huexotzincas.

These, who were always more humane than the Chalcas, rejected the

proposal with disdain. “What reason is there,” they said, “to deprive

of life men who have no other crime than that of being the faithful

messengers of their lord? And even if they were to die, it would bring

us no honor to kill other people’s prisoners. Go in peace, and tell your

master that the Huexotzinca nobility does not wish to dishonor itself

by deeds so unworthy.” With this answer, and with the prisoners, the

Chalcas returned to Toteotzin, who, being determined to make some

friends

p. 211

friends by means of those prisoners, informed the Tyrant Maxtla, leaving to his will the fate of those unfortunates, and hoping by such Bk. III. flattery to calm the anger caused in him by his perfidy and by his inconstancy in abandoning the Tepanec party for that of Prince Nezahualcoyotl. Meanwhile, while he awaited Maxtla’s reply, he had the prisoners shut up in the same prison and under the custody of the same Cuateotzin. The latter, grieved at the misfortune of a young man so illustrious and so brave, summoned on the evening before the day on which the reply from Azcapotzalco was expected a servant of his in whom he greatly trusted, and ordered him to set the prisoners at liberty that very night, and to tell Moctezuma on his part that he had resolved to save his life at the evident risk of losing his own; that if on this account he should have to die, as was to be feared, he should not forget to show his gratitude in the protection of the children he was leaving; and, finally, that he should not go by land to Mexico, because he would again be taken by the guards who were on the road, but should make his way by Iztapalocan to Chimalhuacan, and there embark to be carried to his city.

The servant carried out the order, and Moctezuma followed the counsel of Cuateotzin. They left the prison that night and cautiously made their way toward Chimalhuacan, where they kept hidden the whole following day, and, having nothing else to eat, fed on raw herbs; at night they embarked, and with the greatest speed were carried to Mexico, where they were received with singular rejoicing, since they were already believed dead there.

As soon as the barbarous Toteotzin was informed of the flight of the prisoners, he fell into a rage, and since he did not doubt that Cuateotzin had been the author of their liberty, he had him put to death at once and quartered, together with his wife and his children, of whom a son and a daughter were saved. The latter took refuge in Mexico, where she was much honored out of regard for her father, who by the sacrifice of his own life had rendered so important a service to the Mexican nation.

Dd2 After

p. 212

After this vexation Toteotzin had another no less great in the reply Bk. III. of the Tyrant Maxtla. The latter, being enraged against the Chalcas for

the help given to Nezahualcoyotl and for the slaughter they had made

at Coatlichan, sent Toteotzin a most severe reprimand, calling him a

double-dealer and a traitor, and ordering him to set the prisoners at

liberty without delay. A reward worthy of a perfidious flatterer! Maxtla

did not take this resolution in order to favor the Mexicans, whom he

hated mortally, but only to show contempt for Toteotzin’s homage and

to oppose his wishes. So far was he from favoring the Mexican nation

that he had never been so intent as he was then on its ruin, and had al‐

ready made ready troops to strike a decisive blow at Mexico, and then

to go on and recover all that Nezahualcoyotl had taken from him. This

Prince, aware of Maxtla’s designs, went to Mexico to confer with that

prudent king on the order they were to observe in that war and on the

measures to be taken to thwart the Tyrant’s projects, and they agreed

to unite the Texcocan troops with those of Mexico for the defense of

that city, on whose fate the outcome of the war seemed to depend.

At the rumor of the imminent war the Mexican commoners were so

greatly disturbed that, thinking themselves incapable of resisting the

power of the Tepanecs, whom until then they had acknowledged as

their superiors, they went in crowds to the king to beg him, with cries

and with tears, not to undertake so perilous a war, which would infalli‐

bly bring about the overthrow of their city and of the nation. What

would you have us do, then, said the king, to deliver ourselves from so many

calamities? Let us ask for peace, replied the commoners, of the king of

Azcapotzalco, and offer ourselves to serve him; and to move him to clemency, let

our god be brought into his presence on the shoulders of the priests. Such were

the clamors, accompanied by threats, that the prudent king, fearing

some popular sedition more pernicious than the war with the enemies,

was constrained, against his will,

to

p. 213

to comply with the people’s request. Moctezuma happened to be present, and, unable to endure that a nation which boasted so much Bk. III. of honor should adopt so ignominious a course, he spoke thus to the commoners: “Ah, what are you thinking of, O Mexicans? Have you lost your wits? How has such cowardice entered into your hearts? Have you perhaps forgotten that you are Mexicans, and descendants of those heroes who founded this city, and of those valiant men who have preserved it in spite of the opposition of our enemies? Either change your sentiment, then, or renounce the glory that you have inherited from your forefathers.” And turning then to the king: “How, my lord,” he said to him, “can you permit so great an ignominy in your people? Speak to them once more, and tell them to let us adopt another course before putting ourselves so foolishly and so shamefully into the hands of our enemies.”

The king, who desired nothing more than this, spoke to the commoners, recommending the counsel of Moctezuma, which was well received. “Well, then,” said the king, addressing the nobility, “which of you, who are the flower of the nation, will have the courage to carry an embassy to the lord of the Tepanecs?” They all began to look at one another, and there was none among them who dared to face the danger, until Moctezuma presented himself with youthful intrepidity, saying: “I will go; for if after all I must die, it matters little whether it be today or tomorrow, nor can a better occasion be found for dying with glory, sacrificing my life to the honor of my nation. Here I am, my lord, ready to obey your commands. Command me, then, whatever you please.” The king, pleased with so great a courage, ordered him to go and propose peace to the Tyrant, but without consenting to ignominious conditions. The brave young man set out at once, and meeting the Tepanec guards, persuaded them to let him bear to their lord an embassy of the highest importance. Presented to the Tyrant, he asked him for peace in the name of his king and of his nation, but under honorable conditions. The Tyrant

replied

Tyrant’s own mouth the resolution of war, he performed with him the

ceremonies customary among lords when they challenged one another,

that is, he presented him with certain defensive arms, anointed his head,

and attached feathers to it in the manner in which they did so with the

dead; and besides this he protested to him in the name of his king that,

by refusing to accept the peace offered him, he would without doubt be

ruined together with the whole nation of the Tepanecs. The Tyrant, with‐

out showing displeasure at these ceremonies or at the threats made to

him, gave him also some arms to present to the king of Mexico, and

warned Moctezuma, for the safety of his person, to return in disguise by

a little door of that palace. The Tyrant would not this time have paid so

much heed to the law of nations, if he had foreseen that this same am‐

bassador, whose life he was taking care of, was to be the principal instru‐

ment of his ruin. Moctezuma profited by the warning; but as soon as he

saw himself out of danger, he began to insult the guards, reproaching

them with their negligence and threatening them with their speedy de‐

struction. The guards rushed upon him to kill him; but he defended him‐

self so courageously that he killed one or two men, and others coming

up, he retired precipitately to Mexico, bringing the news that war was al‐

ready declared and that the leaders of both nations had been challenged.

At such news the commoners were again thrown into confusion, and

had recourse to the king to ask of him permission to abandon

So be it, said the Tyrant. commoners again, if you are defeated; but if you gain the victory, from this moment we also bind ourselves, we and our descendants, to be your tributaries, to till your lands and those of the nobles, to build your houses, and to carry for you, every time you go to war, your arms and your baggage. This agreement having been made between the nobles and the commoners, and the command of all the Mexican troops having been committed to the brave Moctezuma, the king gave prompt notice to Prince Nezahualcoyotl to come at once with his army to Mexico, as he did one day before the battle.

It cannot be doubted that causeways on the lake had already been built by the Mexicans at this time for the more convenient communication of their city with those of the mainland; for otherwise the movements and skirmishes of both armies cannot be understood; indeed, we know from history that these causeways were cut by ditches, over which they had drawbridges; but no historian indicates the time at which they were built. (h) For my part, I truly wonder that in the midst of a life so calamitous the Mexicans had spirit enough to undertake and to carry out a work so great and so difficult.

On the day following the arrival of Prince Nezahualcoyotl in Mexico, the army of the Tepanecs showed itself in the field, very numerous and brilliant, no less for the little plates of gold with which they went adorned than for the handsome plumes they wore on their heads, with which they seemed to wish to enlarge their stature. They accompanied their march with great shouts,

boasting

(h) I believe that the Mexicans had by this time built the causeways of Tacuba and of Tepeyac, not that of Iztapalapan, which is larger, and where the lake was deepest.

p. 216

boasting too soon of victory. This army was commanded by a famous Bk. III.

general called Mazatl. The Tyrant Maxtla, although he had accepted

the challenge, nevertheless would not stir from his palace, either be‐

cause he thought it degrading to himself to have to fight with the

king of Mexico, or, what is more likely, because he feared the fortune

of war. As soon as the Mexicans were warned of the movements of

the Tepanecs, they went out in good order to receive them, and the

signal of battle having been given by King Itzcoatl with the sound of

a little drum which he carried on his shoulder, both armies attacked

with indescribable fury, each side being fully persuaded that that bat‐

tle was to decide its fate. For the greater part of the day it could not

be known to which side victory was inclining, since what the

Tepanecs gained they lost again a little later. But a little before sunset,

the Mexican commoners, seeing the forces of the enemy continually

increasing with fresh reinforcements, began to lose heart and to com‐

plain of their leaders. What are we doing, O Mexicans! they said to one

another; will it be well to sacrifice our lives to the ambition of our king and of

our general! How much more salutary will it not be to surrender, humbly con‐

fessing our rashness, in order to obtain pardon and the grace of life?

The king heard these words with the greatest grief, and seeing that

with them his people were losing heart more and more, he sum‐

moned to council the Prince and the general to ask their opinion on

what ought to be done to encourage the commoners, so dismayed.

What? replied Moctezuma, fight to the death. If we die with arms in hand

defending our liberty, we shall have done our duty. If we survive defeated, we

shall remain covered with eternal confusion. Let us go, then, let us go and die.

The clamors of the almost vanquished Mexicans were already begin‐

ning to prevail, among whom there were some so base that, calling to

their enemies, they said: O mighty Tepanecs, lords of the mainland, restrain

your anger; for we now surrender. If you please,

here

p. 217

here, before your very eyes, we will kill our chiefs, to deserve from you pardon

Bk. III. for the rashness into which their ambition has led us. So great was the indignation which the king, the prince, the general, and the nobility felt at such clamors that they would on the spot have punished with death the baseness of their cowardly soldiers, had it not been in order not to make the enemy’s victory easier; and so, dissembling their displeasure, they all cried with one voice, Let us go and die with glory, and threw themselves with such impetus upon the enemy that they drove them back from a ditch which they had occupied and made them retreat. With this advantage the king began to animate his people, while the Prince and the general worked wonders of courage. In the hottest of the fray Moctezuma met the Tepanec general, who came on proud of the terror his soldiers had caused among the Mexican commoners, and dealt him so fierce a blow on the head that he stretched him lifeless at his feet. The rumor of victory spread at once through the whole field, whereby the Mexicans took great courage; but the Tepanecs were so dismayed by the death of their brave general Mazatl that they immediately fell into disorder. The night coming on prevented the Mexicans from continuing their advance: whereupon both sides withdrew to their cities, the Mexicans full of courage and impatient at not being able, because of the darkness of the night, to complete their victory, and the Tepanecs disconsolate and melancholy, though not wholly without hope of avenging themselves on the following day.

Maxtla, greatly afflicted both by the death of his general and by the defeat of his troops, spent that night (the last of his life) encouraging his captains, and representing to them on the one hand the glory of triumph, and on the other the evils to which they would be subject if they should ever be defeated: for the Mexicans, who until that time had been tributaries of the Tepanecs, should they remain victorious, would force the Tepanecs to pay tribute to them. stones, and his corpse was thrown into the field to be food for the

birds of prey. Such was the tragic end of Maxtla, who had not yet

completed three years of his tyranny. Thus ended the injustice, the

cruelty, the ambition, and the perfidy of this man, and the most griev‐

ous injuries done by him to the lawful heir of the kingdom of

Acolhuacan, to his brother Tayatzin, and to the kings of Mexico. His

memory is odious and execrable in the annals of those nations.

The following night the victors busied themselves in sacking the city, in demolishing the houses, and in burning the temples, leaving that court, once so celebrated, in such a state that it could not recover in many years. While the Mexicans and the Acolhuas were gathering the fruits of their victory, the Tlaxcalans and the Huexotzincas, detached from the army, took by assault the ancient court of Tenayuca, and on the following day came to join them, in order to take the city of Cuetlachtepec.

The fugitive Tepanecs, finding themselves reduced to the greatest misery in the mountains, and fearing to be overtaken there too by the victors, thought of surrendering and imploring their clemency; and to obtain it they sent to the king of Mexico a distinguished personage accompanied by other nobles from several places of the Tepanec nation. This ambassador humbly asked the king for pardon in the name of his fellow countrymen, offered him obedience, and promised that all the Tepanecs would acknowledge him as their lawful lord and serve him as vassals. He congratulated himself on their fortune, in the midst of so great an overthrow, in having to submit to a king so worthy and endowed with the most excellent qualities, and finally concluded his speech by earnestly begging him to be willing to grant them the grace of life and the liberty to return to their houses. Itzcoatl received them with the greatest kindness, granted all they wished, and declared that he would receive them not as subjects but as sons, and offered to perform toward them all the offices of a true father; but at the same time he threatened them with utter extermination should they dare to violate the fidelity they had sworn to him. After this favor the fugitives returned to their homes to rebuild their houses and to attend to the interests of their families; and from that time they remained always subject to the king of Mexico, adding by their misfortune to the examples of vicissitude that we constantly observe

Ee2 in

p. 220

in human happiness. But not all the Tepanecs submitted at once to Bk. III.

the obedience of the Conqueror; for those of Coyoacán, a consider‐

able city and state of the same nation, persisted for some time, as we

shall see later, in their original side.

King Itzcoatl, after this famous conquest, had the commoners rati‐

fy the agreement already made by them with the nobility, whereupon

they remained perpetually obliged to serve it, as they always did in

the future; but those who by their clamors and laments had disheart‐

ened the others in the battle, were cut off from the body of the na‐

tion and from the Mexican state, and, as base and cowardly, perpetu‐

ally banished. To Moctezuma then, and to the others who had most

distinguished themselves in the war, the king gave the ownership of a

part of the conquered lands, and assigned another part to the priests

for their sustenance; and after giving the orders suitable to make his

dominion firmer and more stable, he returned with his army to

Mexico to celebrate with public rejoicing the good fortune of his

arms and to thank the gods for their supposed protection.

Acolhuacan. Acolhuacan. If the king of Mexico had set aside fidelity and justice in favor of ambition, he would not have lacked pretexts to take possession of that kingdom also. The Tyrant Tezozomoc had placed Chimalpopoca in possession of Texcoco, and the latter, as lord, had commanded in that court. Itzcoatl, who had entered into all the rights of his predecessor, could consider that state as incorporated for some years past into the crown of Mexico. Having moreover on the other hand lawfully conquered Azcapotzalco and subdued the Tepanecs, he seemed bound to succeed to all the rights of the vanquished, rights which, both by twelve years of possession and by the acceptance of the greater part of the peoples, might perhaps

Even after the defeat of the Tepanecs there were several cities in

the kingdom that did not wish to submit to the heir Prince, for fear

of the punishment they had deserved. One of these was the city of

Huexotla, near the court of Texcoco, whose lord Huitznahuatl (a) had

persisted obstinately in rebellion. The allied troops set out from

Mexico, and, making their way over the plains now called those of

Santa Marta, halted at Chimalhuacan, whence the king and the Prince

sent to offer pardon to those citizens if they would surrender, threat‐

ening to put the city to fire and sword if they persisted in their rebel‐

lion; but the rebels, instead of accepting the offer, came out in battle

array against the royal army. The fight did not last long; for the lord

of that city having been taken by the invincible Moctezuma, the rebel

troops took to flight, and then humbly begged pardon, presenting to

the Victor, as they were accustomed to do, pregnant women, children,

and old men, to move him to compassion. The way to the throne of

Acolhuacan being at last smoothed, and the Prince placed upon it,

the auxiliary troops of Huexotzinco and Tlaxcala were dismissed with

singular demonstrations of gratitude and with a good part of the

booty of Azcapotzalco.

Then the army of the Mexicans and of the Acolhuas marched

Whereupon King Itzcoatl commanded that no Mexican should go to Coyoacán until the insolence of those rebels should be punished. The expedition of Huexotla being therefore ended, he marched against them. In the first three battles he fought he scarcely gained any other advantage than that of making them retreat a little; but in the fourth, while the two armies were fighting furiously, Moctezuma, with the courageous troops he had placed in ambush, fell with such impetus upon the rear guard of the rebels that he threw them into disorder and forced them to abandon the field and flee to the city. He pursued them also, and perceiving that they meant to fortify themselves in the great temple, forestalled them by occupying it, and burned the tower of that sanctuary. At such a blow the rebels were so dismayed that, leaving the city, they fled to the mountains that lie south of Coyoacán; but even there they were pressed by the royal troops for more than thirty miles, until on a mountain west of Cuauhnahuac the fugitives, weary and with no hope of escaping, threw down their arms in token of surrender and gave themselves up to the discretion of the victors.

By this victory Itzcoatl remained master of the whole state of the Tepanecs, and Moctezuma full of glory. It is a marvel, say the historians, that the greater part of the prisoners taken in that war of Coyoacán belonged to Moctezuma and to three brave Acolhua officers; for all four, following the example of the ancient Mexicans in the war against the Xochimilcas, had agreed to cut a lock of hair from all those they took, and on the greater part of them such a mark was found.

Having brought this expedition to so happy an end, and settled the affairs of Coyoacán and of the other conquered cities, both kings returned to Mexico. King Itzcoatl thought it fitting to place at the head of the Tepanecs someone

true electors. The king of Mexico bound himself reciprocally to assist

each of those two kings wherever there might be need. This alliance

of the three kings, which was maintained firm and unaltered for al‐

most a century, was the cause of the rapid conquests which the

Mexicans afterwards made. This was not the only master stroke of

King Itzcoatl’s policy: he also rewarded advantageously all those who

had distinguished themselves in the war, not making so much account

Thus the hope of reward animated them to the most heroic enterprises, being sure that their glory and their advantages did not depend upon certain accidents of fortune, but upon the merit of their own actions. Such a policy was for the most part adopted by the later kings, with great benefit to the state. This famous alliance being established, Itzcoatl went with King Nezahualcoyotl to Texcoco to crown him with his own hand. This ceremony was celebrated with the greatest solemnity in 1426. Thereupon the king of Mexico returned to his court, and the king of Acolhuacan applied himself with the greatest diligence to reforming his own.

The kingdom of Acolhuacan was not so well regulated as

§ 4. Techotlalatzin had left it. The domination of the Tepanecs and the Singular regulations revolutions that had occurred in those twenty years had altered the of King

Nezahualcoyotl. government of the peoples, weakened the vigor of the laws, and in great part spoiled the customs. Nezahualcoyotl, who besides the love he bore to his nation was endowed with singular prudence, made such regulations for the reform of the kingdom that it was soon seen more flourishing than under any other of his predecessors. He gave a new form to the councils already established by his grandfather. He conferred the offices on the most suitable persons. There was a council for purely civil causes, and besides others there sat in it five lords who in his adversities had been constantly faithful to him. There was another council for criminal causes, over which presided two princes, his brothers, of the highest integrity. The Council of War was composed of the most famous captains, among whom the first place was held by the lord of Teotihuacan, son-in-law of the king and one of the thirteen grandees of the kingdom. The council for the royal revenues consisted of the king’s stewards and of the chief merchants of the court. There were three principal stewards, who had charge of the tributes and of the other revenues of the king. He established assemblies in the manner of academies for their poetry, for astronomy, for music,

History of Mexico, Vol. I. Ff for

p. 226

for painting, for history, and for the art of divination, and he sum‐

Bk. IV.

moned to the court the most renowned professors of the kingdom,

who assembled on certain days to communicate to one another their

lights and their inventions; and for each of the aforesaid sciences and

arts, imperfect though they were, he founded schools at the court. As

to the mechanical arts, he divided the city of Texcoco into thirty and

more parts, and assigned to each art its own, to the exclusion of the

others, so that in one place were the goldsmiths, in another the sculp‐

tors, in another the weavers, and so with the rest. For the promotion

of religion he built new temples, created ministers for the worship of

their gods, gave them houses, and assigned revenues both for their

sustenance and for the other expenses to be incurred in the festivals

and in the sacrifices. To increase the splendor of his court he con‐

structed great buildings within and outside the city, and planted new

gardens and woods, which in part were preserved many years after

the conquest, and some vestiges of that magnificence are seen to this

day.

good army under the command of the celebrated Moctezuma, and

fought on the borders of Xochimilco. Great as was the number of the

Xochimilcas, they did not fight with the good order of the Mexicans,

p. 227

fire to the towers of the temples and to other buildings. Unable to

Bk. IV. withstand their attack, the citizens fled to the mountains; but being besieged there also by the Mexicans, they finally surrendered. Moctezuma was received by the Xochimilca priests with music of little flutes and small drums, having dispatched so important an expedition in only eleven days. The king of Mexico presently betook himself to take possession of that city, which was, as we have already indicated, the greatest of the Valley of Mexico after the courts; there he was recognized and acclaimed king, received the obedience of those new subjects, and promised to love them as a father and to take care in the future of their interests.

The misfortune of the Xochimilcas was not enough to frighten the people of Cuitlahuac; indeed, the advantageous situation of their city, founded on a little island of Lake Chalco, encouraged them to provoke the Mexicans to war. Itzcoatl wished to fall upon them with all the forces of Mexico; but Moctezuma offered to humble their pride with smaller forces, and therefore levied some companies of young men, especially of those who were brought up in the seminaries of Mexico; and having exercised them in arms, and instructed them in the order and manner which they were to observe in that war, he prepared a proportionate number of boats and went with such a flotilla against the people of Cuitlahuac. We are wholly ignorant of the particular circumstances of this expedition; but we know that after seven days the city was taken and subjected to the obedience of the king of Mexico, and that the young men returned laden with spoils and brought with them a good number of prisoners to be sacrificed to the god of war. Nor is the precise year known in which this war was made, any more than the time of that of Cuauhnahuac, though the latter seems to have been made toward the end of the reign of Itzcoatl.

The lord of Xiuhtepec, a city of the country of the Tlahuicas more than thirty miles south of Mexico, had asked of his neighbor the lord of Cuauhnahuac a daughter of his for wife, and the latter had granted her. Afterwards the lord

of Tlaltexcal laid claim to her, and to him he gave her at once, heedBk. IV. less of the promise made to the first, either because of some offense

himself; but, unable to do so by himself because of the inferiority of

his forces, he implored the favor of the king of Mexico, promising to

be always his friend and ally and to serve him, whenever he should re‐

war just and the occasion opportune for enlarging his dominions,

armed his people and summoned those of Acolhuacan and of Tacuba.

He certainly needed so large an army, since the lord of Cuauhnahuac

was very powerful and his city very strong, as the Spaniards after‐

wards found by experience when they besieged it. Itzcoatl command‐

ed that the whole army should assault the city at one time, the

Mexicans by Ocuillan on the west side, the Tepanecs by

Tlatzacapechco on the north side, and the Texcocans together with

the people of Xiuhtepec by Tlalquitenanco on the east and south

sides. The people of Cuauhnahuac, trusting in the natural strength of

the city, chose to await the assault. The first to deliver it were the

Tepanecs, who were vigorously repulsed; but the other troops arriving

immediately, the citizens were forced to surrender and to submit to

the king of Mexico, to whom they paid annually from that time for‐

ward tribute in cotton, in paper, and in other commodities, as we

shall say elsewhere. By the conquest of that great, pleasant, and

strong city, which was the capital of the Tlahuicas, a great part of that

country remained under the dominion of the Mexican king; and soon

after there were added to these conquests those of Cuauhtitlan and of

Toltitlan, considerable cities, fifteen miles north of Mexico; but we

are wholly ignorant of their circumstances.

In this manner a city which a little before had been tributary to

the Tepanecs, and not much esteemed by the other nations, found it‐

self in little more than twelve years in a state to command those very

ones who had dominated it, and the peoples who thought

themselves

p. 229

themselves superior. So much does the valor and wisdom of its Head matter to the happiness of a nation! The great Itzcoatl died at last, af‐ Bk. IV. ter so glorious a reign and at a very advanced age, in 1436 of the common era. He was a king justly celebrated by the Mexicans for his singular qualities and for the incomparable services he rendered them. He served the nation for more than thirty years in the office of General, and governed it for nearly thirteen as sovereign. Besides having freed it from the domination of the Tepanecs, enlarged its dominions, restored the royal family of the Chichimecs to the throne of Acolhuacan, enriched his court with the spoils of the conquered cities, and laid, in the triple alliance that he established, the foundations of its future greatness, he also ennobled it with new buildings. Among others, after the conquest of Cuitlahuac he built a temple to the goddess Cihuacoatl, and soon after another to Huitzilopochtli. The Mexicans celebrated his funeral rites with extraordinary solemnity and with the greatest demonstrations of grief, and laid his ashes in the same tomb as those of his predecessors.

The four electors had no need to deliberate in the election of the § 6. new king; for, since no brother of the deceased kings survived, the Moctezuma I, fifth

king of Mexico. election had to fall upon one of the nephews of Itzcoatl; and no one was truly found more worthy than Moctezuma Ilhuicamina, the son of Huitzilihuitl, no less for his virtues than for the important services he had rendered his nation. He was therefore elected with general applause, and notice of this was given at once to the two allied kings, who not only ratified the election but even celebrated it with great praises of the one elected, and sent him presents worthy of his greatness and of their esteem. After the customary ceremonies and the congratulatory speeches of the priests, the nobles, and the soldiers, there were great rejoicings, with banquets, dances, and illuminations. But before coming to the coronation, whether by a law established by the nation or by his own will, he went out to war to take prisoners from among the enemy to be sacrificed at this great ceremony. He determined that they should be Chalcas

Moctezuma

p. 231

Moctezuma, without any regard for the high rank of the prisoners, and without fear of the baneful effects of his inhuman resolution, im‐ Bk. IV. mediately put all five to death; and so that there might never be lacking before his eyes a spectacle in which his cruelty could take delight, he had their bodies salted and dried; and when they were quite dry he placed them in a hall of his house, so that they might serve to hold up the pine torches with which light was made at night.

The report of so horrible an event spread at once through the whole land. The king of Texcoco, whose heart was pierced by this news, asked help of the allied kings to avenge the death of his sons. Moctezuma decided that the Texcocan army should attack the city of Chalco by land, while he and the king of Tacuba with their troops would attack it by water; and to bring this about he assembled an incredible number of boats, capable of transporting so many men, and he wished to command this flotilla in person. The Chalcas, in spite of so great a multitude of enemies, made a vigorous resistance; for besides being warriors by nature, this time despair increased their courage. The lord of that state himself, although he was so old that he could not walk on his own feet, had himself carried in a litter to encourage his subjects by his presence and his voice. Nevertheless they were utterly defeated, the city was sacked, and its lord was punished with the extreme penalty for his atrocious crimes. The booty was divided among the three kings, according to the agreement made under King Itzcoatl; but the city, with all that state, remained from then on subject to the king of Mexico. This victory, according to what the historians say, was owed in great part to the courage of Axoquentzin, the young son of Nezahualcoyotl.

warnings of this song drew tears from those present, to whom love of

life made the memory of death the more distressing.

Conquests of went to the province of the Cohuixcas, south of Mexico, to avenge Moctezuma. the death that those peoples had inflicted upon certain Mexicans. In this glorious expedition he added to his crown the states of Huaxtepec, Yauhtepec, Tepoztlan, Yacapichtla, Totolapan, Tlalcozauhtitlan, Chilapan, which is more than a hundred and fifty miles distant from the court, Coixco, Oztomantla, Tlachmallac, and several others; and returning toward the west he conquered Tzompahuacan, leaving from then on subject to the dominion of the king of Mexico both the great country of the Cohuixcas, who had been the authors of those deaths, and many other states near that country, which perhaps by such insults had provoked his anger. On his return to the court he enlarged the temple of Huitzilopochtli and adorned it with the spoils of those peoples. All these conquests were made by him in the first nine years of his reign.

In the tenth year, which was 1446 of the common era, there was § 11.

Inundation of in Mexico a great inundation caused by overly abundant rains, which Mexico. brought so much water to the lake that, being unable to contain itself within its bed, it overflowed and flooded the city to such a degree that it ruined several houses and left no street where one could go on foot, it being necessary to use boats everywhere.

History of Mexico, Vol. I. Gg Moctezuma

p. 234

Moctezuma, much afflicted by such a calamity, had recourse to the

Bk. IV. king of Texcoco, hoping to receive from his wisdom the suggestion of

some remedy. This prudent king was of the opinion that a great dike

should be built to hold the waters in check, and he prescribed the di‐

mensions and the place where it should be made. The counsel pleased

Moctezuma, and he commanded that it be carried out with the great‐

est promptness. He ordered the people of Azcapotzalco, of

Coyoacán, and of Xochimilco to furnish some thousands of thick

stakes, and other peoples to supply the necessary stones. He also

summoned for this work the inhabitants of Tacuba, of Iztapalapan, of

Colhuacan, and of Tenayuca; and the kings and lords themselves went

before the others in the labor: by which example their subjects were

so encouraged that in a short time a work was seen perfectly com‐

pleted which otherwise could hardly have been completed in several

years. The dike was nine miles in length and eleven braccia in width,

and was composed of two parallel palisades, the space between which

was filled with stone and sand. The greatest difficulty was found in

having to work within the lake, and especially in some places of con‐

siderable depth; but it was overcome by the skill of the Director and

the constancy of the workmen. This dike was in truth most useful to

the city, though it did not suffice to free it entirely from inundations:

nor should this cause wonder, since the Spaniards, although they

availed themselves of European engineers, were nonetheless unable to

make that city wholly safe, either by the labor of two centuries and a

half or by the expenditure of some millions of sequins. While this

work was in progress, the Chalcas rebelled; but they were promptly

reduced to obedience, though not without the loss of some Mexican

captains.

§ 12. To the calamity of the inundation there soon succeeded that of Famine in Mexico.

famine; for in the years 1448 and 49 the maize harvest was very

scanty, because the frost came while the ears were still tender. In

1450 the harvest was likewise lost for lack of water. In

1451

p. 235

1451, besides the weather having been unfavorable, there was scarcely any grain to sow, since almost all of it had been consumed because Bk. IV. of the scantiness of the earlier harvests; so that in 1452 the need of the peoples was so great that, the generosity of the king and of the lords, who opened their granaries for the benefit of their subjects, not being enough to relieve it, they were reduced to buying what they needed with their own liberty. Moctezuma, unable to raise his subjects out of their misery, permitted them to go off to other countries to procure their food; but knowing that some made slaves of themselves for the sustenance of only two or three days, he published an edict in which he commanded that no woman be sold for less than four hundred ears of maize, and no man for less than five hundred. But nothing sufficed to avoid the pernicious effects of the dearth. Some of those who went to seek relief in other countries died of hunger in the roads. Others, who sold themselves elsewhere, never returned to their homeland. The greater part of the Mexican common people sustained themselves, like their ancestors, on aquatic birds and marsh plants, and on the insects and little fish that they caught in the same lake. The following year was not so bad, and finally in 1454, which was a secular year, there was a most abundant harvest, not only of maize but also of legumes and of every sort of fruit.

But the Mexicans could not enjoy their abundance in peace; for

§ 13. they had to go out to war against Atonaltzin, lord of the city and state New conquests, and of Coaixtlahuacan in the country of the Mixtecs. He was a powerful death of Moctezuma. lord who, I know not why, would not allow any Mexican passage through his lands, and did all the harm he could to all who came there for whatever interest. Moctezuma, gravely resentful of his hostilities, sent him an embassy to learn from him the reason for such conduct, threatening him with war if he did not give suitable satisfaction. Atonaltzin received the embassy with scorn, and, having had set before the ambassadors

Mexicans would return with greater forces, he asked help of the

The cause of these wars was the same that gave rise to many others, namely that the inhabitants of those places had killed, in time of peace, some Mexican merchants or couriers.

More difficult and more famous was the expedition undertaken in 1457 against Cuetlachtlan, or Cotaxtla. This province, situated, as we have already said, on the coast of the Gulf of Mexico, and founded, or at least inhabited, by the Olmecs, who had been driven out by the Tlaxcalans, was very populous. We likewise do not know the cause of this war; but we do know that the people of Cotaxtla, foreseeing the storm that hung over them, called to their aid the Huexotzincas and the Tlaxcalans. These, being gravely resentful of the defeat of Coaixtlahuacan, and wishing to avenge themselves, not only offered to help them, but also persuaded the Cholulans, their neighbors, to enter the same confederation. These three republics sent numerous troops to Cotaxtla to await the enemy there. Moctezuma for his part raised a large and brilliant army, in which the flower of the Mexican, Acolhua, Tlatelolca, and Tepanec nobility had enlisted. Besides other personages there were in this army Axayacatl, General, Tizoc, and Ahuitzotl, all three brothers and of the royal house of Mexico, who successively occupied that throne after Moctezuma their cousin. There were likewise the lords of Colhuacan and of Tenayuca; but the most noteworthy by his rank was Moquihuix, king of Tlatelolco, successor of the unfortunate Cuauhtlatoa. When this army left Mexico, the news of the confederation of the three republics with the people of Cotaxtla had not yet arrived; as soon as Moctezuma learned of it, he sent his generals couriers

with

p. 238

with the order not to advance further, but to return at once to the court. The Bk. IV. generals went into deliberation: some were of the opinion that the sov‐

ereign’s orders should be obeyed without reply; others said that there was no

obligation to submit to an order that would do so grave a prejudice to their

honor, since their nobility would be discredited and abased if they ever

shunned fighting on so opportune an occasion to show their bravery. The

first opinion nevertheless prevailed, as the safer; but as they were about to

march toward Mexico, King Moquihuix said to them: “Let those return to

whom courage suffices to turn their backs on the enemy, while I with my

Tlatelolcas alone will win the honor of the victory.” This resolution of

Moquihuix stung and fired the other generals in such a way that all deter‐

mined to face the danger. At last the battle was fought, in which, although

the people of Cotaxtla fought courageously, they were nonetheless defeated

with their allies. Of these the greater part remained on the field, both of the

one and of the other; six thousand two hundred prisoners were taken, who a

little later were sacrificed in Mexico at the festival of the dedication of the

Quauhxicalco, or of the religious edifice destined to preserve the skulls of the

victims. All that province then remained subject to the king of Mexico, who

established a garrison there to keep those peoples in obedience to the crown.

So great a victory was owed principally to the courage of King Moquihuix,

and down to our own times a Mexican ode or song (e), composed then in his

praise, has been preserved. Moctezuma, more pleased at the happy outcome

of the war than offended at the disobedience to his orders, rewarded the

king of Tlatelolco by giving him in marriage a cousin of his, sister of the

aforesaid princes Axayacatl, Tizoc, and Ahuitzotl.

Meanwhile the Chalcas were making themselves every day more deserving

of

of punishment, not only for the rebellion but also for other new crimes. At this time they had the rashness to take prisoner a brother Bk. IV. of King Moctezuma himself, who was, as we believe, lord of Ehecatepec, together with other Mexicans. Such an attempt, carried out upon a person so closely bound in blood to their sovereign, seems to have been a means devised by them to withdraw from the dominion of the Mexicans and make the city of Chalco a rival of that of Mexico; for they wished to make that lord king of Chalco, and often, though in vain, proposed it to him. He, seeing them obstinate in their resolution, told them that he accepted the crown offered to him; and in order that the act of his exaltation might be more solemn, he wished a very tall tree to be planted in the marketplace, and a scaffold to be made upon it, from which he could be seen by all. Everything was done as he required, and, gathering the Mexicans around the tree, he climbed upon the scaffold with a nosegay of flowers in his hand; and from that height, in view of an immense throng of people, he spoke to his own thus: “You know well, brave Mexicans, that the Chalcas wish to crown me king; but may it not please our god that I should betray our country: rather I wish to teach you by my example to prize the fidelity owed to it more than life itself.” And having said this he hurled himself from the scaffold. An act truly barbarous, but quite in conformity with the ideas that the ancients had of magnanimity, and so much the less blameworthy than that of Cato and of others celebrated by antiquity as the motive was nobler and the spirit of the Mexican greater. This resolution so inflamed the anger of the Chalcas that they at once fell upon the other Mexicans and killed them with lance thrusts. The following night they chanced to hear the melancholy cry of an owl, and, as men given to superstition, they took it for a bad omen of their imminent ruin. Nor were they mistaken in their presentiment of their misfortune; for Moctezuma, gravely angered by their rebellion and by their enormous outrages, immediately proclaimed the

p. 240

war, and had fires lit on the summits of the mountains as a signal of Bk. IV.

the condemnation to which he subjected the rebels. He then

marched with his army against that province, and made so great a

slaughter in it that it remained almost depopulated. Very many were

killed, and those who saved their lives fled to the caves of the moun‐

tains that overlook the plains of Chalco, and others, to withdraw far‐

ther from the danger, passing beyond those mountains, took refuge in

Huexotzinco and in Atlixco. The city of Chalco was sacked. To the

fury of vengeance there succeeded in Moctezuma, as is wont in noble

hearts, compassion for the afflicted. He published a general pardon

for all the fugitives, and especially in favor of the old men, the

women, and the children, inviting them to return without fear to

their homeland; and, not content with this, he sent his troops to

scour the mountains to gather up those unfortunate people who, flee‐

ing from men, had sought refuge among the beasts. Thus many re‐

turned, who were distributed in Amaquemecan, in Tlalmanalco, and

in other places; but some, either from distrust of the pardon or from

despair, abandoned themselves to death in the mountains. A part of

the countryside of Chalco was divided by Moctezuma among the cap‐

tains who had distinguished themselves most in the war.

After this expedition the Mexicans conquered Tamazollan,

Piaztlan, Xilotepec, Acatlan, and other places. By these rapid con‐

quests Moctezuma so enlarged his dominions that to the east they ex‐

tended as far as the Gulf of Mexico, to the southeast as far as the cen‐

ter of the great country of the Mixtecs, to the south as far as

Chilapan, and beyond, to the west as far as the Valley of Toluca, to

the northwest as far as the center of the country of the Otomis, and

to the north as far as the end of the Valley of Mexico.

But in attending to war, this famous king did not neglect what per‐

tained to political government and to religion. He published new

laws, increased the splendor of his court, and introduced there a cer‐

tain ceremonial unknown to his predecessors. He built a great temple

to the god of war, instituted

many

p. 241

many rites, and increased the number of the priests. The interpreter

Bk. IV. of the Mendoza collection adds that Moctezuma was sober, and singularly severe in punishing drunkenness, and that by his justice, his prudence, and the goodness of his manners he made himself feared and respected by his subjects. Finally, after a glorious reign of twenty-eight years and some months, he died, lamented by all, in 1464. His funeral rites were celebrated with so much the greater pomp as the magnificence of the court and the power of the nation were already greater.

Before dying he convoked the chief nobility of the court, and

§ 14. made them a speech to exhort them to concord, and to beg the elec‐ Axayacatl, sixth king

of Mexico. tors to elect after his days Axayacatl, whom he judged the man most fit to promote the glory of the Mexicans. The electors, whether out of deference to the opinion of a king so deserving of the nation, or because they themselves knew the merit of Axayacatl, elected him, preferring him to Tizoc, his elder brother. Axayacatl was the son of Tezozomoc, who had been brother of the three kings who preceded Moctezuma and, like them, son of King Acamapichtli.

After the festivals of the election the new king went out to war to procure, following the example of his predecessor, victims to be sacrificed at his coronation. He made his expedition against the province of Tehuantepec, situated on the coast of the Pacific Ocean, about four hundred miles southeast of Mexico. The people of Tehuantepec had prepared themselves well, and allied themselves with their neighbors, to oppose the attempts of the Mexicans. In the furious battle that was fought there, Axayacatl, who commanded as General, pretended to flee in order to lead the enemy into an ambush. The people of Tehuantepec were pursuing the Mexicans, already singing the victory, when suddenly they found themselves attacked in the rear by a part of the Mexican army that came out of the ambush, and in front by those who had been fleeing, who turned against them; so that, harried from both sides, they were utterly defeated

p. 242

The enemies who were able to save their lives by flight were pursued

Bk. IV. by the Mexicans as far as the city of Tehuantepec itself, which they

put to fire and flame; and the Mexicans, taking advantage of the con‐

sternation of those peoples, pushed their conquests as far as

Huatulco, a maritime place whose port was much frequented in the

following century by Spanish ships. Axayacatl returned from this ex‐

pedition rich in spoils, and was crowned with an extraordinary dis‐

play of tributes and of sacrifices of prisoners. In the first years of his

reign he applied himself, following the footsteps of his predecessor, to

promoting conquests. In 1467 he reconquered Cotaxtla and

Tochtepec, which had rebelled. In 1468 he obtained a complete vic‐

tory over the Huexotzincas and the people of Atlixco, and, having re‐

turned to Mexico, undertook the building of a temple which he called

Coatlan. The Tlatelolcas built another in rivalry in their city, which

they called Coaxolotl: whence discord was revived between these two

kings, which proved, as we shall soon see, very disastrous to the

Tlatelolcas. In 1469 died Totoquihuatzin, first king of Tacuba, who in

the forty years and more that he held that little kingdom was con‐

stantly faithful to the kings of Mexico, and served them very well in

almost all the wars that they undertook against the enemies of the

state. His son Chimalpopoca succeeded him in the kingdom, very like

him no less in courage than in fidelity.

§ 15.

Far more regrettable was the loss that the Mexicans suffered in Death and eulogy of 1470 in the death of the great king of Acolhuacan, Nezahualcoyotl. King Nezahualcoyotl.

This king was one of the most renowned heroes of ancient America.

His courage, which in his youth was rather rashness, however great it

was, was yet among the less notable endowments of his soul. His for‐

titude and his constancy were truly admirable in those thirteen years

in which he lived deprived of the crown and persecuted by the usurp‐

er. His rectitude in the administration of justice was inflexible. To

make his nation more civilized, and to correct the disorders intro‐

duced into the kingdom in the time of the Tyrants, he published

eighty laws, which were afterward compiled by his most illustrious

descendant

p. 243

descendant Don Fernando de Alva Ixtlilxochitl in his manuscript History of the Chichimec Lords. He established that no case, civil or Bk. IV. criminal, could be prolonged beyond eighty days, that is, four Mexican months. Every eighty days a great assembly was held in the royal palace, where all the judges and all the accused came together. The cases that had not been concluded in the preceding four months were infallibly concluded on that day, and the accused who were convicted of any crime immediately and irremissibly suffered the penalty proportioned to their crime in the presence of that numerous assembly. For different crimes he prescribed different penalties, and some he punished with the utmost rigor, especially adultery, sodomy, theft, homicide, drunkenness, and treason against the country. If we believe the Texcocan historians, he had four of his sons put to death for being guilty of incest with their stepmothers.

Yet his clemency toward the wretched was singular. In that kingdom it was forbidden, on pain of death, to take anything from another’s field; and this law was so rigorous that stealing seven ears of maize was enough to incur the penalty. Nezahualcoyotl, in order to provide in some way for needy travelers without detriment to the law, commanded that on both sides of the main roads maize and other seeds should be sown, of whose fruits the needy might make use. A great part of his revenues he spent for the poor, particularly the aged, the sick, and widows. To prevent the destruction of the forests he prescribed limits to the woodcutters, and forbade them to pass beyond them under heavy penalties. Wishing to know whether such an order was exactly observed, he went out one day in disguise with another prince, his brother, and betook himself to the foot of the neighboring mountains, where the limits prescribed by him lay. There he found a boy occupied in gathering shavings that had been left from the wood cut, and asked him why he did not enter the forest to make firewood. Because the king, answered the boy, has forbidden us to cross these limits, and if

Hh2 we

p. 244

we do not obey him, he will punish us severely. Neither the entreaties nor Bk. IV.

the promises that the king made him sufficed to induce him to the trans‐

gression. The compassion that this poor boy aroused in the king moved

him to enlarge the limits already determined.

He had a great zeal for the faithful administration of justice, and so

that no one, under the pretext of necessity, might let himself be corrupt‐

ed by any of the litigating parties, he established that all his ministers and

judges should be furnished from the royal treasury with sustenance, cloth‐

ing, and everything needful according to the rank and quality of the per‐

son. So great was what he spent annually on his family and house, on the

sustenance of the ministers and magistrates, and on the relief of the poor,

that it would be altogether incredible, nor should I have the courage to

write it, if it were not established for us by the original paintings seen and

examined by the first apostolic friars who were engaged in the conversion

of those peoples, and confirmed by the testimony of a great-grandson of

that same king, who, converted to the faith of Christ, received in baptism

the name of Don Antonio Pimentel. (*) The expense of Nezahualcoyotl,

reduced to Castilian measures, was therefore as follows:

Of maize 4,900,300 fanegas. (f)

Of cacao 2,744,000 fan.

Of chile, or common pepper, and of tomate 3,200 fan.

Of chiltecpin, or small pepper, very sharp, for sauces 240 fan.

Of salt 1,300 large cakes

Of turkeys, or turkey cocks 8,000

Of what was consumed besides of chia, of kidney beans, and of other

legumes there was no number, nor even of the deer, the rabbits, the

ducks, the quail, and other birds. Everyone can easily understand how

great the hardship of the subjects must have been in amassing

so

so great a quantity of maize and cacao; especially since the cacao had

Bk. IV. to be procured through commerce with the hot countries, there being in the whole kingdom of Acolhuacan no land suited to the cultivation of this plant. For half the year, that is, for nine Mexican months, fourteen cities supplied this provision, and another fifteen the provision of the other half of the year. (g) To the young men was assigned the provision of the firewood, which was consumed in the royal palace in surprising quantity.

The progress made by this celebrated king in the arts and in the sciences was as great as can be made by a great mind that has neither books in which to study nor masters from whom to learn. He was skilled in the poetry of those nations, and made various compositions that were universally applauded. In the sixteenth century the sixty hymns composed by him in praise of the Creator of Heaven were famous even among the Spaniards. Two of his odes or songs, rendered into Spanish verse by his descendant Don Fernando de Alva Ixtlilxochitl, have been preserved down to our own times. (h) One of them was composed by him some time after the ruin of Azcapotzalco. Its subject, not unlike that of the other of which we have already made mention, is to lament the instability of human greatness in the person of the Tyrant Tezozomoc, who, like a great and thick tree, had spread his roots through so many countries and extended his green branches so far as to shade all the lands of the empire; but at last, worm-eaten and spoiled, fell to the ground without any hope of recovering his former greenness.

But

The other fifteen were Otompan, Aztaquemecan, Teotihuacan, Cempoallan, Axapochco, Tlalanapan, Tepepolco, Tizayocan, Ahuatepec, Oztoticpac, Cuauhtlatzinco, Cojoac, Oztotlatlauhcan, Achichillacachocan, and Tetliztacac.

p. 246

But in nothing did Nezahualcoyotl delight so much as in the study of Bk. IV.

nature. He also acquired several astronomical notions by the frequent

observation that he made of the course of the stars. He applied him‐

self likewise to knowing the plants and the animals, and because he

could not keep in his court those that were proper to a different

clime, he had all the vegetables and animals of the land of Anahuac

painted from life in his palaces; of which paintings the celebrated Dr.

Hernández bears witness, who saw them and made use of them in

part. Paintings truly far more useful and more worthy of a royal

palace than those that represent the wicked mythology of the

Greeks. He curiously investigated the causes of the effects that he ad‐

mired in nature, and this continual observation made him recognize

the folly of idolatry. To his sons he said privately that, in outwardly

adoring the idols to conform with the people, they should detest in

their minds that worship, worthy of scorn, because it was directed to

insensible creatures; that he acknowledged no other God than the

Creator of Heaven, and that he did not forbid idolatry in his kingdom,

as he would wish, so as not to be blamed for wanting to contradict

the doctrine of his ancestors. He prohibited sacrifices of human vic‐

tims; but, perceiving afterward how difficult it is to turn a nation

away from its ancient ideas in matters of religion, he returned to per‐

mitting them, but commanding under heavy penalties that no others

should ever be sacrificed than prisoners of war. He built in honor of

the Creator of Heaven a lofty tower of nine stories. The last story was

dark, with a small vault painted on the inside with blue and adorned

with gold moldings. There always resided in this tower men charged

with sounding, at certain hours of the day, plates of very fine metal, at

the sound of which the king knelt to make his prayer to the Creator

of Heaven, and in honor of that God he made at a certain time of the

year a fast. (i)

The

(i) All the aforesaid anecdotes have been taken from the precious manuscripts of

Don Fernando de Alva. As a great-great-grandson of that king, he was able to receive

by word of mouth many accounts from his fathers and grandfathers.

p. 247

The lofty genius of this king, impelled by the great love that he bore

Bk. IV. his people, so illustrated his court that in the future it was considered the homeland of the arts and the center of civilization. Texcoco was the city where the Mexican language was spoken with the greatest polish and perfection, where the best artisans were found, and where poets, orators, and historians were most abundant. (k) Hence the Mexicans and other peoples took many laws: wherefore it might be said that Texcoco had been the Athens, and Nezahualcoyotl the Solon, of Anahuac.

In his last illness, having had all his sons come into his presence, he declared as his heir and successor in the kingdom of Acolhuacan Nezahualpilli, who, although he was the youngest of all, was nevertheless preferred to the others, both for having been born of Queen Matlalcihuatzin and for his well-known rectitude and his singular talent. He charged his eldest son Acapipioltzin to aid the new king with his counsel until he should learn the difficult art of governing. To Nezahualpilli he warmly recommended love of his brothers, care of his subjects, and zeal for justice. Finally, to prevent any disorder that the news of his death might cause, he commanded that it be concealed from the people, as far as possible, until Nezahualpilli should be secure in the peaceful possession of the crown. The princes received with tears the last counsels of their father, and, coming out into the audience hall, where the nobility awaited them, Nezahualpilli was acclaimed king of Acolhuacan, Acapipioltzin declaring that this was the will of their father, who, having a long journey to make, wished first to name his successor. All gave obedience to the new king, and the next morning Nezahualcoyotl died, in the forty-fourth year of his reign and about the eightieth of his age. His sons kept his death secret, and hid

his

his body, burning it secretly, as is to be believed, and instead of hold‐

the news of his death spread at once through the whole land, and

many lords came to the court to condole with the princes.

Nevertheless the common people remained persuaded that that great

king had been transported to the company of the gods as a reward for

his virtues.

§ 16. Not long after the exaltation of Nezahualpilli there occurred the

Conquest of Tlatelolco, and death memorable war of the Mexicans with their neighbors and rivals the of King Moquihuix.

Tlatelolcas. The king of Tlatelolco, Moquihuix, unable to bear the

glory of the Mexican, used every means to obscure it. He was mar‐

ried, as we have already said, to a sister of King Axayacatl, given to

him by Moctezuma as a reward for the famous victory won over the

people of Cotaxtla. On this unfortunate lady he often vented his rage

against his brother-in-law, and, not content with this, he secretly

sought to ally himself with other states that bore the yoke of the

Mexicans unwillingly. These were the people of Chalco, of Xilotepec,

of Toltitlan, of Tenayuca, of Mexicaltzinco, of Huitzilopochco, of

Xochimilco, of Cuitlahuac, and of Mixquic, who agreed to attack the

Mexicans in the rear after the Tlatelolcas had begun the battle. The

people of Cuauhpanco, moreover, the Huexotzincas, and the

Matlatzincas, whose help he had also implored, were to incorporate

their troops with those of the Tlatelolcas for the defense of the city.

The queen learned of these negotiations, and, now from the hatred

that she bore her husband, now from love of her brother and her

country, she warned Axayacatl of everything, that he might avoid so

serious a blow, which would have made his throne totter.

Moquihuix, assured of the help of the confederates, convoked the

nobles of his court to encourage them to the undertaking. An old and

authoritative priest, called Poyahuitl, raised his voice in the assembly,

and in the name of all offered to fight courageously against the ene‐

mies of the country: then

to

p. 249

to encourage them still further, he washed the altar of the sacrifices

Bk. IV. and gave that water, tinged with human blood, to the king and to all the captains to drink; with it, they said, they felt their courage grow, and I do not doubt that it had grown the better to practice cruelty. Meanwhile the queen, impatient of the ill treatment she suffered and frightened by the perils of the war, left her husband and betook herself to Mexico with four children, to put herself under the shelter of her brother. This she could do easily because of the very short distance between the two cities. Such a novelty so increased the mutual ill feeling of the Mexicans and the Tlatelolcas that wherever they met they insulted one another with words, came to blows, and killed one another.

As the time for making war was now drawing near, Moquihuix, together with his captains and many of the confederates, held a solemn sacrifice on the mountain least distant from the city, in order to obtain the protection of their gods; and there the day was fixed on which war was to be declared on the Mexicans. A few days later he gave notice to the confederates, so that they might be well prepared to come to his aid as soon as the attack began. Xiloman, lord of Colhuacan, wished first to assail the Mexicans and then, feigning flight, to provoke them to pursue him, so that the Tlatelolcas might attack them from behind. The day after these embassies Moquihuix performed the ceremony of arming his troops, and then betook himself to the temple of Huitzilopochtli to implore his help; there they returned to drink that abominable draught which Poyahuitl had given them at the first assembly, and all the soldiers passed one by one before the idol, making it a deep reverence. No sooner was this ceremony over than a company of bold Mexicans entered the market square, killing everyone they met; but the Tlatelolca troops, coming up at once, drove them out and took some prisoners, who were sacrificed without delay in a temple called Tlillan. That same day, at sunset, some Tlatelolca women had the boldness to advance into the streets of

History of Mexico, Vol. I. Ii Mexico

p. 250

Mexico and to burn brooms at the doors of the houses, shamelessly Bk. IV.

hurling insults at the Mexicans and threatening them with their swift

ruin; but the Mexicans treated them with the contempt they

deserved.

That same night the Tlatelolcas took up arms, and in the morning,

at first light, they began the attack on Mexico. They were in the

hottest of the fray when Xiloman arrived with his Colhuas; but seeing

that the king of Tlatelolco had begun to fight without waiting for

him, and without heeding his counsel, he withdrew in indignation,

and, wishing to do the Mexicans some harm, had certain canals

closed to prevent any relief that might reach them by water; but they

were soon reopened by order of Axayacatl. All that day they fought

with indescribable ardor on both sides, until night forced the

Tlatelolcas to withdraw. The Mexicans burned the houses of the city

that were nearest to Tlatelolco, perhaps because they hampered them

in the fighting; but in setting the fire twenty of them were taken pris‐

oner by the enemy and immediately sacrificed.

Axayacatl that night distributed his army in all the streets that led

to Tlatelolco, and at daybreak they began to march from every side

toward the market square, which was to be the point of their union.

The Tlatelolcas, seeing themselves attacked on every side, were fall‐

ing back toward that great square, to unite all their forces there and

be able to resist still better; but on arriving at that place they found

themselves more hampered by their own multitude. The shouts with

which King Moquihuix tried from the top of the great temple to en‐

courage his men were of no avail. The Tlatelolcas were wounded and

killed, and those who fell vented their rage against the king with in‐

sults: “Come down from there,” they said to him, “and take up arms,

you coward; for it is not the part of brave men to stand calmly watch‐

ing those who fight and lose their lives in defense of their homeland.”

But these laments, caused by the pain of their wounds and the an‐

guish of death, were

altogether

p. 251

altogether unjust: for Moquihuix did not fail in the duties of a general

Bk. IV. and of a king, since he ought not to expose his own life as much as the soldiers theirs, in order to be able to be more useful to them by counsel and by voice. Meanwhile the Mexicans advanced as far as the stairway of the temple and, climbing it, reached the upper court, from which Moquihuix encouraged his people and defended himself like a desperate man; but a Mexican captain named Quetzalhua threw him down the stairs with a shove, (l) and some soldiers, taking the corpse on their arms, presented it to Axayacatl, who, opening its chest, tore out the heart. A horrible act, but performed without horror, being too common in their sacrifices.

Thus ended the brave Moquihuix, and with him the little monarchy of the Tlatelolcas, governed by four kings in the space of about a hundred and eighteen years. The Tlatelolcas, seeing their king dead, at once fell into disorder and tried to save their lives by flight, passing through the midst of their enemies; but four hundred and sixty were left dead in that square, and among them some officers of note. After this conquest the city of Tlatelolco was perfectly united to that of Mexico, and was no longer considered a distinct city, but as a part, or rather a suburb, of that court, as it is at present. The king of Mexico always kept a governor there, and the Tlatelolcas, besides the tribute of maize, clothing, arms, and armor that they paid annually to the crown, were obliged to rebuild the temple of Huitznahuac whenever it should be needed.

We do not know whether the people of Cuauhpanco, the Huexotzincas, and the Matlatzincas, who had confederated with the Tlatelolcas, were in fact present in this war. As for the other allies,

king. By this glorious victory Axayacatl added to the crown

Xiquipilco, Xocotitlan, Atlacomulco, and all the other places of that

pleasant valley that he still lacked.

As

p. 253

As soon as Axayacatl recovered from the wound, which, however, crippled

Bk. IV. one of his legs for the rest of his life, he gave a great banquet to the allied kings and to the Mexican grandees, at which he put to death Tlilcuezpalin and the two aforesaid Otomi captains. The carrying out of a punishment amid the delights of a banquet did not seem inopportune to those men; for, accustomed to shed human blood, they had changed their natural horror into recreation. So great is the force of custom, and so easy is it for our minds to make the most horrible objects familiar to themselves!

In the last years of his reign, finding the limits of the empire too narrow on the west side, he went out again on campaign through the Valley of Toluca and, passing beyond the mountains, conquered Tochpan and Tlaximaloyan, this place being from then on the frontier of the kingdom of Michoacán. Then, turning toward the east, he took possession of Ocuillan and Malacatepec. Death, which came upon him in the thirteenth year of his reign, which was 1477 of the common era, interrupted the course of his victories. He was very warlike, and severe in punishing those who transgressed the laws published by his predecessor. He left by several wives a great number of sons, among whom was the famous Moctezuma II, of whom we shall shortly speak.

There was someone who made it known to the king; whereupon he,

Thus did those seek to make their name and the glory of their deeds immortal who are believed by many to care nothing for the future.

§ 20.

Already at that time the king of Texcoco had several wives of very Marriage of King

Nezahualpilli, with noble houses; but none had been declared queen by him, since he was two Mexican ladies. reserving that honor for one whom he meant to take from the royal family of Mexico. He asked her of King Tizoc, and the latter gave him a niece of his, daughter of Tzotzocatzin. This marriage was celebrated in Texcoco with a great concourse of the nobility of the two courts. This lady had a sister endowed with singular beauty, who was called Xocotzin. They loved each other so much that, being unable to part, the new queen obtained from her father permission to take her sister with her to Texcoco. By frequent sight of her, and by conversing often with her, the king became so enamored of his fair sister-in-law that he resolved to marry her and to raise her also to the dignity of queen. This second marriage was, according to what the historians say, the most solemn and the most magnificent ever seen in that country. Not long afterward the king had by the first queen a son named Cacamatzin, who was his successor in the crown and, taken prisoner afterward by the Spaniards, died wretchedly. By the other he had Huexotzincatzin, (*) of whom we shall speak shortly, Coanacochtzin, who was also king of Acolhuacan and some time after the conquest by the Spaniards was hanged by the Conqueror Cortés, and Ixtlilxochitl, who allied himself with the Spaniards

While Nezahualpilli was seeking to multiply his descendants, en‐

joying great peace and tranquillity in his kingdom, some of his feuda‐

§ 21. tories were plotting the death of the king of Mexico. Techotlalla, lord Tragic death of King

Tizoc. of Iztapalapan, either resentful over some displeasure received or im‐

patient of Tizoc’s domination, conceived the wicked design of mak‐

ing an attempt on his life, and would reveal it to no one except to

such as seemed to him capable of carrying it out. He and Maxtlaton,

lord of Tlachco, agreed on the manner of executing so dangerous a

crime. The historians do not agree on this point. Some say that they

made use of certain witches, and that these by sorcery took his life;

but this seems to me a popular fable. Others affirm that they found a

way of giving him poison. However that may be, it is certain that

their plot succeeded. Tizoc was killed in the fifth year of his reign, in

1482 of the common era. He was a circumspect man, grave and se‐

vere, like his predecessors and successors, in the punishment of of‐

fenders. Since in his time the power and opulence of that crown were

already so great, he undertook to build for the tutelary god of the na‐

tion a temple that in size and magnificence should surpass all the

temples of that country, and to this end he had prepared infinite ma‐

terials and had even begun the building, when death came to upset

his designs.

The Mexicans, well aware that the death of their king had not

been natural, wished to avenge it before proceeding to a new election.

Their inquiries were so thorough that in a short time they discovered

the authors of the attempt and put them to death in the great square

Ahuitzotl, eighth and Texcocan nobility. The electors then having assembled to create a

23.

of the building of the magnificent temple that his predecessor had designed great temple of

While work was going on at this undertaking, the king often went out to war, and all the enemies who were taken prisoner were reserved for the festival of the Dedication. The wars of these four years were against the Mazahuas, a few miles to the west, who had rebelled against the crown of Tacuba, against the Zapotecs, three hundred miles to the southeast, and against several other peoples. When the building was finished, the king invited to the festival of the Dedication the two allied kings and all the nobility of both kingdoms. The concourse was the largest ever seen in Mexico (n); for people came to be present at so celebrated a ceremony even from the most distant places. The festival lasted four days, during which all the prisoners taken in the four preceding years were sacrificed on the upper court of the temple. The historians do not agree concerning the number of the victims. Torquemada says that there were seventy-two thousand three hundred and forty-four. Others affirm that there were sixty-four thousand and sixty. To carry out such horrible sacrifices with greater display, they arranged the victims in two rows, each about a mile and a half long, which began in the streets of
