Thus this king passed his reign in continual wars until 1496, in which year the war against Atlixco took place. The entry of the Mexican army into this valley was so sudden that the first notice the people of Atlixco had of it was that which they received through their eyes on seeing them enter. They took up arms at once for their defense; but not finding themselves with forces sufficient to resist for long, they asked help of the Huexotzincas, their neighbors. When the ambassadors of Atlixco arrived at Huexotzinco, a famous captain named Toltecatl, whose great courage was in no way inferior to the extraordinary strength of his arm, was playing ball. As soon as he learned the news of the Mexican army, he left the game to go to Atlixco with the auxiliary troops, and, entering the battle unarmed, to display his bravery and to show the contempt he felt for his enemies, he struck down with his fists the first Mexican who presented himself, and took his arms, with which he made great slaughter. The Mexicans, unable to overcome the resistance of their enemies, abandoned the field and returned to Mexico covered with ignominy. The Huexotzincas, to reward the singular prowess of Toltecatl, made him head of their republic. This had been subjected to the dominion of the Mexicans, whose arms they had provoked with their insults; but since the conquered do not bear the yoke of the conqueror except when they cannot shake it off, every time the Huexotzincas found themselves with forces sufficient to resist, they rebelled, and the same happened with the greater part of the provinces subjected by force of arms to the crown of Mexico; whence it was necessary that the Mexican army should be in continual motion to reconquer what the king was losing. Toltecatl accepted the office conferred on him; but

Kk2 scarcely

p. 260

scarcely had a year passed when he was obliged to give up his office

Bk. IV. and his country. The priests and other ministers of the temples, abus‐

ing their authority, entered the houses of private persons and carried

off the maize and the turkeys that were there, and committed other

excesses unbecoming to their dignity. Toltecatl wished to put a stop

to it; but the priests took up arms. The people partly sided with

them, and partly opposed their violence, and between these two fac‐

tions a war broke out, which, like all other civil wars, caused very

grave evils. Toltecatl, weary of ruling so unruly a people, or fearing to

perish in the tempest, withdrew from the city with other nobles and,

crossing the mountains, betook himself to Tlalmanalco. The governor

of this city gave prompt notice of it to the king of Mexico, and he at

once put to death all those fugitives in punishment of their rebellion,

and had their bodies carried to Huexotzinco to frighten the rebels.

§ 25. In 1498 it seemed to the king of Mexico that for lack of water the New inundation of

Mexico. navigation of the lake had become difficult, and he wished to increase

that water with the other from the spring of Huitzilopochco, which

the people of Coyoacán used, and he summoned Tzotzomatzin, lord

of Coyoacán, to give him his orders. Tzotzomatzin represented to

him that that spring was not perennial: that at times the water failed,

and at times came in such abundance that it might cause some dam‐

age to the court. Ahuitzotl, thinking that Tzotzomatzin’s reasons

were mere pretexts to excuse himself from doing what had been com‐

manded him, insisted on his first order; and seeing that man persist in

the difficulty he had put forward, he dismissed him in anger, and af‐

terward had him put to death. This is usually the reward of good

counsels, when princes obstinate in some caprice will not listen to the

sincere remonstrances of their faithful subjects. Ahuitzotl, unwilling

by any means to abandon his project, had a great and ample aqueduct

(*) made from Coyoacán to Mexico, and through it was conducted

the

(*) This aqueduct was entirely demolished either by Ahuitzotl himself, or by

p. 261

the water with many superstitious ceremonies; for some priests went

Bk. IV. about incensing it, others sacrificed quails and anointed with their blood the lip of the aqueduct, others played musical instruments, and all celebrated the arrival of the water. The high priest wore the same dress in which they represented Chalchiuhtlicue, goddess of water. (q)

With such solemnity the water arrived at Mexico; but it was not long before the general joy was changed into weeping; for the rains of that year having been extraordinarily abundant, the water of the lake rose so much that it flooded the city, all the streets were full of boats, and some houses collapsed. One day, the king being in a lower room of his palace, the water entered it so suddenly and in such quantity that, hastening in his fear to go out by the door, which was low, he gave himself such a violent blow on the head that after some time it caused his death. Afflicted by the evils of the inundation and by the clamors of the people, he called to his aid the king of Acolhuacan, who without delay had the dike repaired that, on the counsel of his father Nezahualcoyotl, had been built in the reign of Moctezuma.

Scarcely were the Mexicans freed from the evil of the inundation when in the following year they had to suffer that of the scarcity of grain, the maize having been spoiled by the excessive abundance of water; but in this same year they had the good fortune to discover in the valley of Mexico a quarry of tetzontli, which has been so useful for the buildings of that great city. The king at once began to use this kind of stone in the temples, and in imitation of him private persons in their houses. Besides this the king had all

He spent the last two years of his life in frequent wars, such as

§ 26. those of Izquixochitlan, Amatlan, Tlacuilollan, Xaltepec, New conquests, and

death of King Tehuantepec, and Huexotla in the Huaxteca. Tliltototl, a Mexican

Ahuitzotl. general, the war of Izquixochitlan being over, carried his victorious

arms as far as Cuauhtemallan, or Guatemala, more than nine hundred

miles southeast of the court, in which expedition he performed, ac‐

cording to what the historians say, prodigies of courage; but no one

relates the particular deeds of so renowned a general, nor do we know

whether all that great tract of land remained subject to the crown of

Mexico.

Finally, in the year 1502, after about twenty years of reign,

Ahuitzotl died of an illness caused by the blow to the head already

mentioned. He was a very warlike man, and one of the kings who

most enlarged the dominions of the crown. When he died, the

Mexicans possessed almost all that they had at the arrival of the

Spaniards. Besides courage he had two other royal virtues that made

him famous among his countrymen, namely magnificence and liberal‐

ity. He so embellished Mexico with new and magnificent buildings

that it had already become the finest city of the New World. When

he received the tributes of the provinces, he would assemble the peo‐

ple in a certain place in the city and distribute provisions and cloth‐

ing personally to the needy. He rewarded his captains and soldiers

who distinguished themselves in war, and the ministers and officials

of the crown who served him faithfully, with gold, silver, gems, and

beautiful feathers. These virtues were obscured by some vices; for he

was capricious, vindictive, and sometimes cruel, and so given to war

that he seemed to hate peace; whence the name of Ahuitzotl is used

proverbially even among the Spaniards of that kingdom to signify a

man who with his molestations and vexations does not let another

live. (r) But he was, on the other hand, of

good

(r) The Spaniards say, N. is my Ahuizote; This is N.’s Ahuizote; no one lacks his Ahuizote,

etc.

p. 263

good humor, and so delighted in music that neither by day nor by

Bk. IV. night was this entertainment lacking in the palace; which must have been prejudicial to the public good, since it robbed him of a great part of the time and care that he ought to have given to the affairs of the kingdom. Nor did women occupy his mind any less. His predecessors had had many wives, thinking that their authority and greatness appeared the greater the greater the number of persons devoted to their pleasures. Ahuitzotl, having so greatly enlarged the dominions and increased the power of the crown, wished to show the superiority of his greatness over that of his predecessors in the excessive number of his wives. Such was the state of the court of Mexico at the beginning of the sixteenth century, of that century so fertile in great events, in which that kingdom was to change its face and the whole New World was to be turned upside down.

V. and circumspection, and for his religion, he was much revered. He was a taciturn man and very considerate no less in his actions than in his words, and every time he spoke in the royal council, of which he was a member, he made himself heard with respect. Notice of the election was given to the allied kings, and they betook themselves at once to that court to pay their compliments. Moctezuma, aware of this, withdrew to the temple, as if to protest himself unworthy of so great an honor. The nobility went there to inform him of his election, and found him, according to what the historians say, sweeping the pavement of the temple. He was conducted with a great retinue to the palace, where the electors solemnly announced to him the election that had been made of his person to occupy the throne of Mexico. He then returned to the temple to perform the customary ceremonies; and when they were over, he received on the throne the obedience of the nobility and listened to the congratulatory speeches of the orators. The first was that of Nezahualpilli, king of Acolhuacan, which we present here to the readers just as the Mexicans have preserved it for us.

“The great good fortune of the Mexican monarchy,” he said, “is made manifest in the concord of your election, and in the singular applause with which it is celebrated by all. They have indeed reason to celebrate it; for the kingdom of Mexico has attained such an extent that to bear so great a weight neither a lesser fortitude than that of your invincible heart would suffice, nor a lesser wisdom than that which we admire in you. I see clearly how great is the love that Almighty God bears this nation; for He has enlightened it, so that it might know how to choose what

was more to its advantage. Who will be able to persuade himself that Bk. V. he who as a private man had already explored the folds of the heav‐

spirit shine on so many occasions not have it now, when he needs it

more than ever? Who can believe that where there is so much

courage and so much wisdom, relief will be lacking to the widow and

to the orphan? Without doubt the Mexican empire has reached the

summit of authority; for the Creator of Heaven has communicated so

much of it to you that you inspire respect in all who look upon you.

Rejoice, then, O blessed land, that a prince has fallen to your lot who

will be your support, and by his mercy will be a father and a brother

to his subjects. You have in fact a king who will not take occasion of

his superiority to give himself to softness and to lie stretched on his

bed, abandoned to pastimes and delights; rather, in the sweetest re‐

pose the care he will have of you will disquiet his heart and rouse

him, nor will he find relish in the most delicate food, for his anxiety

for your welfare. And you, most noble prince and powerful lord, take

courage, and trust that the Creator of Heaven, who has raised you to

so eminent a dignity, will give you strength to fulfill the obligations

attached to it. He who has been so liberal toward you until now will

not deny you his precious gifts, having himself raised you to the

throne, in which I wish you many and very happy years.”

Moctezuma listened attentively to this speech, and was so moved

that, though three times he wished to reply, he could not, being pre‐

vented by the tears produced by a sweet pleasure that had the appear‐

ance of humility; but at last, having somewhat restrained his weeping,

he replied in a few words, protesting himself unworthy of the honor

to which he was raised, and thanking that

king

p. 267

king for the praises with which he favored him; and having listened to the other speeches, he remained in the temple to keep the fast of Bk. V. four days, and was then conducted with great pomp back to the royal palace.

He thought next of making war to procure the victims that were to be sacrificed at his coronation. This misfortune fell upon the people of Atlixco, who a little before had rebelled against the crown. The king therefore went out from the court with the flower of the nobility, among whom went also his brothers and cousins. In this war the Mexicans lost some brave officers; but nevertheless they put the rebels back under their former yoke, and Moctezuma returned victorious, bringing with him the unfortunate prisoners he needed for his coronation. This ceremony was celebrated with such a display of games, dances, theatrical performances, and illuminations, and with such abundance and richness of tributes sent from the provinces of the kingdom, that even foreigners never before seen in Mexico came to see it, and the very enemies of the Mexicans, such as the Tlaxcalans and the Michoacans, disguised themselves in order to be present as spectators; but Moctezuma, having learned of it, with royal generosity had them lodged and entertained, and also had some terraces fitted up from which they could observe that great ceremony at their ease.

The first thing he did was to reward with the state of Tlachauhco the great services rendered to his predecessors in several wars by a renowned captain named Tlilxochitl. A truly happy beginning of his reign, if the progress had matched it. But scarcely had he begun to § 2.

Conduct and ceremouse his authority when he made manifest the pride that until then he nial of King had kept hidden under a fair appearance of humility. All his predeces‐ Moctezuma. sors had been accustomed to confer the offices on the most deserving, and on those who seemed to them the fittest to exercise them, honoring with them indifferently nobles and commoners, notwithstanding the solemn agreement made between the nobility and the commoners under the reign of Itzcoatl. Moctezuma, as soon as he took the reins of government, showed himself

Ll2 of

p. 268

of a different mind, and disapproved the conduct of his predecessors, Bk. V. on the pretext that the commoners served according to their station,

and that in all their actions they displayed the baseness of their birth

and of their upbringing. Prompted by this maxim, he stripped the

commoners of all the offices they held, both in his royal household

and at court, declaring them incapable of obtaining any in the future.

A prudent old man who had been his tutor represented to him that

such a resolution might estrange the minds of the common people

from his person; but nothing sufficed to turn him from the course he

had resolved upon.

The whole service of his royal palace was performed by persons of

rank. Besides those who always lived there, who were very many, six

hundred feudatory lords and nobles entered every morning to pay

him court. These spent the whole day in the antechambers, where

servants were not allowed to enter, talking in low voices and awaiting

the orders of their sovereign. The servants who accompanied these

lords were so many that they filled the three courtyards of the palace,

and many remained in the street. No smaller was the number of the

women who lived there, ladies, maidservants, and slaves. All this nu‐

merous flock lived shut up within a kind of seraglio, under the care of

some noble matrons, who watched over their conduct; for those

kings were exceedingly jealous, and punished with rigor any disorder

in the palace, however slight. From these women the king took for

himself those who pleased him, (c) and the others served to reward

the services of his vassals. All the feudatories of the crown had to re‐

side some months of the year at court, and on returning to their

states they left there their sons or their brothers, as hostages required

by the king to assure himself of their fidelity; hence they had to keep

a house in Mexico.

Another

(c) Some historians affirm that Moctezuma had a hundred and fifty of his wives

pregnant at the same time; but this is utterly incredible.

p. 269

Another effect of Moctezuma’s despotism was the ceremonial that he introduced at court. No one could enter the palace, whether to Bk. V. serve the king or to transact some business with him, without first taking off his shoes at the door. No one was permitted to appear before the king in splendid dress, because it was thought a want of respect toward Majesty; so that the greatest lords (except the king’s close kinsmen) stripped off the rich garment they wore, or at least covered it with another, plain one, to show their humility. All, on entering the audience hall, and before speaking to the king, made three bows, saying at the first, Lord, at the second, My Lord, and at the third, Great Lord. (d) They spoke low and with bowed heads, and received the answer that the king gave them through his secretaries as attentively and as humbly as if it had been an oracle. In taking leave, no one turned his back to the throne.

The same audience hall served him for his dinner: the table was a large cushion, and the chair a low stool. The tablecloths, napkins, and towels were of cotton, but very fine, white, and spotless. The dishes were of the fine majolica of Cholula; but none of these things served him more than once, for he immediately gave it to one of the nobles. The cups in which chocolate and the other drinks of cacao were prepared for him were of gold, or of handsome sea shell, or certain natural vessels curiously varnished, of which we shall speak elsewhere. He also had gold plates, but he did not use them except in the temple at certain festivals. The dishes were so many and so varied that the Spaniards who saw them were amazed. The conqueror Cortés says that they covered the floor of a great hall, and that there were presented to Moctezuma plates of every kind of game, fish, fruit, and herbs of that country. Three or four hundred young nobles in good order carried this dinner;

they

they presented it before the king sat down to table, and at once withBk. V. drew; and so that it should not grow cold, every dish was accompa‐

whole time the dinner lasted, together with six of his principal minis‐

As soon as the king sat down to table, the steward closed the door

of the hall, so that none of the other nobles should see him eat. The

ministers kept at a distance and observed a deep silence, except when

they had to reply to what the king said to them. The steward and the

four women served him the dishes, besides two others who brought

him the maize bread kneaded with eggs. Often he listened to music

while dining, and amused himself with the jests of certain deformed

men whom he kept for grandeur. He showed great pleasure in hearing

them, and said that among their jests they were wont to tell him

some important truth. When the dinner was over he took tobacco

mixed with liquidambar in a pipe, or reed, handsomely varnished, and

with that smoke he brought on sleep.

After sleeping a little, leaning on the same stool, he gave audience,

in which he listened attentively to all that was proposed to him, en‐

couraged those who from agitation could not speak, and answered all

through his ministers or secretaries. After the audience there was mu‐

sic, for he took great delight in hearing sung the glorious deeds of his

ancestors. At other times he amused himself by watching various

games, of which we shall speak elsewhere. When he went out of the

house he was carried on the shoulders of the nobles in an open litter

under a rich canopy, with a numerous train of courtiers, and wherever

he passed all stopped with eyes closed, as if they feared to be dazzled

by the splendor

of

p. 271

of Majesty. When he dismounted from the litter to go on foot, carpets were spread so that he should not touch the ground with his Bk. V. feet.

To so great a majesty the grandeur and magnificence of his palaces, of his pleasure houses, of his woods and gardens corresponded. The §Magnificence

3.

of the palace of his ordinary residence was a vast building of stone and lime, palaces

houses.

and royal

which had twenty doors opening on the square and the streets, three great courtyards, and in one of them a handsome fountain, several halls, and more than a hundred rooms. Some of the chambers had their walls faced with marble and other precious stones. The beams were of cedar, cypress, and other excellent woods, well worked and carved. Among the halls there was one so large that, according to an eyewitness and a careful one (*), three thousand men could stand in it. Besides this palace he had others, within and outside the capital. In Mexico, besides the seraglio of his wives, there were dwellings for his councilors and ministers, and for all the officials of his household and of his court, and also for lodging foreign lords who happened to come there, and especially the two allied kings.

He had two houses in Mexico for animals: one for the birds other than birds of prey, and the other for the birds of prey, for the quadrupeds, and for the reptiles. In the first there were many rooms, and corridors supported on columns of marble, each of a single piece. These corridors looked upon a garden, where amid the foliage of a grove of trees ten ponds were laid out, some of fresh water for the aquatic river birds, and the others of brackish water for those of the sea. In the rest of the house there were all the other species of birds, which were so many and so different that the Spaniards who saw them were amazed, and could not persuade

themselves

(*) The Anonymous Conqueror, in his valuable account. He tells us further that, having entered that great palace four times, and having gone round it until he was worn out, he could not see all of it.

p. 272

themselves that any species was lacking of all that exist in the world. Bk. V.

To each was supplied the same food on which it fed in its time of lib‐

erty, now seeds, now fruits, now insects. For the birds alone that live

on fish, ten Castilian weights of fish were consumed every day (as the

conqueror Cortés bears witness in his letters to Charles V), that is,

more than three hundred Roman pounds. Three hundred men, ac‐

cording to the same Conqueror, were employed solely in the care of

these birds, besides their physicians, who observed their diseases and

applied the suitable remedies. Of those three hundred some procured

their food, others distributed it, others had charge of the eggs and of

their hatching, and others, finally, plucked the birds at a certain sea‐

son; for besides the pleasure that the king took in seeing so great a

multitude of animals gathered together, he took chief care of the

feathers, no less for the famous mosaic images, of which we shall

speak in their place, than for the other works that were made of

them. The halls and rooms of these houses were so many that, as the

aforesaid Conqueror testifies, two great princes could have lodged in

them with all their retinue. This celebrated house stood on the site

where the great convent of San Francisco is today.

The other house, intended for the wild beasts, had a large and

handsome courtyard paved in checkerboard pattern, and was divided

into many apartments. In one of them were all the birds of prey, from

the royal eagle down to the kestrel, and of every species many indi‐

viduals. These birds were distributed according to their species in

many underground chambers, which were more than seven feet deep

and more than seventeen long and wide. Half of each chamber was

covered with good slabs, and there were perches fixed to the wall, so

that they could sleep there and shelter from the rain. The other half

was covered only by a lattice with other perches, where they might

enjoy the sun. For the sustenance of these birds as many as five hun‐

dred turkeys were killed every day. In the same house there were

many

halls

p. 273

low halls with a great number of strong wooden cages, in which were shut up the lions, the tigers, the wolves, the coyotes, the wild cats, Bk. V. and all the other species of wild beasts, which were fed on deer, rabbits, hares, techichi, and other animals, and on the entrails of the men who were sacrificed.

Nor did the king of Mexico keep only those species of animals that other princes keep for grandeur, but even those which by their nature seem exempt from servitude, such as crocodiles and serpents. The serpents of several species were kept in certain casks or large vessels, the crocodiles in ponds enclosed by a wall. There were likewise very many ponds for fish, of which two handsome ones still survive, seen by us in the palace of Chapultepec, two miles from Mexico.

Not content with keeping in his palaces every sort of animal, Moctezuma had also gathered there all the abnormal men who, whether by the color of their hair or of their skin or by some other deformity of the limbs, had become singular in their kind. A truly profitable vanity, since it secured the maintenance of so many wretched persons and freed them from the inhuman insults of other men.

In all his palaces he had most beautiful gardens, in which there was every kind of precious flower, fragrant herb, and medicinal plant. He had also woods enclosed by walls and supplied with abundant game, where he was accustomed to amuse himself. One of these woods was on a little island of the lake, known at present to the Spaniards by the name of Peñón.

Of all the aforesaid palaces, gardens, and woods nothing remains but the wood of Chapultepec, which the Spanish viceroys have preserved for their recreation. All the others were wrecked by the conquerors. They demolished the most magnificent edifices of Mexican antiquity, now from an indiscreet zeal for religion, now from vengeance, now to make use of the materials. They abandoned the cultivation of the royal gardens, cut down the woods, and reduced that land to such a state that nowadays the magnificence of those kings could not be believed, if

History of Mexico, Vol. I. Mm it

p. 274

it were not evident to us from the testimony of those very men who an‐

Bk. V.

nihilated it.

Both the palaces and all the aforesaid pleasure places were kept ex‐

tremely clean, even those where he never went; for there was nothing of

which he boasted more than cleanliness in his person and in all his

things. Every day he bathed, and therefore there were baths in all his

palaces. Every day he changed four garments, and the one that he had

§ 4. The good and the once laid aside he did not use again, but destined it to be given as a gift bad in Moctezuma. to the nobles who served him and to the soldiers who bore themselves

well in war. Every morning he employed, according to some historians,

more than a thousand men in sweeping and sprinkling the streets of the

city.

In one of the royal houses there was a great armory, where he had

every sort of arms, offensive and defensive, in use among those nations,

and insignia and military ornaments. In the making of these implements

he kept employed a surprising number of artisans; as also for other

works he had very many goldsmiths, mosaic workers, marble workers,

painters, and others. A whole street was devoted solely to dancers, in‐

tended for his amusement.

His zeal for religion was not inferior to his magnificence. He built

several temples to his gods, and offered them frequent sacrifices, observ‐

ing exactly the established rites and ceremonies. He took great care that

all the temples, and especially the greatest one of Mexico, should be well

served and exceedingly clean; but the vain fear of the auguries and of the

supposed oracles of those wicked deities utterly cowed his spirit.

He was extremely zealous for the observance of his orders and of the

laws of the kingdom, and was inexorable in punishing transgressors. He

often tested, through a third person and with gifts, the rectitude of his

magistrates, and if he ever found anyone guilty, he punished him without

remission, even though he belonged to the most conspicuous nobility.

He was an implacable enemy of idleness, and to banish it

as

p. 275

as far as possible from his dominions, he sought to keep his subjects

Bk. V. constantly occupied: the soldiers in continual exercises of war, and the others either in the cultivation of the fields, or in the construction of new buildings, or in other public works; and even the beggars, that they should not be altogether idle, he required to contribute a certain quantity of those filthy insects that are the nurslings of squalor and the companions of misery.

This oppression in which he held his vassals, the excessive burdens that he had laid upon them, his haughtiness and his pride, and his too great severity in punishing alienated their minds; but otherwise he won their love by liberality, both in providing for the needs of his peoples and in rewarding the services of his captains and ministers. Among the other things worthy of being celebrated with the highest praises, and of being imitated by all princes, he designated the city of Colhuacan as a hospital for all those invalids who, after having faithfully served the crown in military or political employments, needed, whether on account of their age or of their infirmities, to be cared for. There, at the expense of the royal treasury, their support and their cure were attended to. Such were the qualities, partly good and partly bad, of the celebrated Moctezuma, which it seemed to us fitting to set before the readers here, before relating the series of events that befell him.

At the beginning of his reign he put to death Malinalli, lord of Tlachquiauhco, for having rebelled against the crown of Mexico; he brought that state back under his obedience, and conquered that of Achiotlan. Soon afterward another war broke out, more serious and dangerous, in which he was not so fortunate.

Among so many provinces conquered by the Mexicans by force of § 5. arms, or voluntarily submitted to them for fear of their power, the re‐ War of Tlaxcala. public of Tlaxcala had always maintained itself unconquered, never bending its neck to the yoke, although it was so little distant from the court of the Mexican empire. The Huexotzincas, the Cholulans, and other neighboring states, which had formerly been allies of the republic, jealous then of their prosperity

Mm2 had

p. 276

had embittered the Mexicans against them, on the pretext that the Bk. V.

Tlaxcalans wished to take possession of the maritime provinces of the

Gulf of Mexico, and that by their commerce with those provinces

they increased their power and wealth every day, and were stirring up

the minds of the peoples. The commerce of the Tlaxcalans, of which

the Huexotzincas complained, was quite justified and necessary; for

besides the fact that the people of those coasts were originally in

great part from Tlaxcala, and the one and the other considered them‐

selves kinsmen, the Tlaxcalans needed to provide themselves with the

cacao, the cotton, and the salt that they lacked. Nevertheless the rep‐

resentations of the Huexotzincas and of the other rivals of Tlaxcala

so moved the minds of the Mexicans that, beginning with

Moctezuma I, all the kings of Mexico treated the Tlaxcalans as the

greatest enemies of their empire, and always kept strong garrisons on

the frontiers of Tlaxcala, to prevent those people from trading with

the maritime provinces.

The Tlaxcalans, finding themselves deprived of freedom in trade,

and consequently of the acquisition of things necessary to life, re‐

solved to send an embassy to the Mexican nobility (probably in the

time of King Axayacatl), complaining of the wrong done them

through the sinister reports of their rivals. The Mexicans, insolent

from their prosperity, replied that the king of Mexico was universal

lord of the world, and that all mortals were his vassals, and as such

the Tlaxcalans ought to give him obedience and acknowledge him

with tribute, after the example of the other nations; but if they re‐

fused to submit, they would perish without fail, their cities would be

utterly ruined, and their country would be inhabited by other peo‐

ples. To so arrogant and so foolish a reply the ambassadors answered

with these courageous words: “Most powerful lords, Tlaxcala owes

you no homage; nor, since their ancestors came out of the northern

countries to dwell in this land, have the Tlaxcalans ever acknowl‐

edged any prince with tribute. They have always preserved

their

p. 277

their liberty, and, not being accustomed to the servitude to which you claim to reduce them, rather than surrender to your power they will Bk. V. shed more blood than their ancestors shed in the famous battle of Poyauhtlan.”

The Tlaxcalans, distressed by the arrogant and ambitious pretension of the Mexicans, and despairing of being able to induce them to a reasonable agreement, thought seriously of fortifying their frontiers still further to prevent any invasion. They had before surrounded the lands of the republic with great ditches, and had placed good garrisons on the frontiers; now, in view of the threats of the Mexicans, they increased their fortifications, augmenting the troops of the garrisons, and building that famous wall of six miles which barred the entry to enemies on the east side, from which quarter greater danger threatened them. Often they were attacked by the Huexotzincas, the Cholulans, the people of Itzocan, the people of Tecamachalco, and other states neighboring or little distant from Tlaxcala; but they could never take from the republic a palm of land, thanks to the supreme vigilance of the Tlaxcalans and the courage with which they opposed the invaders.

Very many vassals of the crown of Mexico had taken refuge in the land of Tlaxcala, especially Chalcas and Otomis of Xaltocan, who had saved themselves from the ruin of their homelands in the wars already mentioned. These bore a deadly hatred to the Mexicans on account of the evils they had received from them, and therefore seemed to the Tlaxcalans the men best fitted to oppose vigorously the attempts of their enemies; nor were they mistaken; for in fact the Mexicans found greater resistance in no others than in those exiles, especially in the Otomis, of whom the garrison of the frontiers was composed, who served the republic faithfully, and were rewarded by it with distinguished posts.

During all the time that Axayacatl and his successors reigned, the Tlaxcalans remained deprived of commerce with the maritime provinces; hence salt failed the people to such a degree that they became accustomed to eating their food without such a seasoning

and

p. 278

and did not return to the use of it until many years after the conquest Bk. V. by the Spaniards; but the nobles (at least some of them), as they had

secret correspondence with certain Mexican lords, provided them‐

selves with everything necessary, without the commoners of either

side knowing it. No one is unaware that in general calamities it is the

poor who bear the whole weight of the tribulation, while the well-to-

do find in their wealth the means of escaping it, or at least of soften‐

ing it.

Now Moctezuma, unable to endure that the little republic of

Tlaxcala should refuse to render him the obedience and the adoration

that so many peoples paid him, even those most distant from the

court, ordered, at the beginning of his reign, that the states neighbor‐

ing Tlaxcala should make ready their troops and attack that republic

on every side. The Huexotzincas, confederated with the Cholulans,

soon raised troops under the command of Tecayahuatzin, head of the

state of Huexotzinco, but trusting more to their cunning than to

their forces, they tried first, with gifts and promises, to draw to their

side the people of Hueyotlipan, a city of the republic situated on the

frontier of the kingdom of Acolhuacan, and the Otomis, who guarded

the other frontiers; but neither the one nor the other would bind

themselves; indeed they declared that they were prepared to die in

defense of their republic. Whereupon the Huexotzincas, being com‐

pelled to resort to force, entered the lands of Tlaxcala with such fury

that, the garrison of the frontier not sufficing to hold them back, they

advanced, making a great slaughter, as far as Xiloxochitla, a place only

three miles distant from the capital. There Tizatlacatzin, a celebrated

Tlaxcalan captain, put up a great resistance against them; but in the

end he died, overcome by the multitude of the enemy; who, finding

themselves so near the capital, were so afraid of the vengeance of the

Tlaxcalans that they returned from there precipitately to their own

lands. This was the beginning of the continual battles and hostilities

that there were between those two states until the arrival of the

Spaniards. We do not know from history whether on this occasion

there also engaged

in

p. 279

in the war the other states neighboring Tlaxcala; it may be that the Huexotzincas and the Cholulans did not permit the others to have a Bk. V. share in their glory.

The Tlaxcalans remained so enraged against the Huexotzincas that, no longer willing to confine themselves within their own state to defend it, as they had done before, they often went out to attack their enemies. Once they assaulted them along the foot of the mountains that lie to the west of Huexotzinco (*), and pressed them in such a way that, the Huexotzincas not being able to resist, they asked help of Moctezuma, who immediately sent an army under the command of his eldest son. This army marched along the southern slope of the volcano Popocatepetl, where it was swelled by the troops of Chietlan and Itzocan, and thence by way of Cuauhquechollan entered the valley of Atlixco. The Tlaxcalans, aware of the route the Mexicans were taking, determined to forestall them and fall upon them before they could unite with the Huexotzincas. So sudden was their assault that the Mexicans were defeated, and the Tlaxcalans, taking advantage of their disorder, made a great slaughter of them. Among the dead fell the prince-general himself, to whom perhaps so important an office had been given rather to add this honor to the splendor of his birth than out of regard for his skill in the art of war. The rest of the army took to flight, and the victors, laden with spoils, returned to Tlaxcala. It is surprising that they did not fall immediately upon the city of Huexotzinco, when they might have hoped that it would at once surrender; but perhaps their victory was not so complete as to leave them without the loss of many of their own in the battle, and they thought it better to enjoy then the fruits of the victory, to return afterward to the war with greater forces. In fact they returned; but they were repulsed by the Huexotzincas, who had meanwhile fortified themselves, and went back to Tlaxcala without

any

(*) The city of Huexotzinco was not then where it is at present, but farther up, toward the west.

p. 280

any other advantage than that of laying waste the fields of Bk. V. Huexotzinco and of Cholula; whereby those peoples were brought to

As for King Moctezuma, he felt an inexpressible grief at the death

of his eldest son and at the defeat of his army; therefore, to avenge

himself, he had another army immediately made ready in the prov‐

inces surrounding Tlaxcala, to blockade the whole republic; but the

Tlaxcalans, well foreseeing the hostility of the Mexicans, had fortified

themselves extraordinarily and had increased the garrisons every‐

where. There was vigorous fighting on the one side and on the other;

but at last the royal troops were repulsed, leaving no little wealth in

the hands of their enemies. The republic celebrated this victory with

great rejoicings, and rewarded the Otomis, to whom it was chiefly

due, raising the most distinguished of them to the dignity of Texctli,

which was held among them in the highest esteem, and giving to the

chiefs of that nation as wives some daughters of the noblest

Tlaxcalans.

There is no doubt that if the kings of Mexico had engaged serious‐

ly against the Tlaxcalans, they would at length have subjected them to

the crown; for however great the forces of the republic, however sea‐

soned its troops, and however strong its places, it was nevertheless by

far inferior in power and in strength to the Mexicans. Hence what

the historians affirm seems to me quite probable, that the kings of

Mexico purposely left the republic of Tlaxcala in existence, scarcely

sixty miles distant from that capital, having conquered the more dis‐

tant provinces, both so that the Mexican troops might have some‐

where to exercise their courage, and also, and chiefly, to have a place

where they could easily procure victims for their sacrifices. Both of

these they obtained in the frequent assaults that they made on the

places of Tlaxcala.

was a most famous general called Tlahuicole, (e) in whom it was not known which was greater, the courage or the surprising strength of Bk. V. his body. The Maquahuitl, or Mexican sword, with which he fought was so heavy that a man of ordinary strength could hardly lift it from § 6. the ground. His name was the terror of the enemies of the republic, Tlahuicole, celebrat‐

This man, then, Tlaxcalans. in an assault that the Huexotzincas made on a garrison of Otomis, rashly placed himself, in the greatest heat of the fray, in a marshy place, where, not being able to move as nimbly as he wished, he was taken prisoner, shut up in a strong cage, and then carried to Mexico and presented to Moctezuma. This king, who knew how to esteem merit even in his enemies, instead of putting him to death generously granted him the liberty of returning to his native land; but the arrogant Tlaxcalan would not accept the favor, on the pretext that, having been taken prisoner, he had not the heart to present himself with such ignominy to his countrymen. He said that he wished to die, like the other prisoners, in honor of their gods. Moctezuma, seeing him so reluctant to return to his native land, and not wishing besides to deprive the world of so celebrated a man, kept him at court in the hope of making him a friend of the Mexicans and of making use of him for the benefit of the crown. Meanwhile war broke out with the Michoacans, the cause and circumstances of which we do not know at all, and Moctezuma committed to Tlahuicole himself the command of the army that he sent to Tlaximaloyan, the frontier, as we have already said, of the kingdom of Michoacán. Tlahuicole responded advantageously to the confidence placed in him; for although he could not dislodge the Michoacans from the place where they had fortified themselves, he nevertheless took many of them prisoner, and took from them a good quantity of gold and silver. It may be believed that the Mexicans themselves contrived this, so

that he might leave them a glorious posterity, which would ennoble

by its prowess the court and the kingdom of Mexico. At length the

king, seeing the obstinacy with which he refused whatever proposal

was made to him, yielded to his barbarous wishes, and fixed the day

of the sacrifice. Eight days before, the Mexicans began to celebrate it

with dances, and when the term was completed, in the presence of

famous general, whose courage and whose loyalty to his country

a famine was suffered in some provinces of the Mexican empire,

The following year (1505), having had an abundant harvest, the Mexicans went out to war against Cuauhtemallan, a province more than nine hundred miles distant from Mexico toward the southeast. While this war was in progress, caused probably by some hostility committed by the people of Guatemala against the subjects of the crown, the building of a temple erected in honor of the goddess Centeotl was completed in Mexico, whose most solemn dedication was celebrated with the sacrifices of the prisoners taken in that war.

They had also by this time widened the road on the lake from Chapultepec to Mexico, and rebuilt the aqueduct that was upon the same road; but the joy they felt at the completion of so great a work was disturbed by the burning of the tower of another temple, called Zomolli, caused by lightning. The inhabitants of that part of the city which was farther from that temple, and particularly the Tlatelolcas, not having noticed the lightning, persuaded themselves that such a fire had been started by enemies who had come suddenly upon the city; whereupon they at once took up arms to defend it, and ran in crowds toward the temple. Moctezuma conceived such anger at that disturbance, persuading himself that it had been a mere pretext of the Tlatelolcas to stir up some sedition (for there was perpetual distrust of them), that he deprived them of all the public offices they held, and even forbade them to appear at court, neither the protestations they made of their innocence nor the prayers with which they implored the royal clemency then sufficing to turn him from such a resolution; but as soon as that first fire of his wrath died down, he restored them to their offices and to his favor.

Meanwhile the Mixtecs and the Zapotecs rebelled against the crown. The principal leaders of the rebellion, to whom had committed themselves all the lords of both nations

§ 9. Soon after this expedition some dispute or other arose between Dispute between the

Huexotzincas and the Huexotzincas and the Cholulans, their neighbors and friends,

the Cholulans. and, committing the decision to arms, they fought a pitched battle.

The Cholulans, as more practiced in the exercise of religion, of com‐

merce, and of the arts than in that of war, were soon beaten and

forced to retire to their city, to which the enemy pursued them, killed

some of their people, and burned some of their houses. No sooner

had the Huexotzincas obtained such a victory than they repented of

it, because of the punishment that threatened them; hence, to avoid

it, they sent to King Moctezuma two distinguished persons, called

Tolimpanecatl and Tzoncoztli, seeking to justify themselves and to

blame the Cholulans. These ambassadors, whether to make the

courage of their fellow citizens appear greater or for any other mo‐

tive, so exaggerated the slaughter of the Cholulans that they made

the king believe that all had perished, and that those few who had

saved their lives had abandoned the city. Moctezuma, on hearing this

report, was exceedingly grieved by it, and feared the vengeance of the

god Quetzalcoatl, whose sanctuary, which was among the most cele‐

brated and

most

p. 285

most revered in all that land, he believed to have been profaned by

Bk. V. the Huexotzincas. Having therefore taken counsel with the two allied kings, he sent to Cholula some persons of his court to inform themselves fully of the whole affair, and having found it very different from what the Huexotzincas had told him, he conceived such indignation at having been deceived by them that he at once dispatched an army, ordering the general to punish them severely if they should not give fitting satisfaction. The Huexotzincas, sensing the storm that was about to burst upon them, came out drawn up in battle array to receive the Mexicans; but the Mexican general advanced toward them to set forth his commission in these words: “Our lord Moctezuma, who has his court in the midst of the water, Nezahualpilli, who commands on the shores of the lake, and Totoquihuatzin, who reigns at the foot of the mountains, order us to tell you that, having learned from your ambassadors that you have ruined Cholula and killed its inhabitants, they have felt the utmost grief, and are compelled to avenge so great an outrage against the venerable sanctuary of Quetzalcoatl.” The Huexotzincas protested that the report of their ambassadors was too exaggerated and false, and that therefore a body so respectable as that of the city of Huexotzinco could not be the author of it, and they offered to satisfy all three kings by the punishment of the guilty. Then, having caused the aforesaid ambassadors to be brought there, and having cut off their ears and nose (which was the penalty established against those who told lies harmful to the public), they handed them over to the general. Thus they avoided the evils of war, which otherwise would have been inevitable. § 10.

§ 11. Presages of the war This secular year seems to have passed without war, but in 1507 with the Spaniards. the Mexicans made the expedition against Tzollan and Mictlan, states

of the Mixtecs, whose inhabitants fled to the mountains to save

themselves, and the Mexicans had no other advantage than that of

taking prisoner a few Mixtecs who had remained in their houses.

Then they went to subjugate those of Cuauhquechollan who had re‐

his courage. Some brave captains of the Mexicans died in this expedi‐

and took three thousand two hundred prisoners, who were sacrificed,

part in the festival Tlacaxipehualiztli, which was held in the second

Mexican month, and part at the Dedication of the sanctuary Zomolli,

which after the aforementioned fire had been rebuilt with greater

magnificence.

The following year the royal army of the Mexicans, Texcocans, and

Tepanecs marched out against the remote province of Amatlan. As

they were marching over a very high mountain, a furious north wind

with snow came upon them, which wrought a very great slaughter in

the army, for some, being accustomed to a mild climate and going al‐

most naked, died of cold; and others were crushed by the trees that

the wind tore up. Of the rest of the army, which continued its journey

weakened as far as Amatlan, the greater part died in the battle.

These and other calamities, added to the appearance of a comet in

the sky at that same time, threw all those peoples into great conster‐

nation. Moctezuma, who was too superstitious to be able to regard

with indifference such

a

p. 287

a phenomenon, consulted his astrologers about it; but these not knowing how to divine its meaning, he asked it of the king of Bk. V. Acolhuacan, who was also given to astrology and divination. These kings, although they were related to each other and perpetually allied, did not however live in much harmony, ever since the king of Acolhuacan had put to death, as we shall soon see, his son Huexotzincatzin, heedless of the entreaties of Moctezuma, who, as uncle of that prince, had interceded for him. It had already been a long time since they had dealt with each other with that frequency and that confidence with which they used to; but this time the vain terror that weighed upon Moctezuma’s mind impelled him to avail himself of the learning of King Nezahualpilli; hence he begged him to come to Mexico to deliberate together on that matter, which pressed equally upon both. Nezahualpilli went, and after conferring at length with Moctezuma, was of the opinion, according to what the historians say, that the comet announced the future misfortunes of those kingdoms through the arrival of new peoples. But this interpretation not pleasing Moctezuma, Nezahualpilli challenged him to the ball game, which was very frequent even among the kings themselves, and they agreed that if the king of Mexico won the match, the king of Acolhuacan would renounce his interpretation, esteeming it false; but if Nezahualpilli won, Moctezuma should acknowledge it as true and embrace it. A truly ridiculous folly of those men, to believe that the truth of a prediction depended on the dexterity of the player or on the fortune of the game; but yet less pernicious than that of the ancient Europeans, who committed to the barbarity of the duel and the uncertainty of arms truth, innocence, and honor. Nezahualpilli remained victorious in the game, and Moctezuma disconsolate at the loss and at the confirmation of so evil a prognostication. Nevertheless he wished to try another way, hoping to find a more favorable prediction that might counterbalance that of the king of Acolhuacan and the misfortune at the game. He therefore had a most famous astrologer consulted, well versed

in

p. 288

in the superstitions of divination, by which he had made his name so Bk. V.

celebrated in that land, and had won for himself such great authority

that, without leaving his house, he was consulted like an oracle by the

kings themselves. Knowing without any doubt what had passed be‐

tween the two kings, instead of giving his sovereign a pleasing answer,

or at least an equivocal one, as such prognosticators for the most part

do, he confirmed the fatal prediction of the Texcocan. Moctezuma

was so angered by the answer that, in recompense, he had his house

pulled down, and the unfortunate diviner remained buried among the

ruins of his sanctuary.

These and other like prognostications of the fall of that empire are

to be seen in the paintings of the Americans and in the histories of

the Spaniards. I am far from persuading myself that everything we

find written on such a subject is worthy of our belief; but neither can

one doubt the tradition that existed among the Americans, that there

were to arrive in that kingdom new peoples very different from its

own inhabitants, who would take possession of the whole land. There

was no civilized or half-civilized nation in the country of Anahuac

that did not bear witness to this tradition, either by their verbal testi‐

mony or by their own histories. It is not possible to guess the first

origin of so universal a tradition; but in the fifteenth century, and

even in the fourteenth, after the invention of the compass had freed

men from the fear of losing sight of land, and the Europeans, spurred

on by ambition and by the insatiable hunger for gold, had begun to

make the perils of the Ocean familiar to themselves, that malignant

spirit, the capital enemy of mankind, who goes ceaselessly about the

whole earth spying on the actions of mortals, could easily conjecture

the progress of the Europeans, the discovery of the New World, and a

part of the great events that were to take place there; and it is not im‐

probable that he predicted them to nations consecrated to his wor‐

ship, in order to confirm them, by that same prediction of the future,

in the erroneous persuasion of his pretended divinity. But if the Devil

prognosticated the future

calamities

p. 289

calamities in order to deceive those wretched peoples, the most merciful God announced them in order to dispose their spirits to the Bk. V. Gospel. The event that I am about to relate in confirmation of this truth was public and sensational, having occurred in the presence of two kings and of the Mexican nobility. It is also found represented in some paintings of those nations, and a juridical attestation of it was sent to the court of Spain. (*)

Papantzin, a Mexican princess and sister of King Moctezuma, had § 12.

Memorable occurmarried the governor of Tlatelolco, and after his death remained in rence concerning a his palace until the year 1509, when she too died of an illness. Her Mexican princess. funeral was celebrated with a magnificence corresponding to the splendor of her birth, the king her brother and all the Mexican and Tlatelolca nobility attending. Her body was buried in a subterranean cave or grotto that was in the garden of the same palace, near a pond where she used to bathe, and the entrance of the cave was closed with a stone of no great weight. The following day a little girl of five or six years took it into her head to go from her mother’s apartment to that of the deceased’s steward, which lay on the other side of the garden, and in passing she saw the princess seated on the steps of the pond, and heard herself called by her with the word Cocoton, (f) which they use in speaking tenderly to children. The little girl, not being capable at her age of reflecting on the death of the princess, and thinking that she had come to bathe, as she was accustomed to do, approached without fear, and the princess sent her to call the wife of her steward. She went to call her; but the woman, smiling and caressing her, said to her: “My daughter, Papantzin is already dead, and yesterday we buried her.” But as the girl insisted, and even pulled her by the huepilli, or woman’s shift, she, more to please her than because she believed

Moctezuma could scarcely give credence to what he heard.

Nevertheless, in order not to fail in the respect due to so authorita‐

tive an ambassador, he went with him, and with many of the Mexican

nobility, to Tlatelolco, and, entering the hall where the princess was,

asked her whether she was his sister. “I am indeed, lord,” replied the

princess, “your sister Papan, whom you buried the day before yester‐

day; I am truly alive, and I wish to make known to you what I have

seen, because it concerns you.” This said, the two kings sat down, all

the others remaining standing, amazed at what they saw.

Then the princess continued to speak thus: “After I died, or, if you

will not believe that I was dead, after I was left deprived of motion

and of the senses, I suddenly found myself in an extended plain, the

end of which could be seen on no side. In the middle of it I observed

a

road

p. 291

road, which I then saw divide into various paths, and on one side there ran a great river, whose waters made a frightful noise; and as I Bk. V. wished to throw myself into the river to cross to the opposite bank by swimming, I saw before me a handsome youth of good stature, dressed in a long robe, white as snow and shining like the sun, furnished with wings of beautiful feathers, and bearing on his forehead this sign (in saying this the princess made the sign of the cross with her first two fingers); and, taking me by the hand, he said to me: Stay; for it is not yet time to cross this river. God loves you greatly, though you do not know him. Then he led me along the river, on whose bank I saw very many human skulls and bones, and I heard such lamentable groans that they moved me to compassion. Then, turning my eyes to the river, I saw upstream some large boats, and in them certain men of a color and dress very different from ours. They were white and bearded, and carried standards in their hands and helmets on their heads. God, the youth then said to me, God wills that you live, so that you may be a witness of the revolutions that are to come to pass in these kingdoms. The groans that you heard among those bones are those of the souls of your ancestors, who are and always will be tormented for their crimes. The men whom you see coming in the boats are those who by force of arms will make themselves masters of all these kingdoms, and with them will come also the knowledge of the true God, Creator of heaven and earth. As soon as the war is ended and the bath is proclaimed by which sins are washed away, be the first to receive it, and guide your countrymen by your example. This said, the youth vanished, and I, called back to life, rose from the place where I lay, lifted the stone from the sepulcher, and went out into the garden, where I was found by my servants.”

Moctezuma was stunned on hearing such an account, and, with his mind disturbed by a great throng of thoughts, he rose and went out at once to betake himself to a palace of his set apart for the time of mourning, without saying a word to his sister, or to the king of Texcoco, or to any of those who accompanied him,

He did not wish to go back to her again, so as not to hear once more

the melancholy presages of the ruin of his empire. The princess after‐

wards lived many years in the utmost seclusion and abstinence. She

was the first who, in the year 1524, received holy baptism at

Tlatelolco, and from that time on she was called Doña María

Papantzin. In the years that she survived her regeneration she was a

perfect model of Christian virtue, and her death corresponded to her

life and to her marvelous vocation to Christianity. (g)

§ 13. Besides this memorable occurrence there happened in 1510 the Remarkable sudden and violent burning of the towers of the great temple of phenomena.

Mexico on a clear night, without its cause ever being guessed, and in

the preceding year so rapid and so extraordinary an agitation of the

waters of the lake that it ruined some houses of Mexico, there being

neither wind, nor earthquake, nor any other natural cause to which so

rare a phenomenon could be ascribed. It is said also that in 1511

armed men were seen represented in the air, fighting among them‐

ed by Acosta, by Torquemada, and by others, were found exactly de‐

scribed in the Mexican and Acolhua histories. Nor is it improbable

that God, having announced by such prodigies the destruction of

some cities, as is known to us partly from Holy Scripture and partly

from the testimony of Josephus, of Eusebius of Caesarea, of Orosius,

and of other authors, should employ the same providence in the gen‐

eral overthrow of an entire world, which is without doubt the rarest

and most notable event of all those that are read of in human

histories.

The consternation caused in the mind of Moctezuma by such dis‐

mal presages did not turn him from thoughts of war. Many

and new expeditions to Moctezuma, and less in proportion to the magnificence of the of the Mexicans. temple, he had a good stone of immeasurable size sought out, and it was found near Coyoacán. After having had it polished and curiously carved, he commanded that it be carried solemnly to Mexico. A great multitude gathered to drag it; but in crossing a bridge of beams that lay over a canal at the entrance of the city, it broke the beams with its enormous weight and fell into the canal, dragging with it some men, and among them the high priest, who was walking before it incensing it. This misfortune greatly grieved the king and the people; but without abandoning the undertaking they drew the stone out of the water with the utmost labor and carried it to the temple, where it was dedicated with the sacrifice of all the prisoners who had been reserved for this great festival, which was truly one of the most solemn that the Mexicans celebrated. To it the king summoned the principal nobility of his whole kingdom, and he spent great treasures on the gifts that he made to the nobles and to the commoners. This same year there was also celebrated the dedication of the temple of Tlamatzinco, and of that of the Quauhxicalco, of which we shall speak elsewhere. The victims sacrificed at the dedication of these two buildings and at that of the altar of the sacrifices were, according to what the historians say, twelve thousand two hundred and ten.

To furnish so great a number of victims it was necessary to wage war continually. In 1511 the Yopis rebelled, and wished to kill the whole garrison of Mexicans that was at Tlacotepec; but their design having been opportunely discovered, they were punished, and two hundred of them were led as prisoners to the capital. In 1512 an army of Mexicans marched toward the north against the people of Quetzalapan, and with

the

p. 294

loss of only ninety-five men they took one thousand three hundred

Bk. V.

and thirty-two prisoners, who were also brought to Mexico. With

these and other conquests made in the three following years the

Mexican empire attained its greatest extent, five or six years before

its ruin, to which these same rapid conquests contributed greatly.

Every province, every place conquered became a new enemy of the

conquerors, which, impatient of the yoke to which it was not accus‐

tomed, and irritated by the violence, awaited nothing but some good

occasion to avenge itself and to restore itself to its former liberty. The

happiness of a kingdom does not consist in the extent of its domin‐

ions, nor even in the multitude of its vassals; indeed it never ap‐

proaches its end so closely as when, by reason of its vast and immod‐

erate extent, it can maintain neither the necessary union among its

parts nor the vigor that is needed to resist the multitude of its

enemies.

Nor did the revolutions that occurred at this same time in the

kingdom of Acolhuacan, caused by the death of King Nezahualpilli,

§ 15. contribute less to the ruin of the Mexican empire. This celebrated Death and eulogy of king, after having held the throne forty-five years, either weary of King Nezahualpilli.

he often amused himself in the exercise of hunting, and at night he

occupied himself in the observation of the sky, and for this purpose

he had had a small observatory built on the terrace of the palace,

which was preserved until the following century and was seen by

some Spanish historians, who speak of it. There he not only contem‐

plated the motion and course of the stars, but conferred with some

men skilled in astronomy; for this study having always been held in

esteem among them, they gave themselves more to it after they were

p. 295

example of the great King Nezahualcoyotl and of his son and successor. Bk. V.

After six months of this private life he returned to the capital, ordered his dear Xocotzin to withdraw with her children to the palace called Tecpilpan, and shut himself up in the one that was his ordinary residence, without letting himself be seen by anyone save some of his confidants, with the design of concealing his death in imitation of his father. In fact neither the time nor the other circumstances of his death were ever known. It is known only that he died in 1516, and that before dying he commanded the confidants whom he employed to burn his body secretly. Hence it came about that the common people, and even a good many nobles, remained persuaded that he was not dead, but had gone to the kingdom of Amaquemecan, whence his ancestors had their origin, as he had often said he wished to do.

This king was of the same opinion in matters of religion as his great father Nezahualcoyotl. He despised in his mind the worship of idols, although outwardly he conformed with the people. He likewise imitated his father in zeal for the laws and in severity of justice, of which he gave a rare example in the last years of his reign. There was a law that forbade, under pain of death, the speaking of indecent words in the royal palace. This law was violated by one of the princes his sons, named Huexotzincatzin, whom he loved more than all the others, no less for his character and for the virtues that were already shining in his youth than for his being the first-born among the sons he had by his favorite Xocotzin; but the prince’s words had been rather the effect of youthful thoughtlessness than of any wicked purpose. The king learned of it from one of his concubines, to whom such words had been said. He asked her whether this had occurred before other persons, and, having learned that the prince’s tutors had been present, he withdrew to an apartment of the palace that was set aside for occasions of mourning. There he had the tutors summoned to examine them. They, fearing

to

p. 296

to be severely punished if they concealed the truth, testified to it Bk. V.

frankly; but at the same time they endeavored to excuse the prince,

saying that he neither knew the person to whom he was speaking nor

had the words been obscene. But in spite of their representations he

ordered at once that the prince be arrested, and on the same day pro‐

nounced against him the sentence of death. The whole court was dis‐

mayed at so rigorous a sentence; the nobility interceded with prayers

and tears, and the prince’s own mother, trusting in the great love the

king bore her, presented herself to him weeping, and, to move him

the more to compassion, brought her children with her. But neither

reasons, nor prayers, nor tears sufficed to bend the king. “My son,” he

said, “has violated the law. If I pardon him, it will be said that the

laws are not made for everyone. Let my subjects know that to no one

will transgression be pardoned, since I do not pardon it in the son

whom I love most.” The queen, pierced by the keenest grief and de‐

spairing of being able to overcome the king’s firmness, said to him:

“Since for so slight a cause you have driven from your heart all the af‐

fections of a father and of a husband, and wish to make yourself the

executioner of your own son, what is left to you but to give death also

to me and to these tender princes whom I have borne you?” The king

then, with a grave look, commanded her to withdraw, since there was

no longer any remedy. The queen went away disconsolate to her

apartment, and there, in the company of some ladies who went to

console her, abandoned herself to weeping. Meanwhile those who

were charged with the prince’s execution kept delaying, so that, with

time, zeal for justice being relaxed, there might be room for paternal

love and for clemency; but the king, perceiving their intention, com‐

manded that it be carried out at once, as in fact it was, to the general

sorrow of the whole kingdom, and to the very great displeasure of

King Moctezuma, not only because of the kinship that he had with

the prince, but also because the entreaties he had interposed that the

sentence be revoked had been disregarded.

After

p. 297

After the execution had been carried out, the king shut himself up in

Bk. V. a hall for the space of forty days, without letting himself be seen by anyone, in order to give full vent there to his grief, and he had the doors of his son’s apartment walled up, so as to remove from his eyes that incentive to sorrow.

This severity in punishing transgressors was counterbalanced by the compassion that he showed for the misery of his subjects. There was in his palace a window that looked on the marketplace, covered by a lattice, from which he observed, without being seen, the people who gathered there; and when he saw some poorly dressed woman he had her called, and, having informed himself of her way of life and of her need, he provided her with what was necessary for her and for all her children, if she had any. Every day he gave alms in his palace to all the infirm and the orphans. There was in Texcoco a hospital for all those who in war had lost their eyes, or in any other way had been made useless for the exercise of arms, and there they were maintained at the king’s expense according to their condition, and often visited by the king himself. On such works he spent a great part of his revenues.

The intelligence of this king has been much celebrated by the historians of that kingdom. He proposed to himself to imitate, both in his studies and in his conduct of life, the example of his father, and in fact he was very like him. With him the glory of the Chichimec kings may be said to have ended; for the discord that arose among his sons diminished the splendor of the court, weakened the forces of the state, and disposed it to its final ruin. Nezahualpilli did not declare who was to succeed him on the throne, as all his predecessors had done. We likewise do not know the motive of such negligence, which proved so pernicious to the kingdom of Acolhuacan. § 16.

who was an ambitious and enterprising young man, opposed it, saying

that he was alive, and that, the lawful sovereign being alive, it was a

presumption on the part of the subjects to name a successor. The

to contradict him openly; but they begged Coanacochtzin to say what

him with levity and thoughtlessness, since he did not perceive that in

embracing such a course he favored the designs of Moctezuma, who

was too much inclined toward Cacamatzin, and was working to put

him on the throne, hoping to have in him a king of wax, to whom he

could give whatever form he pleased. “It is not reasonable, my broth‐

to me, not to you, would the crown be due?” “It is true,” said Ixtlilxochitl then, “that if in the succession only age is to be consid‐ Bk. V. ered, the crown is due to Cacamatzin, and, he failing, to you; but if regard is had, as is just, to courage, it is due to me rather than to you or to Cacamatzin.” The councilors, seeing that the anger of the princes was growing ever hotter, imposed silence on both and dismissed the assembly.

The two princes went to their mother, Queen Xocotzin, to continue their dispute, and Cacamatzin, accompanied by many nobles, went at once to Mexico to inform Moctezuma of what had happened and to ask his help. Moctezuma, who, besides the love he bore him, saw the right of that prince and the consent of the nation, advised him to place in safety, before anything else, the royal treasure, and promised him to settle the quarrel with his brother, and to employ the Mexican arms in his favor if negotiations should not prove sufficient.

As soon as Ixtlilxochitl learned of the departure of Cacamatzin, and foresaw the consequences of his recourse to Moctezuma, he left the court with all his partisans, and went off to the domains that his tutors had in the mountains of Metztitlan. Coanacochtzin promptly gave notice to Cacamatzin to return without delay to Texcoco, and to take advantage of so opportune an occasion to be crowned. Cacamatzin took the salutary counsel of his brother, and went to that court accompanied by Cuitláhuac, brother of Moctezuma and lord of Iztapalapan, and by many Mexican nobles. Cuitláhuac, without losing time, convoked the Texcocan nobility in the Hueitecpan, or great palace of the kings of Acolhuacan, and presented to them Prince Cacamatzin, that he might be recognized by them as lawful sovereign. All accepted him, and the day was then fixed for the ceremony of the coronation; but it was prevented by the news that arrived at the court, that Prince Ixtlilxochitl was descending from the mountains of Metztitlan at the head of a large army,
