p. 300

This restless young man, as soon as he arrived at Metztitlan, conBk. V.

voked all the lords of the places situated in those great mountains,

and made known to them his design of opposing his brother

Cacamatzin, under the pretext of zeal for the honor and liberty of the

Chichimec and Acolhua nations: that it was an unworthy and very

dangerous thing to obey a king so pliant to the will of him of Mexico;

that the Mexicans, forgetting how much they owed to the Acolhuas,

wished to add to their iniquitous usurpations that of the kingdom of

Acolhuacan; that he for his part was determined to employ all the

courage that God had given him in defending his country from the

tyranny of Moctezuma. With such reasons, suggested to him proba‐

bly by his tutors, he so inflamed the minds of those lords that all of‐

fered to help him with all their forces, and in fact they raised so many

troops that, when the prince came down from the mountains, his

army amounted, by what they say, to more than a hundred thousand

men. In all the places through which he passed he was well received,

either from fear of his power or from inclination to favor his preten‐

sions. From Tepepulco he sent an embassy to the people of Otompan,

ordering them to render him obedience as to their own king; but they

answered that, King Nezahualpilli being dead, they recognized no

other master than Cacamatzin, who had been peacefully accepted in

the capital and was already in possession of the throne of Acolhuacan.

This answer irritated Ixtlilxochitl, and made him march headlong

against that city. The people of Otompan came out to meet him in

battle order; but although they offered some resistance to the enemy

army, they were nevertheless defeated, and their city was taken by the

prince. Among the dead fell the lord of Otompan himself, and this

very circumstance hastened the victory for the prince.

This occurrence put Cacamatzin and all his court in great disquiet;

whence, fearing that the enemy might wish even to besiege the capi‐

tal, he sought to fortify himself; but the prince, content to see him‐

self respected and feared, did not move

then

p. 301

then from Otompan; but he posted guards on the roads with orders

Bk. V. to do harm to no one, not to hinder the passage of private persons who might wish to go from the capital to any other place, and to show respect to persons of the first rank who might pass there. Cacamatzin, seeing the forces and the resolution of his brother, and thinking it a lesser evil to sacrifice a part, though a large one, of the kingdom than to lose it all, sent him, with the consent of Coanacochtzin, an embassy to make some accommodation with him. He sent to tell him that he might keep, if he wished, all the dominions of the mountains, since he himself was content with the capital and the states of the plains; that he wished also to share with his brother Coanacochtzin the revenues of the kingdom; but at the same time he begged him to renounce every other pretension and to disturb no more the public tranquillity. The ambassadors were two personages of the royal blood of Acolhuacan, for whom Ixtlilxochitl had great respect. This prince replied that his brothers might do whatever they pleased; that it was pleasing to him that Cacamatzin should be in possession of the kingdom of Acolhuacan; that he was plotting nothing against them, nor against the state; that he maintained that army for no other purpose than to oppose the ambitious designs of the Mexicans, who had caused the gravest vexations and suspicions to his father Nezahualpilli; that if the kingdom was divided at that time for the common interest of the nation, he hoped to see it united again another time; that above all they should take care not to fall into the snares of the crafty Moctezuma. Nor was Ixtlilxochitl mistaken in his distrust of Moctezuma; for in fact that king was the very one who gave the unfortunate Cacamatzin, as we shall see, into the hands of the Spaniards, in spite of the love he bore him.

By the agreement made with his brother, Cacamatzin remained in peaceful possession of the crown of Acolhuacan, but with his dominions much diminished, since what he had ceded was a considerable part of the kingdom. Ixtlilxochitl kept his troops always in motion, and often let himself be seen with his army in the neighborhood of Mexico, defying Moctezuma

to

p. 302

to fight hand to hand with him. But this king was no longer in a state Bk. V. to accept such a challenge: the fire that he had had in his youth had

already begun to die down with the years, and domestic delights had

weakened his spirit; nor would it have been prudent to expose him‐

self to such a conflict with so resolute a young man, who by secret ne‐

gotiations had already drawn to his side a great part of the Mexican

provinces. Nevertheless the Mexicans often fought with that army,

being now defeated, now victorious. In one of those skirmishes a rela‐

tive of the king of Mexico was taken, who had gone out to the war

with the resolution of making that prince prisoner and bringing him

bound to Mexico, and had so promised Moctezuma. Ixtlilxochitl

learned of this arrogant promise, and, to avenge himself, having had

him bound and covered with dry cane, had him burned alive in sight

of the whole army.

In the course of our history we shall show how large a part this

restless prince had in the good fortune of the Spaniards, who at this

time began to let themselves be seen on the coasts of the Gulf of

Mexico; but before undertaking the narration of a war that threw all

those kingdoms into confusion, it is necessary to make known the re‐

ligion, the government, the arts, and the customs of the Mexicans.

End of the First Volume.
